]
, which iteratively computes improved spectral approximations to
\mathbf{A}
. These improvements are realized by alternating between computing generalized leverage scores (with respect to the current best spectral approximation) and using the current generalized leverage scores to compute an improved spectral approximation. To implement this algorithm, we need a procedure (
) to carry out leverage score sampling with respect to an intermediate spectral approximation. We will give a subroutine (
) to solve the following slightly more general sampling problem that will be useful for
\ell_{p}
regression. In our application,
\mathbf{M}
will be the inverse of these spectral approximations to
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
(or to the inverse Lewis quadratic form for
p\neq 2
; cf.
Definition

1.22
for the definition of Lewis quadratic form.)

Problem 2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a positive semidefinite matrix owned by the coordinator. We have matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{A}^{(s)}
on the coordinators, with
\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}\times d}
for all
j\in[s]
. Denote the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
row of the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
matrix by
\mathbf{a}_{i}^{(j)}
. Then, define the following quantities:

\displaystyle u_{i}^{(j)}

\displaystyle:=\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2},\,\,\,\,v_{i}^{(j)}:=\min\left(\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{p},1\right).

The problem asks to output
r
i.i.d. rows of
\mathbf{A}
from the probability distribution obtained by normalizing the
v_{i}^{(j)}
’s. Specifically, for each individual sample, the probability of choosing row
i_{0}
from block
j_{0}
should be

(1\pm\varepsilon)\frac{v_{i_{0}}^{j_{0}}}{\sum_{j\in[s],i\in[n_{j}]}v_{i}^{(j)}}\pm O(n^{-c}),

where
n
is an upper bound on
n_{j}
, the number of rows in
\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
, and
C
is an absolute constant. For each sampled row, the problem also requires outputting a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-factor approximation to the sampling probability.

We note that the
n^{-c}
term in the problem statement above comes from the
\ell_{p}
-sampler that we borrow. This term can be taken to be
n^{-3}
for example, with no extra cost. It will therefore be irrelevant for us, as we will never apply the sampler more than
n
times per block.

To solve
2.1
, we use the
\ell_{p}
sampling sketch of
[
JW21
]
, which we employ to sample a row of a matrix proportional to the
p^{\text{th}}
power of its norm. Such sketches using polylogarithmic space exist only for
p\in[1,2]
. This is the reason for our restricted range of
p
.

For completeness, we give a statement of their result here.

Theorem 2.2
.

(
\ell_{p}
-sampling.) There is a sketching matrix
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
such that given
\mathbf{S}v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
one can output an index
i
of
v
such that the probability of outputting index
i
is
(1\pm\nu)\frac{|v_{i}|{}^{p}}{\|v\|_{p}^{p}}\pm\frac{1}{\operatorname{poly}(n)}.

The sketching dimension
m
can be taken to be
O(\log^{2}n\log\frac{1}{\delta}(\log\log n)^{2})
. Moreover the entries of
\mathbf{S}
can be taken to have
O(\operatorname{poly}\log n)
bits of precision for
\eta\geq n^{-c}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

(
\ell_{p,2}
sampling procedure)
With an appropriate
\ell_{p}
-sampling sketch, the probability that
, with input matrices
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{X}
, outputs row
\mathbf{x}_{i}

is
(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{i}
where

p_{i}:=\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}}{\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}}.

Furthermore, the output probability estimate
\hat{p}_{i}
of
satisfies
\hat{p}_{i}=(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{i}.

These guarantees fail with probability at most
\delta
, and the total communication used by the protocol is

\widetilde{O}({d}{\varepsilon^{-2}}\log^{2}n\log^{2}({\delta^{-1}})+dL).

Input.
A matrix

\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
held by the coordinator, matrix
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
held by a server, accuracy parameter
\varepsilon
, failure tolerance
\delta
, norm
p\in[1,2]

Output.

On the coordinator side:
(1) A row
\mathbf{x}_{k}
of
\mathbf{X}
such that the probability of sampling row
i
is
(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{i}
where
p_{i}:=\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}}{\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{p,2}
is the
\ell_{p}
norm of the vector of row
\ell_{2}
norms.
and (2) An estimate
\widehat{p}_{k}=(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{k}.

procedure SampleFromBlock
(
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{M}
,
p
,
\varepsilon
,
\delta
):

Set
r={\varepsilon^{-2}}(\log(n)+\log(\delta^{-1}))
. Coordinator forms
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{rd\times rd}
.
Server forms
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}=\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{nr\times dr}

Server draws an
\ell_{p}
sampling sketch,
computes
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
, and sends it to the coordinator

Coordinator samples
\mathbf{g}\sim c_{p}^{1/p}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{rn})\in\mathbb{R}^{rd}
and computes

\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{g}
where
c_{p}=1/\mathbb{E}(|\mathcal{N}(0,1)|^{p})

Coordinator uses the
\ell_{p}
sampling sketch
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}

to sample
a row index
k
of
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{g}

Coordinator requests row
\mathbf{x}_{k}
from the server who then sends that row

Coordinator produces probability estimate
\widehat{p}_{k}
: Coordinator first uses
\mathbf{x}_{k}
to compute
\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}
and an uses the
\ell_{p,2}
norm estimation protocol from
Lemma

2.5
to approximate
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}
to
(1\pm\varepsilon)
multiplicative error.

Algorithm 2
:
\ell_{p,2}
sampling procedure used by

alg]alg:auxillary_sampling_procedure

Proof.

We first prove the communication cost, followed by the correctness of
.

Communication cost.

We use the
\ell_{p}
-sampling sketch
\mathbf{S}
of
[
JW21
]
, as stated above in
Theorem

2.2
.
Recall that given the sketch
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, this this result allows us to sample an index
i
of
\mathbf{v}
with probability proportional to
|v_{i}|^{p},
up to
\operatorname{poly}(1/n)
additive error on the sampling probabilities.
Note that Section 6 of
[
JW21
]
, also gives a sketch to estimate the frequency moment of a sample, which we need here. To obtain a failure probability of
O(\delta)
and an accuracy
O(\varepsilon)
as above, their result gives such a sketch
\mathbf{S}
with space
\widetilde{O}\left(\log^{2}(rn)\log(\delta^{-1})+{\varepsilon^{-2}}\log(rn)\log^{2}(\delta^{-1})\right)
when applied to a single vector of length
rn.

In
Item

, the server applies
\mathbf{S}
to the matrix
\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{nr\times dr}
which
has
dr
columns before sending it to the coordinator, giving the communication bound stated above. This proves the communication cost, since there is no communication cost in the other steps.

Correctness.

Next, we observe that the
\ell_{p}^{p}
mass corresponding to each row of
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}
is preserved by the Gaussian sketch.
Note that the entries of
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{rn}

consist of
rn
Gaussians,
where for each
i\in[n]
, there are
r
mutually independent entries distributed
as
c_{p}^{1/p}\mathcal{N}(0,\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{2})\sim c_{p}^{1/p}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}\mathcal{N}(0,1).
For
(i,j)\in[n]\times[r]
let
z_{i,j}
be independent and standard normal. Then the total
\ell_{p}^{p}
mass corresponding to row
i
of
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}
is distributed as

\frac{\sum_{j\in[r]}c_{p}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}}{\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]\times[r]}c_{p}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}}=\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}\sum_{j\in[r]}c_{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}}{\sum_{i\in[n]}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}\sum_{j\in[r]}c_{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}}.

For all
i,
we have that
\sum_{j\in[r]}c_{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}
is a sum of independent subexponential random variables with mean
1.
So by Bernstein’s inequality
[
Ver18
]
, each fixed sum
Z_{i}:=\sum_{j\in[r]}c_{p}|z_{i,j}|^{p}
satisfies

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(|Z_{i}-r|\geq\varepsilon r\right)\leq 2\exp(-c\varepsilon^{2}r).

So
Z_{i}=(1\pm\varepsilon)r
with probability
1-2\exp(-c\varepsilon^{2}r).
For large enough
r=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta})),
this probability is at most
\delta/n

, and therefore the bound on
Z_{i}
holds for all
i\in[n]
simultaneously with probability all but
\delta.
Conditioned on these bounds, the
\ell_{p}^{p}
mass on row
i
of
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}
is

\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}Z_{i}}{\sum_{i\in[n]}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}Z_{i}}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}}{\sum_{i\in[n]}\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\frac{\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}}{\|(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M})\|_{p,2}^{p}}

The claim now follows from correctness of the
\ell_{p}
sampler.
∎

We need a sketch to approximate the
\ell_{p}
norm of a vector. Such sketches are based on the
p
-stable random variables, which require infinite bits to represent
[
Ind06
,
KNPW11
]
. For completeness, we show that using appropriately rounded Cauchy random variables is sufficient. One might be concerned that the rounding could cause problems as we will sketch vectors with potentially exponentially large entries (corresponding to poorly conditioned linear systems). However this is not actually an issue.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
p\in[1,2].
There is a sketching matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m}{n}
that sketches a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and outputs a
1\pm\varepsilon
multiplicative approximation to
\|x\|_{p}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. This guarantee is achieved with a sketching dimension of
m=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta})
. Moreover, each entry of
S
is represented with
O(\log\frac{n}{\varepsilon})
bits of precision.

Proof.

We first recall the structure of a typical
\ell_{p}
norm sketch. Each row is independent and consists of i.i.d.
p
-stable random variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\sim p-\text{stable}.
Now suppose that we round each
x_{i}
and instead use
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}+f_{i}
where
\mathbf{f}
is the vector of rounding errors.
Then

\left\langle x^{\prime},v\right\rangle=\left\langle x,v\right\rangle+\left\langle f,v\right\rangle\sim\|v\|_{p}Y+\left\langle f,v\right\rangle,

where
Y
is a
p
-stable random variable with the same distribution as the
x_{i}
’s. Since
p\leq 2
, we have

\left\langle f,v\right\rangle\leq\|f\|_{2}\|v\|_{2}\leq\|f\|_{2}\|v\|_{p}.

Thus, each row of the sketch is distributed as
\|v\|_{p}Y
but perturbed by at most
\|f\|_{2}\|v\|_{p}.
The
\ell_{p}
estimation sketch works by taking the median over
1/\varepsilon^{2}
rows, thereby approximating
\|v\|_{p}
to within
\varepsilon\|v\|_{p}
. After rounding, the median is perturbed at most
\|f\|_{2}\|v\|_{p}
, so it suffices to arrange for
\|f\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon.
This can be achieved by rounding each coordinate by no more than
\varepsilon/\sqrt{n},
which means we can round to
O(\log\frac{n}{\varepsilon})
bits of precision.
∎

We also note that the same approach of embedding
\ell_{p,2}
into
\ell_{p}
when combined with an
\ell_{p}
norm estimator, gives an
\ell_{p,2}
norm estimation protocol in our setting.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be held by a server and
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
a matrix held by the coordinator. For
1\leq p\leq 2
, there is a protocol using communication
\widetilde{O}(d\varepsilon^{-2}L\log n\log(\delta^{-1}))
that allows the coordinator to estimate
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}
to
1\pm\varepsilon
multiplicative error.

Proof.

Similar to the setup of
Lemma

2.3
, consider sampling
r=O({\varepsilon^{-2}}(\log n+\log(\delta^{-1})))
independent Gaussians
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots\mathbf{g}_{r}\sim c_{p}^{1/p}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d},
where
c_{p}=1/\mathbb{E}(|\mathcal{N}(0,1)|^{p}).
The protocol that achieves the claimed approximation guarantee is for the server to send a
p
-moment estimation sketch

\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}
to the coordinator (gotten from
Lemma

2.4
), who then computes
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{S}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{r}.
Note that

\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{1}\|_{p}^{p}+\ldots+\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{r}\|_{p}^{p}=\sum_{j\in[r]}\sum_{i\in[n]}|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{j}}|^{p}.

As in the proof above, each inner sum
\sum_{i\in[n]}|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{j}}|^{p}
is
(1\pm\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{2}^{p}

with failure probability at most
\delta.
So
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{1}\|_{p}^{p}+\ldots+\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{g}_{r}\|_{p}^{p}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-approximation to
\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}
.
We choose the sketch to have failure probability most
\delta/r
so that each term above is well approximated. The sketch uses

\widetilde{O}\left({L}{\varepsilon^{-2}}\log n\log(\delta^{-1})\right)\text{ space per column},

giving the claimed communication bound (since we have
d
columns in
\mathbf{X}
).
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

There is a protocol which solves
2.1
with failure probability at most
\delta
and

\widetilde{O}\left((T\log T\log({\delta^{-1}})+r)({d}{\varepsilon^{-2}}\log^{2}n+dL)+s{\varepsilon^{-2}}\log n\log({\delta^{-1}})L\right)

communication, where the parameters here are as given in
2.1
.

Remark 2.7
.

Note that later, in the proof of
Theorem

1.5
, we will apply this result with
T,r=O(d).

Proof.

The first step of
is to produce an estimate of

B^{(j)}:=\sum_{i}v_{i}^{(j)}

for all
j
. To do this, we first estimate

B^{(j)}:=\sum_{i}(u_{i}^{(j)})^{p/2}=\|\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{p,2}^{p}

to within
(1\pm\varepsilon)
multiplicative error using
Lemma

2.5
above.

In order to handle the truncation to
1
, we would next like to find all
u_{i}^{(j)}\geq 1.
Call the corresponding rows “outlying”. To identify the outlying rows we use the
\ell_{p,2}
sampling protocol given in
.

To implement
\ell_{p,2}
sampling across the blocks, the coordinator first uses the values of
\widetilde{B}^{(j)}
to choose a server
\ell
from which to sample. Then they run the protocol

\textbf{SampleFromBlock}(\mathbf{A}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{M},p,0.5,0.5)

from
to sample from the
p
th moment of the row norms, up to a
(1\pm 0.5)
factor on the sampling probabilities.

We would like to identify all outlying rows and (temporarily) remove them as we go. We sample rows via the
\ell_{p}
sampling method described above. Each such row is sent to the coordinator which then checks by direct computation whether or not it is outlying. If it is, then the server temporarily removes that row and sends a new sketch to the coordinator, so that the coordinator can update its norm estimate for that server (as in step

c
).

Suppose that there are
k
outlying rows remaining. Then the total
\ell_{p}^{p}
mass contributed by those rows is at least
k
, and the total
\ell_{p}^{p}
mass of the non-outlying rows is at most
T
(from the statement of
2.1
). Hence the probability of sampling an outlying row is at least
\frac{ck}{T+k}
for a constant
c
, which satisfies
\frac{ck}{T+k}\geq\frac{ck}{2T}
since
k\leq T.
The expected number of samples needed to encounter an outlying row is therefore at most
O(\frac{2T}{k}).
There are at most
T
outlying rows to start with, so up to a constant factor, the expected number of samples needed to find all outlying rows is at most

\frac{2T}{T}+\frac{2T}{T-1}+\ldots+\frac{2T}{1}=2T\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{2}+\ldots+\frac{1}{T}\right)\leq O(T\log T).

This means that after
O(T\log T)
rounds of sampling we identify all outlying rows with constant probability. To boost the success probability to
1-\delta
, we can apply the standard median trick and obtain a bound of
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta}T\log T)
on the required number of rounds of sampling to identify all outlying rows with probability
1-\delta
.

Next, the coordinator counts the total number of outlying rows for each server, and adds this to a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-factor approximation of the
\ell_{p,2}
norm of the remaining rows (computed using a sketch as above). This gives the coordinator
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-factor approximations
\widehat{B}^{(j)}
to
B^{(j)}
.

To sample from the
v_{i}^{(j)}
distribution, the coordinator first decides whether to sample an outlying row, with probability proportional to the number of outlying rows, as described in
Item

.

Otherwise, the coordinator picks a server
j
with probability proportional to
\widehat{B}^{(j)}
, and requests an
\ell_{p,2}
sampling procedure with failure probability
O(\delta/r)
as given in
, for all the non-outlying rows. The coordinator uses this sketch to sample a row index and then requests the corresponding row from the server, as in
Item

.
The probability estimate given by
suffices to produce the estimates required by
2.1
. This procedure is then repeated
r
times.

The communication cost comes from the
\ell_{p,2}
sampling procedure, which is run
O(\log({\delta^{-1}})T\log T+r)
times. We also request an
\ell_{p,2}
norm estimation sketch from each server, incurring an additional
\widetilde{O}(s\log n\log(L\delta^{-1}))
cost. By
Lemma

2.3
this gives the stated communication cost.
∎

2.1.2
Proof of Main Results for
\ell_{p}
Regression

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
p\in[1,2]

Output.
A (weighted) row-sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}
with
O(d\log d)
rows such that

\|\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\|_{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{A}x\|_{p}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Compute
\mathbf{Q}=\textbf{ApproxLewisForm}(\mathbf{A},p)
an
O(1)
spectral approximation to the Lewis quadratic form of
\mathbf{A}

Sample
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}d\log d)
indices of
\mathbf{A}
as in step

‣
3
below, and return the corresponding sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}.

procedure ApproxLewisForm
(
\mathbf{A}
,
p
)

If
\mathbf{A}
has at at most
d
rows, return
\mathbf{A}.

Uniformly sample half the rows of
\mathbf{A}
to obtain
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}

Compute
\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}
by recursively calling
ApproxLewisForm
(
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
,
p
)

For all row indices
i
of
\mathbf{A}
, compute
u_{i}
as constant-factor approximations to
\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{a}_{i}

For all row indices
i
of
\mathbf{A}
, let
q_{i}=\min(1,u_{i}^{p/2})

and normalize to obtain
p_{i}=q_{i}/\sum_{j}q_{i}

Obtain
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
by sampling
N=O(d\log d)
i.i.d. rows of
\mathbf{A}
from the
p_{i}
distribution (each rescaled by
(Np_{i})^{-1/p}
, where the scaling is accurate up to a constant factor)

Compute the Lewis quadratic form for
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
(see
Definition

1.22
) and return it.

Algorithm 3
:
The recursive sampling algorithm from
[
CP15
]
, specialized to the range
1\leq p\leq 2
. When
p=2
this algorithm is essentially the repeated halving algorithm of
[
CLMMPS15
]
.

alg]alg:recursiveSamplingCohenPeng

See
1.5

Proof.

We use the recursive Lewis weight sampling algorithm of
[
CP15
]
, a version of which is reproduced in
. First we note a few minor modifications to their original algorithm. We consider only
1\leq p\leq 2
, which as noted in
[
CP15
]
avoids the extra
\operatorname{poly}(d)
factor in the number of row samples required per recursive call. Further, constant-factor approximations to the sampling probabilities in
Item

suffice, following the original analysis in
[
CP15
]
who show that computing constant-factor approximations to the Lewis weights suffices in each recursive call; constant-factor errors on the row-norms of
\mathbf{A}
have precisely the same effect on the Lewis quadratic form.

To modify this algorithm for the distributed setting, we note that uniformly sampling rows is easy for the coordinator to simulate. Given the number of rows on each server, the coordinator samples a random collection of row indices (but does not request the actual rows).

To implement the sampling described in
Item

through
Item

, we note that this is precisely the setting of our
2.1
with
r=O(d\log d),T=O(d)
and
\varepsilon=O(1)
, where the latter statement is given in the proof of
[
CP15
, Lemma 3.2]
. Note that

is run once per recursive call, of which there are at most
O(\log(dn)).

This yields a constant factor approximation to the Lewis quadratic form on the coordinator side. Then we apply
to
sample (and rescale) an additional
O({d}{\varepsilon^{-2}}(\log n+\log{\delta^{-1}}))
rows. To rescale appropriately, we additionally estimate the sampling probabilities to within
1\pm\varepsilon
error. This lets us obtain an
\varepsilon
distortion
\ell_{p}
embedding for the column space of
\mathbf{A}
. That the resulting subspace embedding is correct with probability
1-\delta
for these parameters follows from
[
WY23a
]
.

∎

2.1.3
Proof of Main Result for
\ell_{2}
-Regression with Known Conditioning

Finally for
\ell_{2}
regression, we observe that we can improve the bit complexity of our protocol provided that the system is known to be well conditioned.

To do this, we first note that the recursive halving algorithm of
[
CLMMPS15
]
(effectively
for
p=2
) works equally well for obtaining a spectral approximation to
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\lambda\mathbf{I}
. One simply runs the recursive leverage sampling algorithm on the matrix
[\mathbf{A};\sqrt{\lambda}\mathbf{I}].
If run directly the intermediate spectral approximations could be poorly conditioned. To fix this, when (recursively) running the “uniform sampling step” we simply insist on additionally sampling all rows in the
\sqrt{\lambda}\mathbf{I}
block with probability
1.
This gives a variant of the recursive leverage sampling procedure for which all intermediate spectral approximations have smallest eigenvalue lower bounded by
\lambda.
We make use of this algorithm below.

See
1.4

Proof.

We use a slight regularization to modify our protocol for computing a constant factor spectral approximation to
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
.
Let
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=[\mathbf{A};\sqrt{\lambda}\mathbf{I}_{d}]\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+d)\times d}
be the matrix formed by vertically concatenating
\mathbf{A}
and
\sqrt{\lambda}\mathbf{I}_{d}
, where
\lambda>0
is chosen with
\sqrt{\lambda}\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A})/2.
Then
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq{(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})\preceq\frac{3}{2}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
. Hence, to spectrally approximate
\mathbf{A}
, it suffices to find a constant-factor spectral approximation for
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}.

To do this we run our algorithm
discussed in
Corollary

2.9
above, but use the repeated halving algorithm above for obtaining a spectral approximation to
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}.
This simply requires a procedure for solving
2.1
under the assumption that
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{\top}
has largest eigenvalue at most
1/\lambda.
To replace the
sdL
dependence with
sd
in
Lemma

2.6
we need to communicate only the JL sketches
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
more efficiently.

Rather than communicating the sketches with bit-precision, suppose that the servers instead communicate
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}+\mathbf{E}^{(j)}
where
\mathbf{E}^{(j)}
represents some small error. As in
Lemma

2.6
, the coordinator will then compute

\sqrt{\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{(j)}}=\|(\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}+\mathbf{E}^{(j)})\mathbf{M}\|_{F}=\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}+\|\mathbf{E}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}.

We would like this quantity to approximate
\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}
to within a constant factor, so it suffices to ensure that

\|\mathbf{E}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}\leq\frac{1}{10}\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}.

Note that
\|\mathbf{E}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}\leq\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{M})\|\mathbf{E}^{(j)}\|_{F}
and that
\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\mathbf{M}\|_{F}\geq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{M})\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\|_{F}.
So we just need

\|\mathbf{E}^{(j)}\|_{F}\leq\frac{\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{M})}{\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{M})}\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\|_{F}=\frac{1}{\kappa(\mathbf{M})}\|\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}\|_{F}.

To accomplish this bound on the term, it is sufficient to communicate each entry of
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
to within
1\pm\kappa(\mathbf{M})^{-1}
multiplicative error. Since
\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M}
is obtained by subsampling from
\mathbf{A}^{\prime},
note that
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})+\lambda.
Therefore
\kappa(\mathbf{M})\leq 1+\frac{\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A})}{\lambda}.

To ensure
\lambda
is chosen small enough, set
\lambda=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}/(2\kappa\sqrt{d})\leq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A})/2.
Note also that
\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A})/\lambda=\frac{2\kappa\sqrt{d}\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A})}{\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}}\leq 2\kappa^{2},
which implies that
\kappa(\mathbf{M})\leq 1+2\kappa^{2}.

We now round each entry of
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
to the nearest value in

\{\pm(1+\kappa(\mathbf{M})^{-1})^{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

To communicate one of these numbers, we send only the sign and exponent. Moreover our
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}
sketch can be taken to be Rademacher, so the entries of
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
can be expressed with
L+\log d
bits. Hence we need only
O(\log(\kappa(\mathbf{M})L)=O(\log(\kappa L))
bits per entry to send our approximation of
\mathbf{S}^{(j)}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
. The improvement of
1/\varepsilon^{2}
to
1/\varepsilon
follows from Sarlos’s trick mentioned in
Corollary

2.9

∎

2.2
Second Protocol: Block Leverage Score Sketching

In this section we use block leverage scores to design an algorithm for constructing an
\ell_{2}
subspace embedding. The coordinator needs to send only
O(\log d)
bits to the servers over the course of
d
rounds. We summarize the result here which will be proven in the following subsections.

Theorem 2.8
.

There is a protocol that constructs an
(1\pm\varepsilon)
distortion
\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for
\mathbf{A}
which runs in
O(\log d)
rounds, and uses
\widetilde{O}(sd+\varepsilon^{-2}d^{2})
communication. Moreover the servers collectively receive a total of only
\widetilde{O}(s)
bits from the coordinator.

Proof.

Theorem

2.20
in the following section will give a protocol for estimating the block leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
to within constant factors. Given such estimates of the block leverage scores,
Theorem

2.23
gives a sampling algorithm to compute a subspace embedding for
\mathbf{A}.
Combining these algorithms gives the guarantee stated here.
∎

As an corollary, we can obtain slightly improved
\varepsilon
dependence for
\ell_{2}
regression.

Corollary 2.9
.

There is a protocol using
\widetilde{O}(sdL+d^{2}{\varepsilon^{-2}}L)
communication that allows the coordinator to produce an
\varepsilon
-distortion
\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for the column span of
\mathbf{A}
, with a failure probability of at most
\delta.
As a consequence, the linear regression problem can be solved with communication
\widetilde{O}(sdL+d^{2}\varepsilon^{-1}L).

Proof.

This follows immediately from the
\ell_{p}
result in
Theorem

1.5
.
However, the above procedure requires a
d\varepsilon^{-2}
dependence to produce a subspace embedding for
\mathbf{A}.
For simply solving the linear regression problem to
1+\varepsilon
multiplicative accuracy, we note that a trick due to Sarlos
[
Sar06
]
allows this to be improved to
\frac{d}{\varepsilon}
.

An argument for this is given in the proof of
[
Woo+14
, Theorem 3.1]
when the subspace embedding is a random sign matrix. However, the same proof applies for leverage score sampling matrices, by using
[
CW17
, Lemma 7.3]
, which shows that leverage score sampling matrices yield an approximate-matrix-multiplication guarantee (and noting that if
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Sigma}\mathbf{V^{T}}
is the singular value decomposition of
\mathbf{A}
, then the leverage scores of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{A}
coincide).

In fact, it is true more generally (although not widely known) that an
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
-distortion subspace embedding suffices to solve the
\ell_{2}
-regression problem to
1+\varepsilon
accuracy
[
BDN15
]
.
∎

2.2.1
Block Leverage Scores: Definition and Basic Properties

Our second protocol is based on the notion of
block leverage scores
, which may be of independent interest. The block leverage score is simply the sum of the leverage scores of the rows in a block (i.e., matrix stored on a server), where the leverage score of each row is with respect to the entire matrix. Intuitively, therefore, the block leverage score captures the importance of the block in the overall rowspace of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
. It is known
[
CLMMPS15
]
that the sum of the (block) leverage scores is at most the rank of the matrix (and hence by
d
in our setting).

Our key technical result is that a simple sketch suffices to estimate the block leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}]
. Our approach is to sketch each block down to roughly an
O(k)\times d
matrix using a separate Rademacher (or other) sketch
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
for each block. The block leverage scores are then estimated to be the those of
[\mathbf{S}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{S}^{(s)}\mathbf{A}^{(s)}]
. Unfortunately, this does not necessarily yield good estimates for all blocks. Indeed the block leverage scores of the sketched matrix are all bounded by
O(k)
, so we may underestimate the scores of outlying blocks. However, by introducing a characterization of the block leverage scores as a
block sensitivity
, we show that we obtain good (over-)estimates for all block leverage scores smaller than
Ck.
Additionally, we can detect those blocks for which our estimates are not good; their leverage score estimates are guaranteed to be larger than
Ck.
These observations yield a simple iterative procedure for computing block leverage score estimates.

Estimating Block Leverage Scores.

In the first round, the coordinator requests a roughly
1\times d
sketch from each block, yielding good estimates of the block leverage scores for all blocks with block leverage score at most
1.
The coordinator now needs to focus only on those blocks with estimated block leverage score larger than
1
, of which there are at most
O(d)
, since the block leverage scores sum to at most
d
. The coordinator then requests roughly a
2\times d
sketch from each server whose block leverage score estimate is large (where the estimate came via the sketch)
This yields good estimates for blocks with leverage score at most
2
and underestimates for those with leverage scores larger than
2
, of which there are at most
d/2.
This procedure is repeated, doubling the number of rows requested in each round while halving the number of servers from which these rows are requested. More generally, in round
r
the server requests a sketch of size
2^{r}\times d
from each of approximately
d/2^{r}
servers. So the procedure requires
O(d^{2})
communication per round, with a cost of
O(sd)
in the very first round. After
O(\log d)
rounds, we find good estimates for all the blocks, and hence use
\widetilde{O}(sd+d^{2})
communication.

Given estimates for the block leverage scores, a “block” version of the standard leverage score sampling algorithm suffices to construct a subspace embedding for the column span of
\mathbf{A}
: We choose blocks proportional to the estimated block leverage scores and then sample a
1\times d
sketch of the rows from that block. By taking
d\varepsilon^{-2}\log d
such samples
we obtain our desired subspace embedding with distortion
\varepsilon.
The entire algorithm (estimating the block leverage scores and sampling) can be implemented simultaneously by having each server send twice as many rows during the leverage score estimation algorithm. The extra rows can then be used later during the sampling phase. Hence the algorithm is nearly one-way in the sense that coordinator needs to communicate only
O(s+d)
bits in total, and only for the purposes of notifying the servers that are active in that round.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}].
We define the block leverage score of block
{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}
to be

\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})=\Tr\left({\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}({\mathbf{A}^{(i)}})^{\top}\right).

(2.1)

For use throughout, we list a few basic properties of the block leverage scores.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}].
The following properties hold:

1.

If
{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, and the rows of
{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}
have leverage scores
\ell_{i1},\ldots,\ell_{ik},
(computed with respect to
\mathbf{A}
) then the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
block leverage score
\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell_{ij}.

2.

The sum of all block leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{s}\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{A}^{(i)}})=\rank(\mathbf{A}).

3.

Consider the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)};\mathbf{A}^{(s+1)}].
For all
i\in[s]
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathcal{L}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}).

Proof.

For property 1, we observe that

\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})=\Tr({\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}{{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}}^{\top})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}_{j:}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}_{j:}}^{\top}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell_{ij}.

In light of property 1, properties 2 and 3 follow from the corresponding facts for classical leverage scores.
∎

We also give a characterization of the block leverage score as a block sensitivity,
which will be the key to our analysis of the block leverage score sketch in the following section.
The
sensitivity sampling
framework was introduced by
[
LS10
]
and was shown recently in
[
WY23
]
to be advantageous over Lewis weights sampling
[
CP15
]
in numerous settings such as under small total sensitivity and structured data matrix (such as low-rank, sparsity, etc. as studied in, e.g.,
[
MMMWZ22
]
). Algorithms for approximating
\ell_{p}
sensitivities have been improved by works such as
[
PWZ23
]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Given a full column rank matrix
\mathbf{A}
consisting of blocks
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{A}^{(s)}
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})=\sup_{\mathbf{X}}\frac{\|\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}},

where the supremum is is over all matrices with dimensions compatible with
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
be the singular value decomposition for
\mathbf{A}
, and let
\mathbf{U}_{j}
have a subset of the rows of
\mathbf{U}
so that
\mathbf{U}_{j}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}=\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
. We are interested in maximizing
\|(\mathbf{U}_{j}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}=||\mathbf{U}_{j}(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{X})\|_{F}^{2}
subject to
\|\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}=1
. Since
\mathbf{U}
is orthonormal, the constraint becomes
\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}=1
.
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
has full rank so the optimization problem is equivalent to maximizing
\|\mathbf{U}_{j}\mathbf{Y}\|_{F}^{2}
s.t.
\|\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}\leq 1
. This is optimized for
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{I}
and the objective is the sum of squares of row norms for
\mathbf{U}_{j}
which is the sum of leverage scores of the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
.
∎

2.2.2
Sketching Block Leverage Scores

We use our sensitivity characterization of the block leverage scores to show that sketching a block does not cause its leverage score to drop too much.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\mathbf{G}^{(1)}
be a sketching matrix which is an
O(1)
distortion oblivious subspace embedding for a
k
-dimensional subspace with a probability of at least
1-\delta
. Then, with a probability of at least
1-\delta
, we have that

\mathcal{L}_{1}\left([\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{A}^{(s)}]\right)\geq C\min(k,\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A})).

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.12
, we can choose an
\mathbf{X}
with
\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}/\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A})
.
[
CLMMPS15
, Theorem 1]
implies that

\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}+(1/k)\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2},

and so

\frac{\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}}\lesssim 1+\frac{1}{k}\frac{\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}}=1+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A})}{k}.

Then

\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\lesssim||\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}||_{2}+||\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}||_{2}\lesssim\left(1+\frac{1}{k}\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A})\right)||\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}||_{2}.

Hence

\frac{\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}}\gtrsim\frac{\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}X\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{2}}\gtrsim\min(k,\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A})),

by Johnson-Lindenstrauss, and the previous bound. Hence
\mathbf{X}
witnesses a sensitivity of at least
C\min(k,\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathbf{A}))
for the first block of
\mathbf{G}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}
as desired.
∎

Next we analyze the situation where all but one block is sketched. To streamline the argument we first lead with a few elementary claims.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be positive semidefinite, let
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
, and suppose that
\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{U})\leq\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}).
Then
\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{U})\geq\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}).

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{X}^{-1}
are simultaneously diagonalizable, the Löewner order inequality
\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{X}^{-1}\geq 2\mathbf{I}
follows from the scalar inequality
x+1/x\geq 2
for
x\geq 0.
Thus
\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{X}^{-1})\mathbf{U}\geq 2\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}
and so
\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{X}^{-1})\mathbf{U})\geq 2\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}).
Therefore by the assumption
\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{U})\leq\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})
, we have

\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{U})\geq 2\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})-\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{U})\geq 2\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})-\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})=\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}).\qed

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a random
d\times d
matrix which is a.s. PSD, and let
\mathbf{A}
be fixed matrix which is PSD and non-singular. Suppose that for every
\mathbf{U}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
it holds that

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{U})\leq\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U})\right)\geq 1-\delta.

Then for every
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
it also holds that

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\Tr(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{V})\geq\Tr(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{V})\right)\geq 1-\delta.

Proof.

Plugging
\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{V}
into the hypothesis gives that for all
\mathbf{V}
,

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\Tr(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{V})\leq\Tr(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{V}))\geq 1-\delta,

or equivalently

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\Tr((\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}))\leq\Tr((\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}))\right)\geq 1-\delta.

By
Proposition

2.14
, this gives that for all
V
,

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\Tr((\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{X}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}))\geq\Tr((\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}))\right)\geq 1-\delta,

which simplifies to the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}]
be non-singular and let
\mathbf{S}^{(1)},\ldots\mathbf{S}^{(s)}
be random sketching matrices of appropriate dimension so that the products
S^{(i)}A^{(i)}
are defined. Assume that each
S^{(i)}
satisfies the
(1,\delta,2)
-JL-moment property. Let
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Tr\left(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}{\mathbf{S}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{V}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(\mathbf{V}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{V}\right).

Proof.

We apply
Proposition

2.15
. Let
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
be an arbitrary fixed matrix. Then we have

\Tr\left(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}{\mathbf{S}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\right)\mathbf{U}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\|\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{U}\|_{F}^{2}.

The block matrix
\mathbf{S}^{(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbf{S}^{(s)}
also has the
(1,\delta,2)
-JL-moment property (see for example Lemma 13 of
[
Ahl+20
]
). So with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sum_{i=1}^{s}\|\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{U}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\|_{F}^{2}=2\Tr(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}).

The claim now follows by
Proposition

2.15
.
∎

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\dots,\mathbf{A}^{(s)}]
, let
\mathbf{S}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{S}^{(s)}
be sketching matrices satisfying the
(1,\delta,2)
-JL-moment property, and let
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=[\mathbf{S}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{S}^{(s)}\mathbf{A}^{(s)}],
where
\mathbf{S}_{k}=\mathbf{I}
for a fixed
k
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathcal{L}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathbf{A}).

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.16
(and the hypothesis that
\mathbf{S}^{(k)}=\mathbf{I}
), we have

\mathcal{L}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})=\Tr\left(\mathbf{A}^{(k)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}{\mathbf{S}^{(i)}}^{\top}{\mathbf{S}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{(k)}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(\mathbf{A}^{(k)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{(k)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathbf{A}).

∎

Combining the two block sketching results
Lemma

2.13
and
Lemma

2.17
gives the following.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\mathbf{S}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{S}^{(s)}
, each with
d
columns, all be (normalized) Rademacher with
O(k\log(s/\delta))
rows. Then for each
i
,

\mathcal{L}_{i}([\mathbf{S}^{(1)}\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{S}^{(s)}\mathbf{A}^{(s)}])\geq C\min\left(k,\mathcal{L}_{i}([\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}])\right),

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Remark 2.19
.

The sketches in the above result were taken to be Rademacher only for convenience. The same argument applies to sparse sketches for example.

2.2.3
Estimating Block Leverage Scores

The sketch from the previous section shows that we can accurately (over-)estimate a given block leverage score by sketching down to dimension roughly
k.
Unfortunately the block leverage scores can be as large as
d
and we are unable to take a sketch of
d
rows from all servers. Fortunately, not many servers can have large block leverage score, so by iteratively pruning off the ones that don’t, we can focus on the servers with the most information.

Input.
For
i\in[s]
, each server
i
has the block
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
of
\mathbf{A}

Output.
List
L
where
L[i]
estimates of the block leverage score of
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}

Initialize.

L\leftarrow[\perp,\ldots,\perp]
of length
s
,
\mathcal{S}_{0}\leftarrow\{1,\ldots,s\}

For
iterations
r=0,1,2,\dotsc,\lceil\log d\rceil
:

k_{r}\leftarrow 2^{r}

For

i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}

(a)

Each server
i
draws
\mathbf{S}_{r,i}
a constant distortion
k_{r}
-dimensional oblivious subspace embedding, and sends
\mathbf{S}_{r,i}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
to the coordinator

Coordinator forms block matrix
\mathbf{A}_{\langle r\rangle}
with blocks given by
\mathbf{S}_{r,i}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
for
i
in
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, and where the blocks are indexed by
\mathcal{S}_{r}

For all
i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}
, coordinator computes
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}=\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}_{\langle r\rangle})

\mathcal{S}_{r+1}\leftarrow\{i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}:\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}\geq Ck_{r}\}

For

i
in
\mathcal{S}_{r}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{r+1}

(a)

L[i]\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}

For
all
i\in[s]
, if
L[i]=\perp
, then set
L[i]\leftarrow d

Return:

L
.

Algorithm 4
:
Block leverage score estimation.

alg]alg:block_lev_est

Theorem 2.20
.

runs with
O(\log d)
rounds of communication, and returns a list
L
satisfying

(i)

L[i]\geq C\mathcal{L}_{i}([\mathbf{A}^{(1)};\ldots;\mathbf{A}^{(s)}])
for all
i

(ii)

\sum_{i=1}^{s}L[i]\leq O(d\log d)

Moreover the servers collectively send at most
O(cs+cd\log d)
vectors of length
d
to the coordinator.

Proof.

We start by bounding the number of servers which are active in a given round. In round
0
,
|\mathcal{S}_{0}|=s.
For
r\geq 1
, note that for every server
i
in
\mathcal{S}_{r}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r-1,i}\geq Ck_{r-1}.
On the other hand there cannot be many such servers, since by
Proposition

2.11
,

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r-1,i}\leq d,

which implies that
|\mathcal{S}_{r}|\leq\frac{d}{Ck_{r-1}}.
This immediately gives a bound on the communication cost. Summing the number of vectors transmitted in each round gives a total of

\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil\log d\rceil}ck_{r}|\mathcal{S}_{r}|\leq c\left(sk_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil\log d\rceil}k_{r}\frac{d}{Ck_{r-1}}\right)=c\left(s+\frac{2d}{C}\lceil\log d\rceil\right)

vectors sent to the coordinator. Next we show that (i) holds. By the algorithm, note that either
L[i]=d
or on round
r
we set
L[i]=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}
. In the first case (i) is trivial since all block leverage scores are at most
d.
In the latter case,
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}\leq Ck_{r}.
But
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}\geq C\min\left(k_{r},\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}_{\langle r\rangle})\right)
by
Lemma

2.18
, which is at least
\min(Ck_{r},C\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}))
by monotonicity.
So we have
k_{r}\geq\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}),
which implies that

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}\geq C\min(k_{r},\mathcal{L}_{i}(A))=C\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}).

Finally we show (ii). Since we have

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{r,i}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{S}_{r}}\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A}_{\langle r\rangle})\leq d,

it follows that the entries of
L
which are set in round
r
sum to at most
d
. Hence the sum of the entries of
L
set in the outer for-loop is at most
(\lceil\log d\rceil+1)d.
By the argument given above for the communication cost, there are at most
\frac{d}{Ck_{r}}\leq\frac{1}{C}
entries of
L
which are not set after the loop.
These entries are set to
d
, which gives

\sum_{i=1}^{s}L[i]\leq(\lceil\log d\rceil+1)d+\frac{d}{C}\leq O(d\log d).

∎

2.2.4
Sampling via Block Leverage Scores

Given the overestimates computed for the block leverage scores in the previous section, a straightforward concentration bound allows to get a spectral approximation via block leverage sampling. By combining with the algorithm for estimating the block leverage scores, this immediately yields an algorithm with
\tilde{O}(sd+d^{2}\varepsilon^{-2})
communication in the coordinator model, for computing an
\varepsilon
distortion subspace embedding for the columns of
\mathbf{A}.

Input.
Sampling distribution
p
, number of samples
N

Output.
A spectral approximation
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
of
\mathbf{A}

Initialize.
Coordinator sets
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{0}

\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}

For
iterations
\ell=1,2,\dotsc,N
:

Sample server
j
from the distribution
p

Server
j
generates a Rademacher random vector
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}
and sends
\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
to coordinator

Coordinator appends
\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(j)}
to
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}

Return:

\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
.

Algorithm 5
:
Block leverage score sampling.

alg]alg:block_lev_sampling

As is standard for analyses of leverage score sampling, we rely on the Matrix Chernoff bound (see
[
Woo+14
]
for example). We state a version here which follows from
[
Tro12
]
. The version we use is slightly less general, but more convenient for our purposes.

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be random matrices which are independent and symmetric PSD, with
\mu_{\min}I\leq\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}_{i}\leq\mu_{\max}I,
and
\|\mathbf{X}_{i}\|\leq R
a.s. Let
\overline{\mathbf{X}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{X}_{i}.
Then for all
\delta\in[0,1),

\operatorname{Pr\/}\lparen\lambda_{\max}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})\geq(1+\delta)\mu_{\max}\rparen\leq d\exp\left(-\delta^{2}\frac{N\mu_{\max}}{3R}\right).

\operatorname{Pr\/}\lparen\lambda_{\max}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})\leq(1-\delta)\mu_{\min}\rparen\leq d\exp\left(-\delta^{2}\frac{N\mu_{\min}}{2R}\right).

The quantities that we apply the matrix Chernoff bound to will have operator norm given by a Hutchinson trace estimator
[
Hut89
]
. This standard application of Matrix Chernoff is the core argument. The additional work simply fixes a technical issue.

Hutchinson’s estimator may very occasionally be much larger than expected, which would require
R
in
Theorem

2.21
to be undesirably large. Fortunately Hutchinson’s estimator has exponential tail decay, and so these potential large values may be safely ignored with high probability. To make this precise, we will use the following technical fact, which is effectively a restatement of results in
[
DM21
]
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be symmetric positive semidefinite, and let
g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Rademacher random vector. Let
\mu=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{g})=\Tr(\mathbf{A}).
Then

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{g}\geq t)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}t/\mu},

for all
t\geq c_{3}\mu.
The
c_{i}
’s are positive absolute constants.

Proof.

We set
\ell=1
in Claim A.3 of
[
DM21
]
. By bounding
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}
each by
\Tr(\mathbf{A})=\mu
, we may take
\nu=c_{4}\mu
and
\beta=c_{5}\mu
in Claim A.3. By properties of subexponential random variables given in
[
Wai15
]
, it then follows that

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{g}\geq\mu+t)\leq 2e^{-c_{6}t/\mu}
for
t\geq\nu^{2}/\beta=c_{7}\mu,
which by adjusting constants rearranges to claim above.
∎

Theorem 2.23
.

Suppose that the input to
satisfies
p_{i}\geq\beta\frac{\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})}{d}
for some
\beta\in(0,1]
, and with
N\geq\Omega\left(\frac{d}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\log\lparen\frac{d}{\beta\varepsilon}\rparen\log d\right),
where
\varepsilon<1
. Then the output
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
of
satisfies

(1-\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\leq\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}.

Proof.

Let
X_{k}
be distributed as
\frac{1}{p_{j}}\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{g}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{j}
where the index
j
is drawn from
p
, and
\mathbf{g}
is independently drawn as a Rademacher random vector. Then
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
is distributed as
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbf{X}_{k}
, so we show concentration for this average. As is standard in such arguments, we show that the following equivalent statement holds with the desired probability:

(1-\varepsilon)\mathbf{I}\leq(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{I}.

First note that

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{X}_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}p_{i}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}}(\frac{1}{p_{i}}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{g}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{g}){\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A},

since
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{g}^{\top}\mathbf{g})=I.
Let
\mathbf{Y}_{k}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}_{k}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}
, and note that by the above,
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}_{k})=\mathbf{I}.
Next we have

\|\mathbf{Y}_{k}\|=\|(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\lparen\frac{1}{p_{i}}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\mathbf{g}\mathbf{g}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}\rparen(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\|=\mathbf{g}^{\top}\lparen\frac{1}{p_{i}}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\rparen\mathbf{g}.

For fixed
i
, this latter expression is the classic Hutchinson’s trace estimator for
\frac{1}{p_{i}}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}
, which has mean

\Tr\left(\frac{1}{p_{i}}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}\right)=\frac{1}{p_{i}}\mathcal{L}_{i}(\mathbf{A})\leq\frac{d}{\beta}.

So by
Proposition

2.22
,

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}_{k}\|\geq t)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}t/\mu},

(2.2)

for
t\geq c_{3}\mu
, where we set
\mu=d/\beta.
At this point we would like to apply Matrix Chernoff to the
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
’s. Unfortunately we cannot since the
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
’s are not bounded a.s. Therefore we let
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}
be the random variable obtained by conditioning
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
on the event that
\|\mathbf{Y}_{k}\|\leq M.
We will show below that taking
M=c_{5}\mu\log\frac{\mu}{\varepsilon}
gives
\|\mathbb{E}\mathbf{Y}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}\|\leq\varepsilon.
As a consequence this gives

(1-\varepsilon)\mathbf{I}\leq\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{I}.

Given this choice of
M
, we apply the Matrix Chernoff bound to the
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}
’s, which now satisfy
\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}\|\leq M
. Setting
\overline{\widetilde{Y}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{Y}_{k}
, and plugging into
Theorem

2.21
gives

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\overline{\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}}-\mathbf{I}\|\geq 3\varepsilon)\leq d\exp\lparen-c\varepsilon^{2}\frac{N}{M}\rparen,

which is bounded by
0.05
for
N\geq\Omega\lparen\frac{d}{\beta\varepsilon^{2}}\log\lparen\frac{d}{\beta\varepsilon}\rparen\log d\rparen.
(We replace
\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon/3
to recover the statement in the theorem.) Possibly by adjusting the constant in the definition of
M
, we can arrange so that with probability at least
0.95
, all of the
N
samples
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
are such that
\|\mathbf{Y}_{k}\|\leq M
(this follows from the exponential tail bound on the
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
’s), and hence indistinguishable from the
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}
’s. So with probability at least
0.9
the
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
’s enjoy the same concentration bound as the
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}
’s above, which then implies the conclusion of the theorem.

Finally we conclude the argument by showing that
\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}_{k}}
is approximately
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{Y}_{k}.
To simplify notation, let
\mathbf{Y}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}
be distributed as
\mathbf{Y}_{k}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}
respectively. We write

\mathbb{E}Y=\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}})\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\leq M)+\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M)\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M).

Thus we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}\mathbf{Y}-\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\leq M))\|\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|+\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M)\|

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\|\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|+\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M)\|

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|+\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\mathbb{E}(\|\mathbf{Y}\||\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{Y}\|+\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\mathbb{E}(\|\mathbf{Y}\||\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M),

where in the last step we observed that
\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|\leq\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{Y}\|
. We bound each of the relevant terms.

We will take
M\geq c_{3}\mu.
As shown above,
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{Y}\|\leq\mu.
By
Eq.

2.2
,
\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}M/\mu}
. To handle the last term,

\mathbb{E}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\bigg|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq t|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M\right)\,dt=M+\int_{M}^{\infty}\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq t|\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M\right)\,dt.

So

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)\mathbb{E}(\|\mathbf{Y}\||\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq M)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Pr\/}(\|\mathbf{Y}\|>M)M+\int_{M}^{\infty}\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\|\mathbf{Y}\|\geq t\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}M/\mu}M+\int_{M}^{\infty}c_{1}e^{-c_{2}t/\mu}\,dt

\displaystyle=c_{1}e^{-c_{2}M/\mu}M+c_{4}\mu e^{-c_{2}M/\mu}.

Putting the pieces together gives

\|\mathbb{E}\mathbf{Y}-\mathbb{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}\|\leq c_{1}\mu e^{-c_{2}M/\mu}+c_{1}Me^{-c_{2}M/\mu}+c_{4}\mu e^{-c_{2}M/\mu},

which is bounded by
\varepsilon
for
M\geq c_{5}\mu\log\left(\frac{\mu}{\varepsilon}\right).

∎

2.3
Low-Rank Matrix Approximation

In the preceding section, we showed how the coordinator can learn a subspace embedding matrix
\mathbf{S}
for
\mathbf{A}
. We now show how to utilize this embedding to learn with efficient communication a projection giving a good rank-
k
approximation to
\mathbf{A}
.

See
1.7

Proof.

We first reduce the number of columns
of
\mathbf{A}
by right-multiplying by a Rademacher random matrix
\mathbf{R}
with
O(k/\varepsilon)
columns. Note that this computation can be carried out locally on each server; each server simply computes
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{R}
, where
\mathbf{R}
is known using shared randomness. Since
\mathbf{R}
is Rademacher, the bit complexity of each
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{R}
is at most
L+O(\log d).

By
[
CW09
, Theorem 4.2]
, we have that

\min_{\textrm{rank}(\mathbf{X})=k}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)\|\mathbf{A}_{k}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F},

where
\mathbf{A}_{k}
is defined in
Section

1.1.1
as the best rank-
k
approximation of
\mathbf{A}.
We would like to find an approximate minimizer
\mathbf{X}
, since then
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}
is a rank-
k
approximation of
\mathbf{A}.
To do this efficiently, we use our protocol from
Section

2.1
6
6

6

One could also apply our protocol from
Section

2.2
(with minor modifications when we apply the black-boxed Lemma 32 below as the resulting matrix is not a true leverage score sampling matrix, since we sample sketches of the rows from each block).
to construct a row-sampling matrix
\mathbf{S}
from the leverage score distribution of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}
(up to constant factors on the probabilities), with
\widetilde{O}(k/\varepsilon)
rows. Running our protocol requires
\widetilde{O}\left((\frac{sk}{\varepsilon}+\frac{k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}})\cdot L\right)
communication (note that the rows of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}
have dimension
O(k/\varepsilon)
).
Then Lemma 32 and Theorem 36 of
[
CW17
]
together imply that

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})=k}\|\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\|_{F}=\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})=k}\|\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A})\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})=k}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}.

The coordinator can learn
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}
using total communication
\widetilde{O}(kdL/\varepsilon^{2}).
This allows the coordinator to compute
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}
with

\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})=k}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{A}_{k}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}.

Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection onto the row space of
\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}
which has dimension at most
k
by construction. For a given row
\mathbf{a}_{i}
of
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}
is in the row space of
\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}
. So
\|\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\Pi-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\|^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\|^{2}
, and hence

\|\mathbf{A}\Pi-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{A}_{k}-\mathbf{A}\|_{F}.

∎

3
High-Accuracy Linear Regression in the Coordinator Model

In this section, we present our result for the communication complexity of computing high-accuracy solutions to linear regression problems as in the setup of
1.3
. The primary result of this section is the following.

See
1.8

Our framework for achieving the results in
Theorem

1.8
builds upon Richardson’s iteration with preconditioning. This circumvents the need to send to the coordinator the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
— the approach in the previous best result
[
VWW20
]
for this problem, which incurs a communication cost
\Omega(sd^{2}L)
7
7

7

To see this, we observe that each of
s
servers computes and sends to the coordinator
\langle\mathbf{A}^{(i)},\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, each of which takes
O(d^{2})
bits.
, thus exceeding our targeted budget. To ensure convergence with this Richardson-style iteration, the key conceptual idea is to use a matrix
\mathbf{M}
that spectrally approximates
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
as a preconditioner. We construct
\mathbf{M}
via sampling with respect to overestimates of the leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
, which are computed using an iterative process we build upon the refinement sampling framework of
[
CLMMPS15
]
. The main novelty of our algorithm and analysis are in careful roundings and bit complexity analysis to guarantee convergence while ensuring a small number of bits are communicated. In
Section

3.1
, we discuss the main components of our algorithm and analysis.

3.1
An Overview of Our Algorithm and Analysis

Our main algorithm for solving the linear regression problem to high-accuracy is
. It has three main components that we explain next.

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}:=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and vector
\mathbf{b}:=[\mathbf{b}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine stores matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
and vector
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
; accuracy parameter
0<\varepsilon<1
; probability parameter
c
.

Output.
Vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\|\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\|_{2}+\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}

Run
with inputs
\mathbf{A}
and
c
. This returns
\widehat{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
, which satisfies
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A})
and
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
. We store the coordinates of
\widehat{\tau}
on the corresponding machines.

Using
\widehat{\tau}
from
Item

, each machine
i\in[s]
forms a diagonal sampling matrix
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\ni\mathbf{S}^{(i)}=\texttt{Sample}(\widehat{\tau},100,c)
(cf.
Definition

3.3
) and sends to the coordinator the following objects: the nonzero entries of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
and the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
corresponding to the nonzero entries of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
.

The coordinator forms the matrix

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1.1}}[\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]
,
where
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}
is the matrix left after removing the zero rows of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
. Set
\mathbf{M}=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
.

Implement the protocol in
with
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{b}
, and
\mathbf{M}
as input.

Algorithm 6
:
Protocol for linear regression in the coordinator setting.

alg]alg:lin-reg-coordinator-poly-cond

Leverage score computation.

The first step of
, as shown in
Item

, is to compute
\widehat{\tau}
, a vector of sufficiently accurate overestimates of
\tau(\mathbf{A})
, the true leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
. We do this using
, which iteratively refines our initial crude overestimates. In each iteration of
, we sample a matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
, a spectral approximation of
\mathbf{A}
, and use
(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\dagger}
to compute a new
\widehat{\tau}
with improved accuracy. This reduces
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}
by a constant factor in each iteration, and therefore
runs for only
O(\log n)
iterations before
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq O(d)
, the required accuracy of
\widehat{\tau}
. A detailed discussion is presented in
Section

3.2
.

Throughout
, the machines communicate to the coordinator only the
\widetilde{O}(d)
rows they sample locally according to their leverage score overestimates. The coordinator forms
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
, a constant-factor spectral approximation to
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
and sends to the machines its sketch formed by a combination of rounding and a Johnson-Lindenstrauss random projection. Then the machines use this sketch to update their overestimates to
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}
, an approximation of
\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}(\mathbf{A})
, the generalized leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
computed with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
.
While this approach is similar to that of
[
CLMMPS15
]
, our target communication complexity necessitates additional approximations, e.g. via roundings, of the objects we communicate between the coordinator and the machines (cf.
Item

and
Item

of
), whereas
[
CLMMPS15
]
is analyzed in exact arithmetic. We elaborate this in
Proposition

3.6
and the overall communication complexity bound for computing the leverage score overestimates in
Lemma

3.2
.

Sampling a spectral approximation.

After computing a sufficiently accurate
\widehat{\tau}
, in the next step (
Item

of
), each machine locally samples a set of rows according to its leverage score overestimates and communicates these to the coordinator. The coordinator uses these rows to form
\mathbf{M}
, the final spectral approximation of
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, as stated in
Item

of
.

Richardson-type iteration.

Finally, in
Item

of
,
\mathbf{M}^{-1}
is used as a preconditioner in a Richardson-type algorithm, displayed in
. In each iteration of
, a residual vector is computed on each machine and communicated to the coordinator. In
Item

and
Item

of
, the coordinator linearly combines these residual vectors from all the machines and communicates back to the machines a vector carefully designed to optimize communication complexity. The output of
is the solution to the given regression problem at the specified accuracy, with a communication cost of
\widetilde{O}(sd(L+\log\kappa)\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
(cf.
Lemma

3.10
). A detailed discussion is presented in
Section

3.3

Remark 3.1
.

The primary theme of all the components of
is the simple idea that we communicate only the bits that are necessary for the convergence of our algorithms. This idea is perhaps most apparent in the last component (
). To this end, we carefully round intermediate vectors and matrices (i.e., discard bits with low place values) and reuse some bits (e.g., by not communicating bits with high place values). The latter is possible essentially because in an iterative algorithm, when the solution is converging, the bits with high place values are the same from one iteration to the next.

Discussion of our analysis.

We conclude this overview by reiterating that although our approach is conceptually fairly simple, the overall algorithm and analysis are involved due to careful bit modifications in intermediate steps such as the JL projection, matrix inversions, and Richardson’s iteration to reduce the communication complexity while ensuring convergence. The careful bit modification and analysis in turn plays a crucial role in yielding improvements compared to
[
VWW20
]
, particularly for matrices with a condition number of
e^{o(dL)}
, as summarized in
Table

1
. In subsequent sections, we explain each of the components of our approach. We first discuss the computation of leverage score overestimates in
Section

3.2
and our iterative preconditioning approach for solving the linear regression problem in
Section

3.3
. We finally prove the main result of this section in
Section

3.4
.

3.2
Leverage Score Overestimates

We now focus on the point-to-point communication complexity of computing a vector of overestimates for leverage scores of the matrix. We emphasize that although
[
VWW20
]
considers computing the leverage scores in the blackboard model, this is significantly more complicated in the coordinator (point-to-point) setting due to bit complexity issues involving inverse of matrices. The main result of this section is
Lemma

3.2
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Given the linear regression setting of
1.3
with matrix
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
n\geq 5
, there is a randomized algorithm that, with

\widetilde{O}(d^{2}L+sd(L+\log(\kappa)))\text{ bits of communication}

and, with high probability, computes a vector
\widehat{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
and
\widehat{\tau}_{i}\geq\tau_{i}(\mathbf{A})
, for all
i\in[n]
, where
\tau_{i}(\mathbf{A})
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
leverage score of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
. Each entry of
\widehat{\tau}
is stored on the machine that contains the corresponding row.

We achieve the results in
Lemma

3.2
via
. As described in
Section

3.1
, this algorithm is then used as a black-box in our main algorithm (
) as a pre-cursor to construct a spectral approximation of
\mathbf{A}
.

is based on the
Refinement Sampling
algorithm of
[
CLMMPS15
]
to approximately compute leverage scores of a matrix, with appropriate modifications for the coordinator setting. We provide an overview of the distributed version of
Refinement Sampling
in
Section

3.2.1
and prove
Lemma

3.2
in
Section

3.2.4
. While the results of
[
CLMMPS15
]
are provided under the exact arithmetic model, whereas we need to employ careful bit complexity analysis under fixed-point arithmetic.

3.2.1
An Overview of Refinement Sampling

The
Refinement Sampling
algorithm returns a small-sized spectral approximation to an input matrix
\mathbf{A}
by iteratively refining (hence the name)
\widehat{\tau}
, the leverage score overestimates of
\mathbf{A}
; when
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq O(d)
, it is accurate enough for use (with appropriate scaling) in sampling a matrix with the desired approximation guarantee, leading to termination of the algorithm.

The algorithm starts by setting
\widehat{\tau}=\mathbf{1}
, the vector of all ones. In each iteration, the algorithm uses
\widehat{\tau}
to construct a matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{n}\times d}
composed of a subset of
\widetilde{n}
(rescaled) rows of
\mathbf{A}
, where each row
i
is sampled independently with probability
\mathbf{p}_{i}\propto\alpha\widehat{\tau}_{i}
for some sampling rate
\alpha>0
(cf.
Definition

3.3
for the precise row sampling). The vector
\widehat{\tau}
is then updated to
\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}(\mathbf{A})
, the generalized leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
(cf.
Definition

1.23
), and this process continues iteratively.

We pick
\alpha
such that the number of rows of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
, which is proportional to
\alpha\cdot\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}
, is
\widetilde{O}(d)
with high probability. This ensures (see
Item

) that the number of rows communicated between the coordinator and machines is only
\widetilde{O}(d)
, incurring a bit complexity of
\widetilde{O}(d^{2}L)
.
Therefore, iteratively reducing
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}
by a constant factor enables a corresponding increase in
\alpha
, which eventually yields the desired spectral approximation (
Lemma

3.4
and
Lemma

3.5
, first stated in
[
CLMMPS15
]
), all the while maintaining a row size of
\widetilde{O}(d)
for the matrix used to compute generalized leverage scores. We state these formal guarantees next since they are used in our proof of correctness of
.

Definition 3.3

(Sampling Function
[
CLMMPS15
]
)
.

Given a vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
, a parameter
\alpha>0
, and a positive constant
c
, we define vector
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
as
\mathbf{p}_{i}=\min(1,\alpha c\log d\cdot\mathbf{u}_{i})
. We define the function
\texttt{Sample}(\mathbf{u},\alpha,c)
to be one which returns
a random diagonal
n\times n
matrix
\mathbf{S}
with independently chosen entries:

\mathbf{S}_{ii}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathbf{p}_{i}}}&\mbox{}\textrm{with probability }\mathbf{p}_{i}\\
0&\mbox{}\textrm{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

The spectral approximation guarantees of this sampling approach are formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4

(Spectral Approximation via Leverage Score Sampling; Lemma 4 of
[
CLMMPS15
]
)
.

Given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, a sampling rate
\alpha>1
, and a fixed constant
c>0
. Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
be a vector of leverage score overestimates, that is,

\mathbf{u}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A}),\,\,\text{ and }\mathbf{S}:=\texttt{Sample}(\mathbf{u},\alpha,c)

as in
Definition

3.3
. Then, with probability at least
1-d^{-c/3}-(3/4)^{d}
, the following results hold:

\textbf{nnz}({\mathbf{S}})=2c\alpha\|\mathbf{u}\|_{1}\log d\text{ and }\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{-1/2}}}\mathbf{SA}\approx_{\left(\frac{1+\alpha^{-1/2}}{1-\alpha^{-1/2}}\right)}\mathbf{A}.

The following lemma formalizes how
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{1}
shrinks when we use a spectral approximation
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
obtained by the sampling approach of
Definition

3.3
(cf.
Lemma

3.4
) to update
\widehat{\tau}
(the leverage score estimates) to
\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}(\mathbf{A})
(the generalized leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
with respect to
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
).

Lemma 3.5

(Leverage Score Estimate Update via Undersampling; Theorem 3 of
[
CLMMPS15
]
)
.

Given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and an undersampling parameter
\alpha\in(0,1]
, let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
be vectors such that:

\mathbf{u}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A}),\,\,\mathbf{S}:=\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\texttt{Sample}(\mathbf{u},9\alpha,c),\text{ and }\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\textrm{new})}:=\min\{\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{A}),\mathbf{u}_{i}\}\text{ for all }i\in[n].

Then, with probability at least
1-d^{-c/3}-(3/4)^{d}
,
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\textrm{new})}
is a leverage score overestimate, i.e.,
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\textrm{new})}\geq\tau_{i}(\mathbf{A})
. Furthermore,

\|\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{1}\leq 3d/\alpha\text{ and }\textbf{nnz}(\mathbf{S})=O(\alpha\cdot\|\mathbf{u}\|_{1}\cdot\log d).

3.2.2
Invariance of Leverage Score Overestimates:
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A})

The bulk of communication in
happens in

Item

and
Item

. In
Item

, we send
only the rows of the original matrix and the associated vector of probabilities. We construct the probabilities to be powers of two so that they can be communicated with a small number of bits. However, in
Item

, we need to send the product of the inverse of a matrix with other matrices. To do this, we need to round the product and communicate the rounded version. The following two technical lemmas (
Proposition

3.6
and
Proposition

3.7
) help us bound the error in computing the generalized leverage scores arising from this rounding process.

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}:=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, where
n=\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}
and the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine stores matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
; probability parameter
c
.

Output.
Vector
\widehat{\tau}
that satisfies
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A})
and
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
. The
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine stores
\widehat{\tau}^{(i)}
, the set of coordinates of
\widehat{\tau}
corresponding to the rows of
\mathbf{A}
stored on that machine.

Initialize.
Set the total number of iterations
T=\left\lceil\log_{2}(n/d)\right\rceil
and
r=10^{6}\cdot\log n
. Set the leverage scores estimate vector
\widehat{\tau}=\mathbf{1}
. Set
\lambda=\frac{1}{100\kappa^{2}}
, where
\kappa
is the condition number of
\mathbf{A}
.

For
iterations
\ell=1,2,\dots,T
:

Each machine
i
computes
t_{i}=\|\widehat{\tau}^{(i)}\|_{1}
, the sum of its leverage score overestimates, and sends it to the coordinator.

The coordinator computes
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}t_{i}
and sends it to all the machines.

All machines set
\widehat{\alpha}
to the smallest power of half
that is at least
\frac{25d\log d}{t}
. Set
\alpha=\min\{1,\frac{\widehat{\alpha}}{\log d}\}
.
Each machine
i
forms an
n_{i}
-by-
n_{i}
random diagonal matrix
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}=\texttt{Sample}(1.01\widehat{\tau}^{(i)},9\alpha,c)
as per
Definition

3.3
and sends to the coordinator those rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
and coordinates of
\widehat{\tau}^{(i)}
that correspond to the nonzero entries of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
.

Using the received entries of
\widehat{\tau}^{(i)}
for all
i\in[s]
, the coordinator computes
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}
, the non-zero rows of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}

as per
Definition

3.3
. It then constructs the matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{(i)}=\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
and sets
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=[\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{n}\times d}

and
\mathbf{B}=[\widetilde{\mathbf{A}};\sqrt{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{I}]\in\mathbb{R}^{(\widetilde{n}+d)\times d}
.

The coordinator samples a JL sketching matrix
\mathbf{G}\in\{-1,+1\}^{r\times(\widetilde{n}+d)}
and uses
\mathbf{B}
from the previous step to compute

\widehat{\mathbf{G}}:=\frac{\sqrt{1.01}}{0.99\sqrt{r}}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
. It then generates
\mathbf{J}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
by rounding the entries of
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
so that for all
i_{1},i_{2}
,
|\widehat{g}_{i_{1}i_{2}}-j_{i_{1}i_{2}}|<\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}d\sqrt{r}\cdot 2^{L}}
and sends
\mathbf{J}
to all the machines.

The coordinator computes an integer basis for the kernel of
[\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]
(e.g., by Gaussian elimination). It computes a linear combination
\mathbf{v}
of the kernel basis by picking independent and uniformly random coefficients for in
[-2^{dL},2^{dL}]
. It picks
z=\left\lceil 100\log(n)\right\rceil
random prime numbers
y_{1},\ldots,y_{z}
less than
(dL)^{2}
. For each prime number
y_{k}
, it sets
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}
to be
\mathbf{v}
modulo
y_{k}
. It then sends all
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}
’s and
y_{k}
’s to all the machines.

If for all
k\in[z]
,
\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{(k)}=0
modulo
y_{k}
, then we set
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\infty
(on the machine holding row
\mathbf{a}_{j}
). Otherwise,
we set
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. Then we update
\widehat{\tau}_{j}^{\textrm{new}}
to the smallest power of two that is at least
\max\left\{\min\left\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}\right\},\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\right\}
.

Each machine updates its overestimates with
\widehat{\tau}_{j}=1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}_{j}^{\textrm{new}}
.

Algorithm 7
:
Protocol for computing leverage score overestimates in the coordinator setting

alg]alg:levscoresRefinementSampling

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
0<\widehat{\varepsilon}<1
