be an accuracy parameter, and let
\mathbf{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be vectors satisfying, for all
i\in[d]
, that
|u_{i}-\widetilde{u}_{i}|\leq\widehat{\varepsilon}
. Then for any
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
|{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{v}}|\leq\sqrt{d}\cdot\widehat{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}
. Moreover if
\mathbf{B},\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
such that for all
i\in[m],j\in[d]
,
|b_{ij}-\widetilde{b}_{ij}|\leq\widehat{\varepsilon}
, then

|\|\mathbf{B}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}-\|\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}|\leq\sqrt{md}\cdot\widehat{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}.

Proposition 3.7

(Approximating Ridge Leverage Scores with Leverage Scores)
.

Let

\tau(\mathbf{A})
and
\tau^{\lambda}(\mathbf{A})
be the vector of leverage scores and
\lambda
-ridge leverage scores (see
Definition

1.25
), respectively, of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
n\geq d
. Let
\lambda\geq 0
and
\sigma_{\min}
be the smallest
nonzero
singular value of
\mathbf{A}
. Then

\frac{\sigma_{\min}^{2}+\lambda}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\geq\tau_{j}(\mathbf{A})\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\text{ for all $j\in[n]$. }

Equipped with these technical results, we prove the different components of
Lemma

3.2
, starting with the invariant of
that the leverage score overestimates vector
\widehat{\tau}
indeed always remains larger than
\tau^{\lambda}(\mathbf{A})
, the
\lambda
-ridge leverage scores vector of
\mathbf{A}
. As we see in
Lemma

3.2
, this implies
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau(\mathbf{A})
.

Lemma 3.8

(Invariance of Leverage Score Overestimates)
.

Assume that at the start of each iteration
\ell\in[T]
of
, we have
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau^{\lambda}(\mathbf{A})
. Then, at the end of this iteration, we have
\widehat{\tau}^{\textrm{(new)}}\geq\tau^{\lambda}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.8
.

The random vector
\mathbf{v}
computed in
Item

of
satisfies
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})
(we remove the
\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}
coefficient only to be sure that the matrix is an integer matrix and we can find an integer basis for the kernel). Therefore if a vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
, then
\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{v}=0
as well as
\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{(k)}=0
. Otherwise, if
\mathbf{w}\notin\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
, then with high probability,
\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\neq 0
. Since both
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{a}_{j}
are integer vectors, we can look at
\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
modulo
y_{k}
. Since we are using Gaussian elimination to compute the integer basis for the kernel and since the
\mathbf{A}
is an integer matrix with bit complexity
L
,
|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}|\leq\operatorname{poly}(n)\cdot 2^{dL}
. Therefore the number of prime factors of
|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}|
is
O(dL\log n)
. Therefore if we select a random prime number less than
(dL)^{2}
, then with a large probability, we pick a prime
y_{k}
that is not a factor of
|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}|\leq\operatorname{poly}(n)\cdot 2^{dL}
. Therefore if
|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}|\neq 0
, then with high probability,
|\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{v}|
modulo
y_{k}
is also not zero. In
Item

of
, we select multiple random primes independently to boost this probability even further. Then by taking union bound over all the rows of
\mathbf{A}
, the algorithm, with high probability, can detect which ones are orthogonal to the kernel of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
. Thus if
\mathbf{a}_{j}\notin\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
,
then with high probability, we set
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\infty
, and therefore (from
Item

), we have the desired inequality

\widehat{\tau}_{j}^{(\textrm{new})}\geq\max\left\{\min\left\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}\right\},\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\right\}\geq\widehat{\tau}_{j}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A}),

where the final inequality follows from the lemma’s assumption.

Next, consider the case in which
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
. In this case,
Item

of
sets
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
for
\mathbf{J}
as defined in
Item

of
. We now proceed to show
\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
; since the lemma assumes
\widehat{\tau}_{j}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
, we then have that

\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\implies\widehat{\tau}_{j}^{\textrm{new}}\geq\max\left\{\min\left\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}\right\},\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\right\}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A}),

which is the desired inequality.
In the rest of this proof, we show
\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
.

First note that
\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\mathbf{S}^{(i)}:=\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\texttt{Sample}(1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}^{(i)},9\alpha,c)
(as set in
Item

of
) has a distribution equivalent to

\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}:=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}}\texttt{Sample}(1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}^{(i)},9,c)
with some nonzero entries set to zero. This is because in
Item

of
, we set
\alpha\leq 1
, which implies that
\texttt{Sample}(1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}^{(i)},9\alpha,c)
has a smaller sampling rate compared to
\texttt{Sample}(1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}^{(i)},9,c)
for all entries, but the value of any entry selected in both matrices (i.e., nonzero entry) is the same in
\sqrt{\frac{3\alpha}{4}}\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}
. Therefore by
Lemma

3.4
and since
1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}
is a vector of overestimates for leverage scores of
\mathbf{A}
, with high probability,

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\preceq\mathbf{A}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A},

where
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the diagonal matrix obtained by putting
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}
together (as block-diagonals of
\mathbf{S}
). Therefore, for
\mathbf{B}=[\mathbf{A};\sqrt{\lambda}\mathbf{I}]
as defined in
Item

, we have

\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}+\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I}.

Thus
(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\succeq(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I})^{-1}
, and

\tau^{\mathbf{B}}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
, for all
j\in[n]
. Moreover,

\tau^{\mathbf{B}}_{j}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\mathbf{a}_{j}=\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\mathbf{a}_{j}=\|\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}.

Consequently, by
Lemma

1.20
, for
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}
, as defined in
Item

of
, and any
j\in[n]
, since
\frac{(0.01^{2}-0.01^{3})\cdot r}{4}>20\log n
,

\frac{101}{99}\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\leq\frac{101}{99}\tau^{\mathbf{B}}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\leq\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\mathbf{B}}_{j}(\mathbf{A}),

with probability at least
1-2n^{-20}
. Taking the union bound, with probability of at least
1-2n^{-19}
,
Section

3.2.2
holds for all
j\in[n]
, where
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
. Now
by
Proposition

3.6
, we have

|\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}-\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}|\leq\sqrt{rd}\cdot\varepsilon\|\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}\leq d\sqrt{r}\cdot\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}d\sqrt{r}2^{L}}\cdot 2^{L}=\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}.

We now consider two cases.

Case 1:

\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\geq\tau_{j}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{A})
.

Then by the construction in
Item

of
we have

\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A}),

which is the claim of the lemma.

Case 2:

\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})>\frac{1}{2n^{2}}
.

Recall that our goal is to show
\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A}).
For the sake of contradiction, suppose instead that
\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}<\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
; then we have

\left(\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}\right)^{2}<\left(\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})+\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})+\frac{1}{5000n^{2}}+\frac{1}{10^{8}n^{4}}\leq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})+\frac{1}{2500n^{2}},

where the penultimate inequality follows from the fact that
\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\leq 1
. Further, observe that we may combine
Section

3.2.2
and
Section

3.2.2
to conclude

\frac{101}{99}\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})\leq\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left(\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}\right)^{2}.

Combining
Section

3.2.2
with
Section

3.2.2
,
we have

\frac{2}{99}\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})<\frac{1}{2500n^{2}},

which is a contradiction to our assumption that
\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})>\frac{1}{2n^{2}}
. This shows
\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}(\mathbf{A})
, which then finishes the proof because of
Section

3.2.2
.

∎

3.2.3
\ell_{1}
Bound on Leverage Score Overestimates:
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq O(d)

Lemma 3.9
.

The vector
\widehat{\tau}
returned by
satisfies
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq O(d)
.

Proof.

Define the vector

\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}:=\min\{\tau_{j}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}},\widehat{\tau}_{j}\}.

Note that the vector
\mathbf{u}^{\textrm{(new)}}_{j}
is not the same as
\min\{\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j},\widehat{\tau}_{j}\}
as seen in
Item

of
. In
Item

of
, if
\mathbf{a}_{j}\notin\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
, then we set
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}=\infty
, with high probability. Otherwise, we have
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
, and we set
\widehat{\tau}_{j}=\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. Then, by
Section

3.2.2
and the fact that
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\preceq\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}+\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I}:=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}})^{\perp}
, we have

\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\mathbf{B}}_{j}\leq\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}.

Case 1:

\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}<\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}
.

By combining the assumed inequality with
Section

3.2.3
, we get

\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}.

Therefore, we may combine
Item

of
with the value of
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}
, definition of
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textrm{new}}
and
Section

3.2.3
to get

\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}\leq 2\cdot\min\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}\}+2\cdot\frac{1}{2n^{2}}=2\cdot\min\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\leq 2\cdot\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\leq 2.1\cdot\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Case 2:

\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}\geq\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}
.

In this case, we may combine
Section

3.2.2
and
Section

3.2.3
to obtain

\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left(\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}+\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\frac{101}{99}\cdot\sqrt{\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}}+\frac{1}{10^{4}\cdot n^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq 1.05\cdot\tau^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}+\frac{1}{2500n^{2}},

where the last inequality follows from
\sqrt{x}\leq\max(x,1)
.
Therefore by the construction of
Item

of
, the value of
\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}
, definition of
\mathbf{u}_{j}^{\textrm{new}}
, and
Section

3.2.3
, we get

\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}\leq 2\cdot\min\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\widehat{\tau}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}}_{j}\}+2\cdot\frac{1}{2n^{2}}=2\cdot\min\{\widehat{\tau}_{j},\|\mathbf{J}\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\leq 2\cdot\frac{101^{2}}{99^{2}}\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\leq 2.1\cdot\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}_{j}+\frac{2502}{2500}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Combining
Section

3.2.3
and
Section

3.2.3
from the two cases and using
Lemma

3.5
, we have

\|\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{1}\leq 2.1\cdot\|\mathbf{u}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{+}\frac{2502}{2500}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\leq 2.1\cdot\frac{3d}{\alpha}+\frac{2502}{2500}\cdot\frac{1}{n}.

If
\alpha=1
, since
n,d\geq 1
,

\|\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{1}\leq 8d.

Otherwise,
\alpha\geq\frac{25d}{\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}}
. Therefore since
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\geq 1
and
n\geq 5
,

\|\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{1}\leq\frac{6.3}{25}\cdot\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}+\frac{2502}{2500}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}.

Therefore after
\left\lceil\log_{2}(n/d)\right\rceil
iterations, either
\alpha
becomes one, which means
\|\widehat{\tau}^{(\textrm{new})}\|_{1}\leq 8d
, or
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}
is cut by half in each iteration which means since at the beginning
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}=n
, at the end
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq d
. Finally in
Item

, we multiply the vector
\widehat{\tau}
by
1.01
which by
Proposition

3.7
and choice of
\lambda
is guaranteed to be a vector of leverage score overestimates. Moreover by the above discussion for the final
\widehat{\tau}
, we have
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 1.01\cdot 8d\leq 9d
.

∎

3.2.4
Correctness and Communication Complexity of Leverage Score Overestimates

We are now ready to prove the result stated at the start of
Section

3.2
, which we first restate below.
See
3.2

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

We first prove the correctness of
, and then we bound its communication complexity.

Correctness.

The algorithm initializes
\widehat{\tau}=\mathbf{1}
, which makes it a vector of leverage score overestimates. In
Lemma

3.8
, we show that throughout the algorithm, we maintain the invariant
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau^{\lambda}
. This implies for each
j\in[n]
:

1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}_{j}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}^{2}+\lambda}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\cdot\widehat{\tau}_{j}\geq\frac{\sigma_{\min}^{2}+\lambda}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\cdot\tau^{\lambda}_{j}\geq\tau_{j},

where the first inequality follows from
\sigma_{\min}\geq\frac{1}{\kappa}
and
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{100\kappa^{2}}
, the second inequality
is by the invariant
\widehat{\tau}_{j}\geq\tau^{\lambda}_{j}
, and the third inequality is
by
Proposition

3.7
. This implies
that
1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}
, which is used in
Item

of
for sampling
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
, satisfies
1.01\cdot\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau
. Finally,
Lemma

3.9
implies the claimed bound
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
.

Communication complexity.

Having proved the correctness of the returned output, we next bound the communication complexity of
.
First, note that by construction, the entries of
\widehat{\tau}
are always powers of two and in the interval
[\frac{1}{2n^{2}},1]
. Therefore communicating their summations in
Item

and
Item

of
can be done with
O(s\log n)
bits in each iteration. Therefore the cost of these steps over the course of the algorithm is
O(s\log^{2}n)
. The part of
Item

that forms
\widehat{\alpha}
and
\alpha
does not pose any communication cost since the machines can compute them given
t
.

In
Item

, by choice of
\alpha
, with high probability, we only need to communicate
O(d\log d)
rows of
\mathbf{A}
to the coordinator — note that the extra factor of
1.01
for the sampling only increases the number of sampled rows by a constant factor. Since each row is a vector of size
d
with bit complexity
L
, the cost of sending the rows to the coordinator is
O(d^{2}L\log d)
in each iteration. Moreover, as we mentioned, since the entries of
\widehat{\tau}
are always powers of two and in the interval
[\frac{1}{2n^{2}},1]
, we can send the entries of leverage score overestimates to the coordinator with
O(d\log(d)\log(n))
bits in each iteration. Therefore the total cost of this step over the course of the algorithm is
O(d^{2}L\log(d)\log^{2}(n))
.
Item

does not incur any communication cost.

Note that
\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}\succeq\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I}
. Therefore
(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\preceq\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{I}
. Moreover since
\widehat{\tau}\geq\frac{1}{2n^{2}}
and by choice of
\lambda
,
we have

\|\widehat{\mathbf{G}}\|_{F}\leq\operatorname{poly}(n)\cdot 2^{L}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda}=\operatorname{poly}(n)\cdot\kappa^{2}2^{L}.

Therefore due to error tolerance for
\mathbf{J}
described in
Item

, the bit complexity of
\mathbf{J}
is
O((L+\log\kappa)\cdot\log n)
. Therefore since the number of rows and columns of
\mathbf{J}
is
O(\log n)
and
d
, respectively, it can be sent to all the machines with
O(sd\cdot(L+\log\kappa)\cdot\log n)
bits of communication, which concludes the cost of
Item

in each iteration.

In
Item

, since the primes are less than
(dL)^{2}
, the primes and the entries of the vectors
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}
only need
O(\log(dL))
bits of communication. Therefore the total cost of
Item

in each iteration is
O(d\log(dL)\log n)
.

Finally, note that
Item

and
Item

do not pose any communication cost. The total communication complexity of
is then bounded by the above discussion.

∎

3.3
Richardson-Type Iteration with Preconditioning

The main export of this section is
Lemma

3.10
, wherein we use an approximate preconditioner (obtained by sampling according to leverage score overestimate computed using
and
Lemma

3.2
) within a Richardson-type iteration to solve our linear regression problem iteratively. As alluded to earlier, in
, in addition to appropriate roundings, we communicate only the functions of difference of consecutive vectors in the computation, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
. This prevents redundant communication and permits the claimed communication bounds in
Lemma

3.10
.

differs from the classic Richardson’s iteration in a few key aspects, which allow for smaller communication complexity. Richardson’s method requires
\log(\varepsilon^{-1})
iterations, in each of which, we multiply the current vector by the Gram matrix of the original matrix and inverse of the preconditioner. Such a multiplication increases the bit complexity of the vector by
\log(\kappa)+L
even if we use a rounded version of the inverse of the preconditioner. Therefore at iteration
k
, the bit complexity of the vector is
k(L+\log\kappa)
. Communicating such vectors between the coordinator and the machines would result in a communication complexity of

\Omega(sd\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\log\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}k(L+\log\kappa))=\Omega(sd\cdot(L+\log\kappa)\log^{2}(\varepsilon^{-1})).

In this section, we show that one of the
\log(\varepsilon^{-1})
factors in the above expression can be avoided.
Item

of
is a classic Richardson’s step which gives
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}
— see the example in
Eq.

3.14
, which we display here again. After this computation, we modify the bits of the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
coordinate
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}
to obtain
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}
by setting the bits with small place values to their values in
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}
(cf.
Item

of
). The underlying principle is that since the place value of these bits is small, this modification does not affect the convergence of Richardson. Moreover, since our algorithm is converging (proven in
Lemma

3.10
), the bits with high place values do not change from one iteration to the next. These bits are represented by blue in the following equation. Thus, since the machines store
\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}
, upon receiving the bits of
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}
represented by red, they can construct
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}
. Our proof essentially boils down to showing the number of bits in this red middle part of
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}
is
O(L+\log\kappa)
and that the modification of bits with small place value does not affect the convergence. This then implies both the communication complexity bounds and correctness as stated in
Lemma

3.10
. We note that (
3.14
) does not completely reflect all cases. For example, it is possible that
\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}=1.0000
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}=0.1111
. In this case, it might appear that the bits with high place values are not equal. However, in the difference
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}=0.0001
, the bits with high place value are equal to zero. This is carefully written and analyzed in our algorithms and proofs.

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}&=\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}101.0110101001}}_{\text{is same as $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}$}}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}011011010010101}}_{\text{is different than $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}$}}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0010000010101\cdots}}_{\text{is different than $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}$}}\\
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}_{i}&=\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}101.0110101001}}_{\text{is same as $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}$}}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}011011010010101}}_{\text{is same as $\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}_{i}$}}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1010100001111\cdots}}_{\text{is same as $\mathbf{x}^{(k)}_{i}$}}\end{split}

(3.14)

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}:=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, where
n=\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}
, and vector
\mathbf{b}:=[\mathbf{b}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine stores matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
and vector
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
. The matrix
\mathbf{M}
with
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{M}\preceq\lambda\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, with
\lambda\geq 1
, stored on the coordinator machine; accuracy parameter
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Output.
A vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
on all machines such that
\|\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{b}\|_{2}+\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}
.

Initialize.
Set
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}=\vec{0}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}
, and
T\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer such that
(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda})^{T}\leq\varepsilon
. Moreover each machine
i\in[s]
, computes
(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{b}^{(i)}
and sends it to the coordinator.

For
iterations
k=0,1,\dots,T-1
:

The coordinator computes
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}=\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b})
.

The coordinator sets each coordinate
j
of
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}
equal to the corresponding coordinate
j
of
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}
at all bits except those with a place value less than
\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}}{\lambda\cdot nd^{2}\cdot 2^{2L+1}}
, at which bits the coordinator sets the bit to the corresponding bit of coordinate
j
of
\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
.

The coordinator sends the vector
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the machines.

Each machine
i\in[s]
computes the vector
(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and sends it to the coordinator.

Algorithm 8
:
Protocol for computing the solution of a linear regression problem given a preconditioner.

alg]alg:richardson

In the next result, we assume the matrix is full-rank. However, this is not a limitation of our approach since we can concatenate the matrix with a small-scaled identity matrix — see Appendix A in
[
GPV23
]
.

Lemma 3.10

(Richardson-Type Iteration)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and the linear regression setting of
1.3
with input matrix
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}_{i}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and vector
\mathbf{b}=[\mathbf{b}_{i}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a matrix stored in the coordinator such that
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{M}\preceq\lambda\cdot\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, for constant
\lambda\geq 1
. Then there is an algorithm that outputs a vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\|\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}+\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}

using
\widetilde{O}(sd(L+\log\kappa)\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
bits of communication.
Moreover, the vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
is available on all the machines at the end of the algorithm.

Proof.

We show that
returns the specified solution with the specified communication complexity bounds.
As a first step, we argue that the vectors that are required for computation at the coordinator can indeed be computed from the information that is communicated to the coordinator.

First, note that
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{b}^{(i)}.
The quantity
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
needs to be communicated to the coordinator only once (since it does not change through the algorithm). Since the bit complexities of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
are
L
and each machine sends to the coordinator
(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{b}^{(i)}
, it takes a total of
O(sdL\log n)
to communicate
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
.

We now show by induction that the coordinator can compute
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
. Initially
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}=0
. Therefore
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(0)}
, and the coordinator has this information. Then, assume as the base case of the induction that the coordinator has the value of
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
and the machines are sending the vectors
(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)})
to the coordinator (see
Item

of
). We observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)})=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}).

Therefore, if the coordinator has
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
then
Item

enables it to compute
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
are also stored on the coordinator, it can perform the computation in
Item

.

We now prove that
converges. The optimal vector
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}
satisfies
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
. Subtracting it from the iterate
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}
and applying the update from
Item

of
in each iteration, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b})-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}=(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}).

(3.15)

To obtain a bound on the rate of shrinkage of the distance (in
\mathbf{M}
-norm) of the algorithm’s iterate from the true optimizer, we study the squared
\mathbf{M}
-norm of the right-hand side in
Eq.

3.15
.

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})^{\top}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{M}^{-1})\cdot\mathbf{M}\cdot(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}).

(3.16)

To simplify notion, we define
\mathbf{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}
. Therefore, we can check the computation

(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{M}^{-1})\cdot\mathbf{M}\cdot(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{M}^{1/2}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{H})^{2}\mathbf{M}^{1/2}.

Moreover, by the assumption
0\prec\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{M}\preceq\lambda\cdot\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, the definition of
\mathbf{H}
, and the properties stated in
A.1
, we have

\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbf{I}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}\preceq\mathbf{H}\preceq\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{-1/2}=\mathbf{I}.

Therefore
0\preceq\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{H}\preceq(1-\tfrac{1}{\lambda})\mathbf{I}
; applying to this
A.1
, we have

0\preceq(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{M}^{-1})\cdot\mathbf{M}\cdot(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})\preceq(1-\lambda^{-1})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{M}.

Plugging
Section

3.3
into
Eq.

3.15
and
Eq.

3.16
gives

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}=\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}\leq(1-\lambda^{-1})^{2}\|\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}.

By the update rule in
Item

of
and the expression for
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}=-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b})=-\mathbf{M}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\ast})=-\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}).

(3.19)

By applying the assumption
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{M}
to
Eq.

3.19
and using
A.1
, we get

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}.

(3.20)

We then have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}\leq\lambda\cdot nd\cdot 2^{2L}\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}\|_{2}\leq\widehat{\varepsilon}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}\leq\widehat{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}},

(3.21)

where the first step uses the assumptions that
\mathbf{A}
has a bit complexity of
L
and associated
1.26
and also that
\mathbf{M}\preceq\lambda\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, the second step uses
Item

from
(specifically, the example visually depicted in
Eq.

3.14
), and the third step uses
Eq.

3.20
.
Combining
Section

3.3
and
Eq.

3.21
along with the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}\leq(1-\lambda^{-1}+\widehat{\varepsilon})\|\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}=(1-\tfrac{1}{2\lambda})\|\mathbf{x}^{(k)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{M}}.

(3.22)

Since
\lambda
is a constant, this implies that after
O(\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
iterations, we achieve the required accuracy. Starting from the zero vector and since
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{M}\preceq\lambda\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, we have

\|\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{x}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}}.

We then have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}-(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{x}^{\ast})\|_{2}^{2}+\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}.

Then applying
Section

3.3
to the above inequality, we have the claimed convergence bound:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{(T)}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}+\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}.

We now bound the bit complexity of each iteration of
. Denoting
\sigma_{\textrm{min}}
to be the minimum singular value of
\mathbf{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textrm{min}}}\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textrm{min}}}\left(\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\sigma_{\textrm{min}}}\cdot\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}},

where the first step uses
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{M}}\geq\sigma_{\textrm{min}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}
for any vector
\mathbf{u}
, the second step is by triangle inequality, and the third step uses the rounding we performed in
Item

of
.
Moreover note that if
\|\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{2}\neq 0
, by construction, it is more than
\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}}{\lambda\cdot nd^{2}2^{2L+2}}
. Let
p
be an integer such that
2^{p}\leq\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}}{\lambda\cdot nd^{2}2^{2L+2}}<2^{p+1}
. Then we have
for any
j
corresponding to a nonzero entry of
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
,

\displaystyle 1\leq 2^{-p}|(\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)})_{j}|\leq 2^{-p}\cdot\frac{1+\widehat{\varepsilon}}{\sigma_{\min}}\cdot\|\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}\|_{\mathbf{M}}<\frac{(1+\widehat{\varepsilon})}{\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\sigma_{\min}}\cdot\lambda\cdot nd^{2}2^{2L+3}.

Therefore we only need to communicate
\log(p)+\log(\frac{(1+\widehat{\varepsilon})}{\widehat{\varepsilon}\cdot\sigma_{\min}}\cdot\lambda\cdot nd^{2}2^{2L+3})
bits to each machine in
Item

. Finally, in
Item

, when the machines multiply
\mathbf{x}^{(k+1)}-\mathbf{x}^{(k)}
by
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
and send it back to the coordinator this just adds

O(L\log n)
bits.
∎

3.4
Proof of Main Result on High-Accuracy Linear Regression

In this section, we prove our main result for the communication complexity of linear regression in the point-to-point model of communication.

Proof of
Theorem

1.8
.

We show that
returns the correct output and satisfies the communication complexity bounds. We start with the proof of correctness.

Correctness.

By
Lemma

3.2
,
Item

of
returns a vector of overestimates
\widehat{\tau}\geq\tau
with
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
. Then by
Lemma

3.4
, sampling according to these leverage score overestimates with
\alpha=100
produces the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
in
Item

which is a
\frac{1.1}{0.9}
-spectral approximation of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
. Finally, in
Item

, we use this spectral approximation to return a high-accuracy solution as proved in
Lemma

3.10
.

Communication complexity.

First, observe that
Item

incurs no communication. Next, the communication complexities of
Item

and
Item

directly follow from
Lemma

3.2
and
Lemma

3.10
and are

\widetilde{O}(d^{2}L+sd(L+\log\kappa))
and
\widetilde{O}(sd(L+\log\kappa)\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
, respectively. Finally,
Item

requires the communication of the selected rows and their probabilities, which we now compute. Since
Item

provides vector
\widehat{\tau}
of leverage score overestimates that satisfies
\|\widehat{\tau}\|_{1}\leq 9d
, we are guaranteed by
Lemma

3.4
that, with high probability, there are only
O(d\log d)
non-zero rows in
Item

. These can be communicated with
\widetilde{O}(d^{2}L)
bits since the bit complexity of the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is
L
and each vector has
d
coordinates. As per
Definition

3.3
, the sampling probabilities can be computed at the coordinator by knowing the leverage score overestimates for the selected rows at the coordinator. Therefore since before the last step of
, the leverage score overestimates are powers of two, we can communicate these powers of two (that are in the interval
[\frac{1}{2n^{2}},1]
) with
O(d\log(d)\cdot\log(n))
, bits and then the coordinator can obtain the leverage score overestimates by multiplying these by
1.01
and computing the probabilities accordingly.
∎

4
Linear Programming in the Coordinator Model

In this section, we bound the communication complexity of solving the following linear program in the setup of
1.3
:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}:\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x},

(4.1)

with
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]_{i\in[s]}
,
\mathbf{c}=[\mathbf{c}^{(i)}]_{i\in[s]}
, the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine holding
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
and
\mathbf{c}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
. We assume that the bit complexities of
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}
are bounded by
L
, and the vector
\mathbf{b}
is available to all machines.
The main result of this section is the following.

See
1.10

Our framework for achieving the results in
Theorem

1.10
is an adaptation of the algorithm of
[
BLSS20
]
to the coordinator setting,
which in turn builds upon the techniques of
[
LS14
]
. While we describe our procedure in more detail in
Section

4.1
,
essentially each iteration of our interior-point method involves solving a linear system of the form
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{1/2}\mathbf{g}
, where
\mathbf{v}
is the variable vector and
\mathbf{W}
is a nonnegative diagonal matrix — see
Section

4.1
. This is equivalent to solving a linear regression of the form
\min_{\mathbf{v}}\|\mathbf{W}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{g}\|_{2}^{2}
. To this end, we use a randomized approach based on leverage score sampling.
Since the randomness of previous iterations determines the solutions of next iterations, this might cause adaptive adversary issues — see
[
LS15
]
.
To prevent such an issue, we solve the linear regression instance in each iteration to high-accuracy by
Theorem

1.8
.

4.1
An Overview of Our Algorithm

Set in the framework of primal-dual path-following interior-point method
[
Ren88
,
Ren01
]
, our algorithm maintains a primal feasible point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
satisfying (the primal feasibility condition)
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
and a dual feasible point
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
satisfying (the dual feasibility condition)
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{c}\geq 0
(note that all vector inequalities in this section are coordinate-wise.) The goal is to steadily decrease the primal-dual gap

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{s})^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{x},

where
\mathbf{s}:=\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}
is the
slack
variable, and the other terms cancel out due to the aforementioned feasibility conditions. At each iteration, a primal-dual path-following algorithm trades off decreasing the gap
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{s}
against maintaining feasibility. Collecting all these requirements yields the following optimality conditions we want the algorithm to satisfy in each iteration:

\mathbf{x}\odot\mathbf{s}=\mu\cdot\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s}),\,\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\,\,\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{c},\,\,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s}\geq 0,

where we use
\odot
to denote coordinate-wise product of two vectors,
\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})
is a carefully designed function that tracks the primal-dual gap, and
\mu
is a parameter the algorithm gradually decreases. The (unique) set
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s})
satisfying this system of equations is said to follow the weighted central path. Decreasing
\mu
puts more emphasis on decreasing the primal-dual gap (i.e., making progress on the objective value), and the algorithm alternates between decreasing
\mu
and taking a Newton-like step from the current approximate solution of
Section

4.1
towards that with the updated
\mu
.

The choice of weight function
\tau
plays an immensely critical role in the convergence rate of the overall algorithm. For example, setting
\tau
to be the all-ones vector yields the standard log-barrier-based central path
[
Ren88
,
Meh92
,
Gon92
,
NN94
,
YTM94
]
which takes
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
iterations for convergence
[
Kar84
,
Vai89a
]
. Drawing on geometric connections between Lewis weights and ellipsoidal approximations of polytopes, the breakthrough work of
[
LS14
]
developed an algorithm with
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})})
iterations by choosing
\tau
to be
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of a certain matrix for
p=\Omega(\log n)
. The work of
[
BLSS20
]
simplified this to the leverage scores of a certain matrix, with an added regularizer for simplicity of analysis, and this is the weight function we also use:

\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s}):=\sigma(\mathbf{S}^{-1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{X}^{1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{A})+\frac{d}{n}\cdot\bm{1},

where
\sigma(\mathbf{B})
denotes the vector of leverage scores of matrix
\mathbf{B}
, the uppercase
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{S}
denote, respectively, the diagonal matrices formed using the vectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{s}
, and the parameter
\alpha=O(\tfrac{1}{\log(n/d)})
.

In order to improve the proximity of the next point to the true central path described by
Section

4.1
, the algorithm attempts to ensure, for the updated
\mu
, that
\mathbf{x}\odot\mathbf{s}\approx\mu\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})
defined in
Section

4.1
. This proximity is called
centrality

[
LS14
]
, and to measure and track it, prior works
[
CLS21
,
LSZ19
,
Bra20
,
BLSS20
]
have successfully used the following “soft-max”-like potential

\Phi(\mathbf{v})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp(\lambda(\mathbf{v}_{i}-1))+\exp(-\lambda(\mathbf{v}_{i}-1)),

with
v=\tfrac{\mu\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})}{\mathbf{x}\odot\mathbf{s}}
and
\lambda=O(\varepsilon^{-1})
; this is what we also therefore use. Making fast progress along the central path therefore entails updating
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{s}
so that
Section

4.1
decreases sufficiently fast.

To achieve this goal, one observes that the updated points
\mathbf{x}+\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{s}+\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
must also satisfy
Section

4.1
; considering the fact that
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{s}
also satisfy this equation, one infers that the updates
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
and
\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
must satisfy

\mathbf{X}\delta_{\mathbf{s}}+\mathbf{S}\delta_{\mathbf{s}}=\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}},\,\,\mathbf{A}^{\top}\delta_{\mathbf{x}}=0,\,\,\mathbf{A}\delta_{\mathbf{y}}+\delta_{\mathbf{s}}=0,

where we have introduced the notation
\widetilde{\bm{\mu}}:=\mu\cdot\tau(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s})=\mu\cdot(\sigma(\mathbf{S}^{-1/2-1/p}\mathbf{X}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\frac{d}{n}1)
, and
\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}\approx\nabla\Phi(\tfrac{\widetilde{\bm{\mu}}}{\mathbf{x}\odot\mathbf{s}})
is the update step we choose to decrease the potential, thereby improving the centrality of the next point. Plugging this value of
\delta_{\widetilde{\bm{\mu}}}
back into
Section

4.1
yields the solution

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bm{\delta}_{x}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})\tfrac{\mathbf{I}}{\sqrt{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}}}\cdot\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}\text{ and }\mathbf{\bm{\delta}_{s}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{X}}}\mathbf{P}\tfrac{\mathbf{I}}{\sqrt{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}}}\cdot\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}},

(4.6)

where
\mathbf{P}
is the orthogonal projection matrix given by the following closed-form expression

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tfrac{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{S}}}\mathbf{A}\left(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\tfrac{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{S}}\mathbf{A}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\sqrt{\tfrac{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{S}}}.

(4.7)

Our high-level strategy then is to first compute an initial feasible solution (
Section

4.2
) and then iteratively compute the updates described in
Eq.

4.6
. To perform these updates efficiently, we use inverse maintenance, whose iteration count and communication complexity we discuss in
Section

4.3
. Finally in
Section

4.4
, we put these together to prove
Theorem

1.10
.

4.2
Finding an Initial Feasible Point

We construct our initial iterates following the approach of
[
BLSS20
]
. We first obtain an initial set of primal and dual points
\mathbf{x}\approx\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{s}\approx\mathbf{1}
, which are feasible for the modified linear program in
Definition

4.1
, and as stated in
Lemma

4.2
(proved in
[
BLSS20
]
), these vectors may be easily transformed to be a set of feasible points for the original linear program.

Definition 4.1

(Theorem 12 of
[
BLSS20
]
)
.

For a linear program
\min_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x}\geq 0}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
with outer radius
R
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we define the modified linear program to

\min_{\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}=\overline{\mathbf{b}},\overline{\mathbf{x}}\geq 0}\overline{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}},

where

\overline{\mathbf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot\bm{1}_{n}\\
0&\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\\
\frac{1}{R}\mathbf{b}^{\top}-\bm{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+2)\times(d+1)},\overline{\mathbf{b}}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{R}\mathbf{b}\\
(n+1)\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},\overline{\mathbf{c}}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{c}\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2}.

Lemma 4.2

(Theorem 12 of
[
BLSS20
]
)
.

The following are feasible primal and dual vectors for the modified linear program stated in
Definition

4.1
:

\overline{\mathbf{x}}=\bm{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2},\,\,\overline{\mathbf{y}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}_{d}\\
-1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},\,\,\overline{\mathbf{s}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{1}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\|c\|_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{c}\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2}.

Let
(\widehat{\mathbf{x}},\widehat{\mathbf{y}},\widehat{\mathbf{s}})
be an arbitrary set of primal dual vectors of the modified linear program, and let
\rho:=\frac{1}{\mu}\cdot\|\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}-\overline{\mathbf{b}}\|^{2}_{(\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}}=O(1)
. Then,

\|\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\|_{\infty}=O(n)\cdot(1+\rho/\mu).

Moreover for
\mu<\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8d}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{x}},\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\odot\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\approx_{0.5}\mu\cdot\tau(\widehat{\mathbf{x}},\widehat{\mathbf{s}})
(where
\tau
is defined in
Section

4.1
), if we set
\mathbf{x}=R\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{1:n}
(where
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{1:n}
is the vector of the first
n
coordinates of
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}
), then
\mathbf{x}\geq 0
is an approximate solution to the original linear program in the following sense:

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\min_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x}\geq 0}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+O(nR\cdot\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2})\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}+\varepsilon),\,\,\|\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}\leq O(n^{2})\cdot(\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot R+\|\mathbf{b}\|_{2})\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}+\varepsilon).

It is critical to note that although
(\overline{\mathbf{x}},\overline{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{s}})
in
Lemma

4.2
is feasible, it is not necessarily on the central path (or even near it) since
\overline{\mathbf{x}}\odot\overline{\mathbf{s}}
is not necessarily near
\mu\cdot\tau(\overline{\mathbf{x}},\overline{\mathbf{s}})
. To enforce this centrality with
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{1}_{n+2}
and
\mu=1
, we must have
\mathbf{s}\approx\tau(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{s}),
which corresponds roughly to the definition of regularized
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
for
p=\tfrac{1}{1+\alpha}.

The fact that
\alpha>0
corresponds to
p<1
, which is a range of
p
for which
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights may be efficiently computed via a simple fixed-point iteration
[
CP15
]
. We note that for the interval complementary to that in
[
CP15
]
,
[
FLPS22
]
provides an efficient (but different) algorithm for computing
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights. Since in our setting,
p<1
, we use the algorithm by
[
CP15
]
, whose main guarantee we restate below.

Lemma 4.3

(Lemma 3.2 of
[
CP15
]
)
.

Given
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
0<p<4
, and vectors
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{v}\approx_{\zeta}\mathbf{w}
, define

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}=(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{V}^{1-2/p}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i})^{p/2}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}=(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{1-2/p}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i})^{p/2}.

Then
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
satisfy the approximation

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\approx_{\zeta\cdot{|p/2-1|}}\widehat{\mathbf{w}},

where, for some given
\alpha>0
, we use the notation
x\approx_{\alpha}y
to denote
y\cdot e^{-\alpha}\leq x\leq y\cdot e^{\alpha}
.

The proof of
[
CP15
]
for convergence to the Lewis weights, for
p\in(0,4)
, through the iterative process described above is based on the fact that
|p/2-1|<1
and hence results in a contraction. We also use this observation to bound the communication complexity of computing the (approximate) regularized Lewis weights in
Lemma

4.7
, adapted from Corollary 3.4 of
[
CP15
]
and Theorem 13 of
[
BLSS20
]
. Before proving this lemma, we first establish some notation for easier readability, followed by two technical results, which we invoke in order to prove
Lemma

4.7
.

Definition 4.4
.

Given a vector
\mathbf{x}
and a diagonal matrix
\mathbf{X}
formed using
\mathbf{x}
, we define the functions

\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{1-2/p}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}\text{ and }\mathcal{T}_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=(\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+\eta\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1})^{p/2},

and the following approximation to
\mathcal{T}
, with both additive and multiplicative error,

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+\eta\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1})^{p/2},\text{ where }\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+e^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}\leq\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\leq e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}.

We remark that our
\mathcal{T}
is identical to
T
in Theorem
13
of
[
BLSS20
]
.

Claim 4.5

(
[
BLSS20
]
)
.

For some
\alpha>0
, let
x
and
y
be positive numbers satisfying
x\approx_{\alpha}y
. Then, for
\lambda,u,k>0
, we have
\lambda x\approx_{\alpha}\lambda y
,
(x+u)\approx_{\alpha}(y+u)
, and
x^{k}\approx_{\alpha k}y^{k}
.

Next, we extend
4.5
to the following lemma, which essentially says that if two vectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
are
\alpha
-approximations of each other (in the sense stated in
Lemma

4.3
), then
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{x})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{y})
are also close. We use this lemma to inductively prove
Lemma

4.7
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Consider the notation from
Definition

4.4
. Suppose for some
\alpha>0
, the vectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
satisfy
\mathbf{x}\approx_{\alpha}\mathbf{y}
. Then we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\approx_{p/2(\varepsilon_{1}+\alpha|1-2/p|)}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}(\mathbf{y})
, where
\varepsilon_{1}=\begin{cases}p\cdot\varepsilon/4&\text{ if }p\leq 2\\
\frac{\varepsilon\cdot(4-p)}{4}&\text{ if }p>2\end{cases}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{x}\approx_{\alpha}\mathbf{y}
, by
4.5
, we have

\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{y})\approx_{\alpha|1-2/p|}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\text{ and }\mathbf{y}_{i}^{2/p-1}\approx_{\alpha|1-2/p|}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1}.

Let
\gamma=\varepsilon_{1}+\alpha|1-2/p|
for some
\varepsilon_{1}>0.

Then the above approximations may equivalently be expressed as

\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\approx_{\gamma-\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{y})\text{ and }\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1}\approx_{\gamma-\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{2/p-1}.

This implies, for the above choice of
\gamma,
the following inequalities
hold:

e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{y})+\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}+\eta\mathbf{y}_{i}^{2/p-1}\leq e^{\gamma}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+e^{\gamma-\varepsilon_{1}}\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}+\eta e^{\gamma}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1},

e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}+\eta\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2/p-1}\leq e^{\gamma}\mathbf{q}_{i}(\mathbf{y})+e^{\gamma-\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}+\eta e^{\gamma}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{2/p-1}.

Chaining these inequalities with the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
in
Definition

4.4

finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Given
1>\eta>0
,
0<\varepsilon<0.5
,
p\in(0,4)
and the setting of
1.3
with input matrix
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, there is a randomized algorithm that, with high probability, outputs a vector
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
such that

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\approx_{\varepsilon}\sigma(\widehat{\mathbf{W}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\eta\cdot\bm{1}.

The number of bits of communication this algorithm uses is

\widetilde{O}\left((d^{2}L+sd(L+\log\kappa+p^{-1}\log(\eta^{-1})))\cdot\frac{\log(\eta^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}p^{-1})}{1-|p/2-1|}\right).

Each machine has access to
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
, which is the part of
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
corresponding to the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

For conciseness inside this proof, we use the notation from
Definition

4.4
. The algorithm that achieves this lemma’s stated guarantee constructs iterates
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}
as follows:

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(0)}=\eta\cdot\bm{1}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t-1)}).

Similarly, the algorithm constructs iterates
\mathbf{w}^{(t)}
as follows:

\mathbf{w}^{(0)}=\eta\cdot\bm{1}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\mathbf{w}^{(t)}_{i}=\mathcal{T}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t-1)}),

noting that the difference from
Section

4.2
is in the use of
\mathcal{T}
instead of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
.
Starting with the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(0)}
and update rule in
Section

4.2
, we obtain the following lower bound on
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(0)})+\eta^{2/p})^{p/2}\geq(\eta^{2/p-1}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}+\eta^{2/p})^{p/2}\geq\eta=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(0)}.

(4.12)

To obtain an upper bound on
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(1)}
, we start with the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(1)}
, followed by the upper bound from
Definition

4.4
with
\varepsilon_{1}<0.04
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(0)})+\eta^{2/p})^{p/2}\leq(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\eta^{2/p-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}+\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}+\eta^{2/p})^{p/2}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\cdot(1+1.1\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}+\frac{1}{n^{2}})^{p/2}\leq\eta\cdot\exp((p/2)\cdot(1.1\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}))

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(0)}_{i}\cdot\exp((p/2)\cdot(1.1\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}+\frac{1}{n^{2}})),

(4.13)

where the second step uses
Definition

4.4
, the third step uses the upper bound
\mathbf{a}_{i}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}\leq 1
, and the fourth step uses the inequality
1+x\leq e^{x}
.
Therefore, from
Eq.

4.12
and
Eq.

4.13
,
we may conclude that
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(1)}\approx_{\beta}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{(0)}
for
\beta=(p/2)\cdot(1.1\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}+\frac{1}{n^{2}})
.
We may now invoke
Lemma

4.6
on
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(1)}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(0)}
inductively to claim that

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}\approx_{\beta\cdot|p/2-1|^{t}+\theta_{t}}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)},

where
\theta_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}\cdot(p/2)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{t}|p/2-1|^{j-1}
. If
p\leq 2
, then this formula implies that
\theta_{t}\leq\varepsilon_{1}
; if
p>2
, then we have
\theta_{t}\leq\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\frac{p}{4-p}
.
For
p\leq 2
, we pick
\varepsilon_{1}=p\cdot\varepsilon/4
, and if
p>2
, we pick
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{\varepsilon\cdot(4-p)}{4}
.
Since
0<p<4
,
0<\eta<1
, and
|\frac{p}{2}-1|\leq\exp(|\frac{p}{2}-1|-1)
,
after
O(\frac{\log(\eta^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}p^{-1})}{1-|p/2-1|})
iterations, we have
k
such that

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}\approx_{p\cdot\varepsilon/2}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k-1)}.

Next, based on the update rule for
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{(t)}
in
Section

4.2
, we infer

\mathbf{w}^{(k+1)}_{i}=(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}_{i})^{1-p/2}\cdot(\sigma_{i}((\widehat{\mathbf{W}}^{(k)})^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\eta)^{p/2}
.
By
Lemma

4.6
and arguments similar to above,

\mathbf{w}^{(k+1)}\approx_{p\cdot\varepsilon/2}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}.

Therefore

(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}_{i})^{1-p/2}\cdot(\sigma_{i}((\widehat{\mathbf{W}}^{(k)})^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\eta)^{p/2}\approx_{p\cdot\varepsilon/2}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}_{i}.

Thus

(\sigma_{i}((\widehat{\mathbf{W}}^{(k)})^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\eta)^{p/2}\approx_{p\cdot\varepsilon/2}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}_{i})^{p/2}.

Raising above to the power of
2/p
, we have

\sigma_{i}((\widehat{\mathbf{W}}^{(k)})^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})+\eta\approx_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}^{(k)}_{i}.

The communication complexity follows from the number of iterations and
Lemma

3.2
. Note that the guarantee of
Definition

4.4
follows from the proof of
Lemma

3.2
and the term
sd\cdot\frac{\log(\eta^{-1})}{p})
appears because we need a
\frac{\eta^{2/p}}{n^{2}}
additive error for the leverage score computation.
∎

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and vectors
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with parameters in
Theorem

1.10
; Error parameters
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Output.
A vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
satisfying
Theorem

1.10
.

Let
\alpha:=\tfrac{1}{4\log(4n/d)}
,
\lambda:=\tfrac{\alpha}{32000}\cdot\log(2^{16}n\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}})
, and
\gamma:=\min\{\tfrac{\alpha}{64000},\tfrac{\alpha}{50\lambda}\}
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}=0.1

Let
\overline{\mathbf{A}},\overline{\mathbf{b}},\overline{\mathbf{c}},\overline{\mathbf{x}},\overline{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{s}}
be as defined in
Definition

4.1
for the modified linear program.

Set
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
to a vector with
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\approx_{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\sigma(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{A})+\frac{d}{n}\cdot\bm{1}
(see
Lemma

4.7
).

Set
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}=\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\tau}}=\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{0}
,
\widehat{\mu}=1

Let
(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{(\text{end})},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}^{(\text{end})},\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{(\text{end})},\widehat{\bm{\tau}}^{(\text{end})},\widehat{\mu}^{(\text{end})})=\textsc{IPM}(\overline{\mathbf{A}},\overline{\mathbf{b}},\widehat{\mathbf{c}},\overline{\mathbf{x}},\widehat{\mathbf{y}},\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{\bm{\tau}}^{(\text{end})},\widehat{\mu},\theta(n^{2}\sqrt{d}/(\gamma\alpha^{2})))

Set
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}=\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{(\text{end})}
,
\mathbf{y}^{(0)}=\overline{\mathbf{y}}^{(\text{end})}
and
\mathbf{s}^{(0)}=\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{(\text{end})}+\overline{\mathbf{c}}-\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
,
\bm{\tau}^{(0)}=\widehat{\bm{\tau}}^{(\text{end})}
,
\mu^{(0)}=\widehat{\mu}^{(\text{end})}

Let
(\mathbf{x}^{(\text{end})},\mathbf{y}^{(\text{end})},\mathbf{s}^{(\text{end})},\bm{\tau}^{(\text{end})},\mu^{(\text{end})})=\textsc{IPM}(\overline{\mathbf{A}},\overline{\mathbf{b}},\overline{\mathbf{c}},\mathbf{x}^{(0)},\mathbf{y}^{(0)},\mathbf{s}^{(0)},\bm{\tau}^{(0)},\mu^{(0)},\varepsilon^{2}/(512\cdot n^{4}d))

return

R\cdot\mathbf{x}^{(\text{end})}_{1:n}

Algorithm 9
:
LP Solver

alg]alg:ipm-init

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and vectors
\mathbf{d}^{(0)},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{d}^{(r)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and their corresponding diagonal matrices
\mathbf{D}^{(0)},\mathbf{D}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}^{(r)}
. For
i\geq 1
, each
\mathbf{d}^{(i)}
is received after we returned the output for
\mathbf{d}^{(i-1)}
.

Output.
For
i\geq 0
, the output is a spectral approximation to
(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(i)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}
.

Let
\gamma=1000C\cdot\log d
(where
C
is some absolute constant)

Compute (with high probability using
)
\tau^{(\text{apr})}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
0.99\cdot\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}\leq\tau_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{d}^{(0)})_{i}\leq 1.01\cdot\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}

Set
\mathbf{d}^{(\text{old})}=\mathbf{d}^{(0)}
and
\tau^{(\text{old})}=\tau^{(\text{apr})}

For each
i\in[n]
, let

\mathbf{h}_{i}^{(0)}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{d}_{i}^{(0)}/\min\{1,\gamma\cdot\tau_{i}^{\text{apr}}\}&,\text{ with probability }\min\{1,\gamma\cdot\tau_{i}^{\text{apr}}\}\\
0&,\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Set
\mathbf{K}^{(0)}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{(0)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}

For
j=1,\ldots,r

(a)

Use Richardson’s iteration and
\mathbf{K}^{(j-1)}
to compute JL sketchings of the form
(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(j)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{D}^{(j)})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}
(Each machine computes its own part of
\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{D}^{(j)})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}
and sends it to the coordinator and then there is a back and forth to solve the linear system using Richardson). Then compute
(\mathbf{D}^{(j)})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(j)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{D}^{(j)})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}
and use it to compute
\tau^{(\text{apr})}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
0.99\cdot\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}\leq\tau_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{d}^{(j)})_{i}\leq 1.01\cdot\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}

(b)

For each
i\in[n]
, if
|\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}-\tau^{(\text{old})}_{i}|/\tau^{(\text{old})}_{i}>0.1
or
|\mathbf{d}^{(j)}_{i}-\mathbf{d}^{(\text{old})}_{i}|/\mathbf{d}^{(\text{old})}_{i}>0.1
, then

i.

Set
\mathbf{d}^{(\text{old})}_{i}=\mathbf{d}^{(j)}_{i}
,
\tau^{(\text{old})}_{i}=\tau^{(\text{apr})}_{i}
, and

\mathbf{h}_{i}^{(j)}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{d}_{i}^{(j)}/\min\{1,\gamma\cdot\tau_{i}^{\text{apr}}\}&,\text{ with probability }\min\{1,\gamma\cdot\tau_{i}^{\text{apr}}\}\\
0&,\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(c)

else

i.

\mathbf{h}_{i}^{(j)}=\mathbf{h}_{i}^{(j-1)}

(d)

Set
\mathbf{K}^{(j)}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{H}^{(j)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}

Algorithm 10
:
Inverse Maintenance for LP

alg]alg:inv-maintenance

As we discussed, after computing
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\approx_{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\sigma(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}^{-1/2-\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{A}})+\frac{d}{n}\bm{1}
, we have a point near the central path for the modified linear program for the modified objective vector
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}=\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
. Therefore, we can run the IPM so that
\mu
is small enough. This part is illustrated as Step 7 of
. By notation of
, and the guarantees of our IPM, we have

\widehat{\mathbf{x}}^{(\text{end})}\odot\widehat{\mathbf{s}}{(\text{end})}=\widehat{\mu}^{(\text{end})}\cdot\widehat{\bm{\tau}}^{(\text{end})}.

Since we have
\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{y}}^{(\text{end})}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{(\text{end})}=\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
(this equality is exact since we do not explicitly update
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}
) and
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}=\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
,
\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{y}}^{(\text{end})}+\mathbf{s}^{(0)}-\overline{\mathbf{c}}+\widehat{\mathbf{c}}=\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
. Thus
\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{y}}^{(\text{end})}+\mathbf{s}^{(0)}=\overline{\mathbf{c}}
. Moreover, by construction
\mathbf{s}^{(0)}=\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{(\text{end})}+\overline{\mathbf{c}}-\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
. Therefore
\frac{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}-\mathbf{s}^{(\text{end})}}{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}=\frac{\overline{\mathbf{c}}-\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}
. Since by the guarantees of the IPM
\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\odot\mathbf{x}^{(0)}\approx_{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\mu^{(0)}\cdot\bm{\tau}^{(0)}
, and
\widehat{\varepsilon}\leq 0.5
, for each
i\in[n]
, we have

\mathbf{s}^{(0)}_{i}\geq\frac{\mu^{(0)}}{2\mathbf{x}^{(0)}_{i}}\cdot\frac{d}{n}\geq\frac{\mu}{\Omega(n^{2})},

where the second inequality follows from
Lemma

4.2
by
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}=O(n)
. Moreover, note that
\|\overline{\mathbf{c}}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\|_{\infty}=\|\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\|_{\infty}\leq 3
. Therefore by picking the appropriate constant in Step 7 of
, for any constant
\beta
, we have

\|\frac{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}-\mathbf{s}^{(\text{end})}}{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\|_{\infty}=\|\frac{\overline{\mathbf{c}}-\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{16n^{2}}{\mu^{(0)}}\leq\frac{\gamma\cdot\alpha^{2}}{\beta\cdot\sqrt{d}}.

Therefore, we can pick
\beta
small enough so that
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}\odot\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\approx_{2\widehat{\varepsilon}}\mu^{(0)}\bm{\tau}^{(0)}
, where

\bm{\tau}^{(0)}\approx_{\gamma/4}\sigma((\mathbf{S}^{(0)})^{-1/2-\alpha}(\mathbf{X}^{(0)})^{1/2-\alpha}).

Thus after Step 9 of
, we have
\mathbf{x}^{(\text{end})}\odot\mathbf{s}^{(\text{end})}\approx_{\widehat{\varepsilon}}\mu^{(\text{end})}\cdot\bm{\tau}^{(\text{end})}
.

We finally need to bound the condition number of the matrix
\overline{\mathbf{A}}
. To do this, we use the following result.

Lemma 4.8

(Lemma 5.15 of
[
GPV23
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
n>d
, be a matrix with full column rank. Moreover let
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Suppose
\kappa>1
, and

\|\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\|(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\|\mathbf{g}\|_{2},1/\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\mathbf{g}\|_{2}\leq\kappa.

Then

\|\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\|(\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq 8\kappa^{7}
,
where

\overline{\mathbf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}|\mathbf{g}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that setting

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\\
0\\
\frac{1}{R}\mathbf{b}^{\top}-\bm{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\end{bmatrix},

since
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}+(\frac{1}{R}\mathbf{b}^{\top}-\bm{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{\top}(\frac{1}{R}\mathbf{b}^{\top}-\bm{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A})
, the condition number of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
is smaller than the condition number of
\mathbf{A}
. Now by
Lemma

4.8
, we have

\|\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\|(\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq 8(\kappa(\mathbf{A})+2\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}})^{7}=O((\kappa(\mathbf{A})+2^{L}\sqrt{nd})^{7}).

4.3
Inverse Maintenance for IPM

In this section, we present the subprocedure
IPM
of
. This is presented in
. Essentially, in each iteration, in
Item

b
, each machine computes its own part of the gradient of the potential at a specific point and sends it to the coordinator. The coordinator then in
Item

c
sums the vectors obtained from the machines and pre-multiplies this sum by a matrix
\mathbf{H}^{-1}
that spectrally approximates
(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}
and sends the result to all the machines. In
Item

d
, each machine uses this vector to update its own part of the primal and slack vectors. As discussed in
Lemma

4.2
, we do not need exact feasibility, and therefore, the vectors sent to the coordinator or to the machine can be rounded down. Essentially, for each entry, we only need to send
L+\log(\kappa R/r)
of its bits to guarantee convergence (see
[
BLSS20
, Theorem 32]
).

The only remaining part for bounding the communication complexity of the algorithm is to show that
Item

f
of
does not resample too many rows over the course of the algorithm.

To improve the running time of the IPMs based on the Lee-Sidford barrier,
[
LS15
]
introduced the following
\sigma
-stability property. As discussed in Lemma 21 of
[
BLSS20
]
, the primal and slack vectors in
satisfy this property. This then allows us to bound the number of resampled rows in
Item

f
of
.

Definition 4.9

(
\sigma
-stability assumption)
.

We say that the inverse maintenance problem satisfies the
\sigma
-stability assumption if for each
k\in[r]
(where
r
is the number of rounds/iterations of the algorithm), we have

\|\log(\mathbf{d}^{(k)})-\log(\mathbf{d}^{(k-1)})\|_{\sigma(\mathbf{D}^{(k)}\mathbf{A})}\leq 0.1,

\|\log(\mathbf{d}^{(k)})-\log(\mathbf{d}^{(k-1)})\|_{\infty}\leq 0.1,

and

\beta^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(0)}\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(k)}\mathbf{A}\preceq\beta\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{(0)}\mathbf{A},

for
\beta=\operatorname{poly}(n)
.

Input.
A matrix
\mathbf{A}:=[\mathbf{A}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and vector
\mathbf{c}:=[\mathbf{c}^{(i)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine stores matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
; initial feasible primal, dual, slack and weight vectors
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{y}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{s}^{(0)}
,
\bm{\tau}^{(0)}
, respectively. Initial and final centrality parameter
\mu^{(0)}
and
\mu^{(\text{end})}
.

Output.
Vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

procedure
\textsc{IPM}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}^{(0)},\mathbf{y}^{(0)},\mathbf{s}^{(0)},\bm{\tau}^{(0)},\mu^{(0)},\mu^{(\text{end})})
:

Coordinator sets
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{4\log(4n/d)}
,
\lambda=\tfrac{2}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{2^{16}n\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}})
,
\gamma=\min(\frac{\varepsilon}{4},\frac{\alpha}{50\lambda})
,
\mu=\mu^{(0)}
.

Each machine sets its components of the vectors as follows:
\overline{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}^{(0)}
,
\overline{\mathbf{s}}=\mathbf{s}^{(0)}
,
\overline{\bm{\tau}}=\bm{\tau}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{x}^{(\text{tmp})}=\mathbf{x}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{s}^{(\text{tmp})}=\mathbf{s}^{(0)}
,
\bm{\tau}^{(\text{tmp})}=\bm{\tau}^{(0)}
,
\overline{\mathbf{w}}=\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{s}}
.

The coordinator computes
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\mathbf{H}\approx_{\tfrac{\beta\widehat{\varepsilon}}{d^{1/4}\log^{3}n}}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\tfrac{\overline{\mathbf{X}}}{\overline{\mathbf{S}}}\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{H}^{-1}
.

while
true
do

(a)

Let
\Phi(\mathbf{v}):=\exp(\lambda(\mathbf{v}-1))+\exp(-\lambda(\mathbf{v}-1))
for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(b)

Each machine
i
computes
{(\mathbf{S}^{(i)})}^{-1}\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
and
{(\mathbf{A}^{(i)})}^{\top}{(\mathbf{S}^{(i)})}^{-1}\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}\approx\nabla\Phi(\overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1}\widetilde{\bm{\mu}})
, and sends it to the coordinator.

(c)

The coordinator computes
\mathbf{H}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{A}^{(i)}}^{\top}{(\mathbf{S}^{(i)})}^{-1}\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and sends it to all of the machines.

(d)

Each machine
i
computes
\frac{\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{(i)}}{\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{(i)}}\mathbf{A}^{(i)}\mathbf{H}^{-1}{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}{\overline{\mathbf{S}}}^{-1}\delta_{\widetilde{\mu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
and uses it to compute
\mathbf{\bm{\delta}_{x}}^{(i)}
and
\mathbf{\bm{\delta}_{s}}^{(i)}
to update, respectively,
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}
and
\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{(i)}
.

(e)

Each machine sets its portion of the vectors as the following:
\mathbf{x}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}=\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i}
if
\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\approx_{\gamma/8}\mathbf{x}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}
;
\mathbf{s}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}=\overline{\mathbf{s}}_{i}
if
\overline{\mathbf{s}}_{i}\approx_{\gamma/8}\mathbf{s}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}
;
\bm{\tau}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}=\overline{\bm{\tau}}_{i}
if
\overline{\bm{\tau}}_{i}\approx_{\gamma/8}\bm{\tau}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}
. Set
\overline{\mathbf{w}}=\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{s}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{v}}=\mu\overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1}\overline{\bm{\tau}}
.

(f)

For any
i
where either of
\mathbf{x}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i},\mathbf{s}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}
or
\bm{\tau}^{(\text{tmp})}_{i}
has changed, we resample the
i
’th row according to its leverage scores and send it to the coordinator with the corresponding probability. The coordinator updates
\mathbf{H}^{-1}
accordingly and sends a sketch of it to the machines.

(g)

The machines use the sketch to update their leverage scores.

(h)

If
\mu>\mu^{(\text{end})}
, set
\mu=\max\{\mu^{(\text{end})},(1-\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}})\mu\}
. Otherwise, set
\mu=\max\{\mu^{(\text{end})},(1+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}})\mu\}
.

(i)

If
\mu=\mu^{(\text{end})}
and
\Phi(\overline{\mathbf{v}})\leq\frac{2^{16}n\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^{2}}
,
break
.

Return
(\overline{\mathbf{x}},\overline{\mathbf{y}},\overline{\mathbf{s}},\overline{\bm{\tau}},\mu)
