5
Finite-Sum Minimization in the Blackboard Model

In this section, we study finite-sum minimization (in the distributed setting), i.e.,

\text{minimize}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}),

where each
f_{i}
is convex, Lipschitz, and supported on only
d_{i}
coordinates of
\mathbf{x}
. While finite-sum minimization itself is a general problem class encompassing, for example, empirical risk minimization, with each
f_{i}
measuring the loss incurred by the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
data point from the training set, the additional structural assumption (of dependence on
d_{i}
coordinates) is also seen in prominent problem classes like decomposable submodular function minimization
[
AKMSV21
]
. There exist numerous fast variants of stochastic gradient descent
[
RM51
,
BC03
,
Zha04
,
Bot12
]
for
Section

5
such as
[
RSB12
,
SZ13
,
JZ13
,
MZJ13
,
DBL14
,
Mai15
,
AY16
,
HL16
,
SLB17
]
but most of these algorithms depend on the problem’s condition number, which could be quite large (and hence undesirable) for non-smooth
f_{i}
. Conversely, both the cutting-plane methods
[
LSW15
]
and robust interior-point methods
[
LV21
]
exchange their reliance on condition number for worse dependencies on the problem dimension.

In this section, our goal is to solve this problem with efficient communication complexity. We now formally state our problem setup and all the main results of this section.

See
1.13

In order to obtain our communication bound, we first derive the following fine-grained cost (in terms of certain weights), which also yields improved rates for submodular function minimization.

See
1.14

See
1.15

5.1
An Overview of Our Algorithm

The goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

1.13
. We obtain this result via

obtained by generalizing a technique introduced in
[
DJLPY22
]
and then setting it in the distributed framework.

Every machine holds a copy of all the data (i.e., the current variable
\mathbf{x}

, and each machine
i
holds the corresponding function’s subgradient oracle
\mathcal{O}_{i}
.
Per the technique of
[
DJLPY22
]
, we first use the standard epigraph trick to reduce
Section

5
to the following formulation,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{minimize}&\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x},\\
\mbox{subject to}&\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}+1}\;\forall i\in[s]\\
&\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}.\end{array}

where
\mathbf{x}=[\mathbf{x}_{i}]
concatenates the
s
vectors
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
, and all the sets
\mathcal{K}_{i}
are disjoint and convex.

Section

5.1
transfers the overlap in support between the original
f_{i}
’s into
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
. This reduction requires only the knowledge of support of each
f_{i}
, so this reduction can be done using
O(\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}L)
bits of communications. After the reduction, all the machines hold all the data — vectors
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}
, matrix
\mathbf{A}
— and the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine holds the separation oracle
\mathcal{O}_{i}
for the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
set
\mathcal{K}_{i}
. This oracle is the only means to access the sets
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and is obtained via a reduction from the corresponding subgradient oracle for
f_{i}
, see
[
LSV18
]
.
Specifically, at any queried point
\mathbf{z}_{i}
, the oracle either asserts
\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
, or returns a separating
hyperplane that separates
\mathbf{z}_{i}
from
\mathcal{K}_{i}
. Formally, we prove the theorem below.

Theorem 5.1

(Main theorem for
Section

5.1
)
.

Consider the convex program
described in
Section

5.1
,
with every machine holding all the data and the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine having a separation oracle access to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Denote
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\times\mathcal{K}_{2}\times\dotsc\times\mathcal{K}_{s}
. Assuming we have

outer radius
R
: For any
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}\leq R
, and

inner radius
r
: There exists a
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z},r)\subset\mathcal{K}
,

then, for any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
, we can find a point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
and

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}+1}\forall i\in[s],\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}\cdot R,

in
\operatorname{poly}(sd\log(R/(r\varepsilon))
time in
O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}^{2}\log(dR/r\varepsilon)\cdot L\right)
bits of communication where
L=O(\log(dR/r)
.

We explain our main algorithm (
) to obtain our guarantee of
Theorem

5.1
for solving
Section

5.1
.
Our algorithm’s inputs are the functions
f_{i}
(and their corresponding first-order oracles).
All parameters of this algorithm are set in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.
Before explaining
, we briefly review the algorithm of
[
DJLPY22
]
and then describe the algorithm obtained by directly adopting this in the blackboard model.

Overview of
[
DJLPY22
]
.

This algorithm updates the variable
\mathbf{x}
via updates inspired by interior-point methods, following a central path parametrized by a parameter
t
, with a barrier function over the set
\mathcal{K}
.
Therefore, the ideal iterates would follow the points

\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\left\{t\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\phi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\right\},

with
t
being updated, per the classical theory of interior point methods, based on the complexity parameter of the self-concordance barrier
\sum_{i=1}^{s}\phi_{i}
defined over
\mathcal{K}
. However, the algorithm does
not
have an explicit closed-form expression for
\mathcal{K}
. Therefore, as a proxy to
\mathcal{K}
, it maintains inner and outer set approximations
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
respectively, satisfying
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
for each
i\in[s]
, and performs IPM-style updates with a barrier on
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
. Thus, this algorithm essentially alternates between making progress on
\mathbf{x}
or
t
(per the IPM framework) and updating the set approximation for
\mathcal{K}
(using ideas from classical cutting-plane methods).

Blackboard adaptation of
[
DJLPY22
]
.

In the blackboard model adaptation of the above algorithm, each server would run a copy of the above algorithm. The only step where communication happens is in
Item

a
: Before updating
\mathbf{x}
to the currently set target point
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
, each server checks for feasibility of
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
; if there is potential infeasibility of the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
block
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
(
Item

), then server holding the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{i}
sends the output of querying
\mathcal{O}_{i}
on
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
to the blackboard, while all other servers read this (for free, as allowed by the model).

The above framework, developed in
[
DJLPY22
]
, would yield a communication complexity of
\widetilde{O}(d_{\max}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}L)
where
d_{\max}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}
, which comes from scaling the oracle query complexity of
[
DJLPY22
]
by the worst-case cost of communication (i.e., sending the hyperplane with the maximum
d_{i}
). This
d_{\max}
factor arises under the assumption that each subgradient oracle has the same cost. However, this assumption
does not align with our communication complexity setting, where we charge for each subgradient oracle call by the length of the vector it outputs.

Our modification.

To capture the above nuance, we consider a
weighted
version of oracle complexity, which we denote by
oracle cost
. Given some arbitrary but fixed weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}_{\geq 1}
, the oracle
cost
is
\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\cdot n_{i}
where
n_{i}
is the number of times the
i
-th subgradient oracle queried.

Thus, one of our technical contributions is a more fine-grained analysis of this technique by using a
weighted
potential. (In terms of the algorithm, the only change that happens is the rate of update to
t
, which we do at
O\left(\tfrac{\eta}{\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}}\right)
(as opposed to the previous rate of
O\left(\tfrac{\eta}{\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}}\right)
).) As a result of this change in potential function, we can now conclude that the total
cost
of communication is
\sum_{i}w_{i}d_{i}L
, where we can choose what
w_{i}
are. This is in contrast with the previous result, which was about the total
oracle
complexity. As a result of this change, we now choose
w_{i}=d_{i}
, which gives us the improved communication cost of
\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}^{2}L
. This is an improvement over the previous rate when the largest
d_{i}
is much larger than the rest. In the following subsection, we go into more detail into the individual steps of
, which would help in understanding the analysis.

5.1.1
Details of Our Algorithm

Given the current outer approximating set
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
, the current central path parameter
t
, and some self-concordant barrier
\varphi_{\mathrm{out,i}}
defined on each set
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
, we define the total barrier and the analytic center of
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}\cap\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\}
with respect to this barrier as

\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\varphi_{\mathrm{out,i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i}),\text{ and }\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\left\{t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\varphi_{\mathrm{out,i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right\}.

(5.3)

As mentioned earlier, not knowing
\mathcal{K}
explicitly forces us to choose, as the constraint set, between its proxies
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
; we choose
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
to ensure that we do not miss a potential solution.

Having computed the current target
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
, we move the current candidate
\mathbf{x}
towards it via a Newton step, provided certain conditions of feasibility and minimum progress are satisfied.
If either condition is violated, we first update either
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
, or the parameter
t
, then recompute
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
and repeat the checks until they are satisfied. In our Newton step update (
Item

), we normalize by by the radius of the appropriate Dikin ellipsoid,
which ensures the feasibility of the updated
\mathbf{x}
since, by self-concordance, the unit radius Dikin ball lies
inside the domain of the self-concordance barrier.

The conditions we check before moving our candidate
\mathbf{x}
towards
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
are that
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
(
Item

) and the central path parameter
t
is large enough (
Item

). Recall that
t
determines the suboptimality gap at the current candidate
\mathbf{x}
: so, if
\mathbf{c}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star})\leq O(\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}})
, then we need to first update our next goal along the central path by updating
t
.
If we have already reached an approximate optimum, which we verify by checking whether
t\geq O(1/\varepsilon)
in
Item

a
, then the algorithm terminates by returning

\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ret}}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\left\{t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\right\}.

The point
\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{ret}}
is feasible because it is in
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
by definition, and
O(1/t_{\textrm{end}})=O(\varepsilon)
ensures that it is an approximate optimum for the original problem.
Otherwise, following classical interior-point method theory, we increase
t
by a scaling factor of
1+O(1/\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i})
in
Item

b
to set the next “target suboptimality”.
We then recompute
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
by
Eq.

5.3
.
Since
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}>{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}}+O(1/t)
is not guaranteed with the new
t
and
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
, the algorithm jumps back to the start of the main loop.

To check if
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
, we check if
\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\rangle+\eta\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}\leq O(\nu_{i})
for all
i\in[s]
and for some constant
\eta>0
. Any point in the domain of a self-concordant barrier satisfies the inequalities in
Theorem

A.12
and
Theorem

A.13
,
hence violating this condition
implies that
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
is far from
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
, and as a result,
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
is potentially not (yet) a good candidate to move
\mathbf{x}
towards.

Therefore, to rectify the situation of
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}\notin\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
, we must update either
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
or
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
and compute a new
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
.
To decide which option to take,
we query
\mathcal{O}_{i}
at
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
:
if the oracle indicates that
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
then we incorporate
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
into
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
by redefining
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}=\textrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i},\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\right\}
to be the convex hull of the current
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
(
Item

(a)i
).
If, on the other hand,
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\notin\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we choose to update the outer set
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
.
Then we redefine
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}\cap\mathcal{H}_{i}
(
Item

(a)ii
).
In either case, the only communication that takes place is when the server that queries the oracle sends the output of the oracle to the blackboard for all other servers to read (and update their data).
After processing this update of the sets, the algorithm recomputes
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
and returns to the main loop
since updating the sets does not necessarily imply that the new
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
satisfies
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
.
Updating a set only after checking the very specific condition
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\notin\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
dramatically reduces the number of calls to the separation oracle (since this is the only part of the algorithm it is invoked) as compared to arbitrarily checking all sets.
Over the course of the algorithm, we gradually expand
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and shrink
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
, until they well approximate
\mathcal{K}
, and the algorithm’s final output is approximately optimal.

Input.
A total of
s
servers and a blackboard, with the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
server storing indices
D_{i}\subseteq[d]
where
d_{i}=|D_{i}|
and a convex function
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}
. Each server
i
has access to the subgradient oracle of
f_{i}
. An initial vector
\theta^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\|\theta^{(0)}-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}\leq R
.

Output.
A vector
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
on all machines such that
\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}(\mathbf{\theta})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}(\theta^{\star})+\varepsilon\cdot\mu R.

Initialize.
Set initial parameters

m=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}
,
t=\frac{m\log m}{\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R}
,
t_{\textrm{end}}=\frac{8m}{\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R}
, and
\eta=\frac{1}{100}
. Reformulate the problem into
Section

5.1
using
Lemma

5.12
. Find the initial
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
using
Theorem

5.13
.
Modify the program according to
Lemma

5.14
and obtain initial feasible
\mathbf{x}
with
\alpha=2^{16}\frac{m^{2.5}R}{r\varepsilon}
. Compute
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
(via
Eq.

5.3
)

while
true
do

All servers check if
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}+\frac{4m}{t}
, and if true

(a)

All servers check
t\geq t_{\textrm{end}}
; if true, they compute and return
\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\left\{t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\right\}

(b)

All servers update
t
to
t\cdot\left(1+\frac{\eta}{4m}\right)

(c)

All servers update
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
(
Eq.

5.3
) and jumps to the start of the
while
loop

All servers find the smallest index
\mathsf{idx}
such that
\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\rangle+\eta\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}\geq 4\nu_{i}

For
all
i\in[s]

do

(a)

Each server
i
checks if
i=\mathsf{idx}
; if so then query
\mathcal{O}_{i}
at
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}
and send
\mathcal{O}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star})
to the blackboard, otherwise read the result of
\mathcal{O}_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star})
from blackboard.

i.

If
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
then
set
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}=\textrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i},\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}\right\}

ii.

Otherwise,
set
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}\cap\mathcal{H}_{i}

iii.

Update
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
and jump to the start of the
while
loop

Set
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}},
where

\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}

Set
\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}+\delta_{\mathbf{x}}

Return:
Recover
\theta
from
\mathbf{x}
according to
Lemmas

and

.

Algorithm 12
:
Minimizing sum of convex functions in the blackboard model.

alg]alg:min-sum-convex-blackboard

5.2
An Overview of Our Analysis

We note that in
, after initialization, machines send a message to the blackboard only if the separation oracle is queried; each such message encodes a halfspace in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
, which can be encoded using
O(d_{i}\cdot L)
bits. Therefore, the communication complexity of

can be bounded as
\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}d_{i}L
, where
n_{i}
is the number of separation oracle calls on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

In this section, we show that for given any weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}^{s}
, the total cost of all separation oracle queries is at most

\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}n_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}\log(sdR/(\varepsilon r)).

Although we will simply use
w_{i}=d_{i}
in this paper, we believe that the use of other weights could be useful in other applications.

To analyze the oracle cost of
,
we define a potential function
that captures the changes in
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
,
t
, and
\mathbf{x}

in each iteration. We define
\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\varphi_{\mathrm{out,i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
and use

\widehat{\varphi}_{\text{out}}
to denote
\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}
restricted to the set
\{\mathbf{u}:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{b}\}
. We further use
f^{*}
to denote the Fenchel conjugate of the function
f
. Then we define our potential as

\Phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\underbrace{t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}+\widehat{\varphi}_{\text{out}}^{*}(-t\mathbf{c})}_{\text{outer potential terms}}+\underbrace{\sum_{i\in[s]}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})}_{\text{inner potential terms}},

(5.4)

where
\widehat{\varphi}_{\text{out}}^{*}(-t\mathbf{c})=\max_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{b}}-t\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{u}-\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathbf{u})=-\left(\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{b}}t\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{u}+\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathbf{u})\right).

Following the choice of the barrier functions in
[
DJLPY22
]
, we use the universal barrier
[
LY21
]
for
\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}
and the entropic barrier
[
BE15
,
Che23
]
8
8

8

While
[
DJLPY22
]
uses a simple entropic barrier for the entire
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
, we are using a weighted sum of entropic barriers on all
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
’s.
for
\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
.
In the subsequent
sections, we study the changes in each of these potential functions along with obtaining bounds on the initial and final potentials
and combine them to bound the algorithm’s separation oracle complexity.

5.2.1
Potential Change Upon Shrinking an Outer Set

Ideally, we want to show that the potential decreases
uniformly after each separation oracle query to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Formally, we want to use a self-concordant barrier function satisfying the condition below, which we conjecture holds for all self-concordant functions.
In this paper, we prove that the entropic barrier satisfies it (and use this fact in our analysis).

Conjecture 5.2
.

Let
\phi
be a self-concordant barrier. Denote
\phi_{\mathcal{S}}
to be
\phi
restricted to the set
\mathcal{S}
. Given a bounded convex body
\mathcal{K}
and cost vector
\mathbf{c}
, define
\mathbf{x}^{\star}=\arg\min\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})
, and let hyperplane
\mathcal{H}
contain a point
\mathbf{z}
such that
\|\mathbf{x}^{\star}-\mathbf{z}\|_{(\nabla^{2}\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x}^{\star}))^{-1}}<0.01
.
Then

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}+0.1.

The analysis in
[
Vai89
]
suggests that the volumetric barrier might satisfy the condition above. In this paper, we instead use a weighted version of the entropic barrier on
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
, which is easier to analyze and implement.
Before we prove that the entropic barrier satisfies the conditions above, we first show how
5.2
implies the desired potential decrease.

Lemma 5.3
.

Consider the Cartesian product
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{K}_{s}
, where each
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a bounded convex set associated with a self-concordant barrier function
\varphi_{i}
satisfying the inequality in
5.2
. Given a weight vector
\mathbf{w}_{\geq 1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the cost vector
\mathbf{c}
, we define the weighted barrier
\varphi(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\varphi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
and the corresponding analytic center
\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})
. Fix
i
, let
\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\text{new}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{K}_{i}\cap\mathcal{H}
, where
\mathcal{H}
contains a point
\mathbf{z}
such that
\|\mathbf{z}-\mu_{i}\|_{(\nabla^{2}w_{i}\varphi_{i}(\mu_{i}))^{-1}}\leq 0.01
. We define
\mathcal{K}^{\text{new}}
and
\varphi^{\text{new}}
correspondingly.
Then, for any
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
, we have

\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})\geq\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})+0.1w_{i}

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{x}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})
. Using the appropiate Lagrange multiplier
\mathbf{z}
, we can find
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
such that

\mathbf{x}^{\star}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x}).

These definitions of
\mathbf{x}^{\star}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
imply the following connection:

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{\star}+\varphi(\mathbf{x}^{\star})=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x}).

(5.5)

Then, by the definition of
\varphi^{\text{new}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\varphi_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})+w_{i}\varphi_{i}^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\varphi_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})+w_{i}(\varphi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})+0.1)\right\}

\displaystyle=0.1w_{i}+\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=0.1w+\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\star},

(5.6)

where the second is by
5.2
applied to
\varphi_{i}
, the third step follows by definition of
\varphi
, and the last step follows by
Eq.

5.5
. Since our desired bound is on
\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})
, we now define the minimizer

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x}).

(5.7)

We may now observe the following upper bound on

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})
:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}})+\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}},

(5.8)

where the first step is by restricting the set of minimization, the second step uses the fact that
\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}\in\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\}
, the third step uses the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
, and the final step is by plugging in the definition of
\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}
from
Eq.

5.7
. Chaining
Eq.

5.6
and
Eq.

5.8
gives

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varphi^{\text{new}}(\mathbf{x})-\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\geq 0.1w_{i}+\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}})=0.1w_{i},

(5.9)

where the second step is because the definitions of
\mathbf{x}^{\star,\mathrm{new}}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\star}
imply that both
satisfy
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}.

∎

Now, we show that the entropic barrier satisfies the condition in
5.2
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Given a bounded convex body
\mathcal{K}
and a vector
\mathbf{c}
, let
\phi
be the entropic barrier and
\mathbf{x}^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})
. Let the halfspace
\mathcal{H}
contain a point
\mathbf{z}
such that
\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}^{\star}\|_{(\nabla^{2}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{\star})^{-1}}\leq t
, then

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}-\log(1-1/e+t).

Proof.

Let
\phi_{\mathcal{K}}
be the entropic barrier, we note that
the Fenchel conjugate of
\phi_{\mathcal{K}}
,
\phi_{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(-\mathbf{c})
is

\phi_{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(-\mathbf{c})=-1\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}}({\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})}).

Recall that
\phi^{*}(\cdot)
is the logarithmic Laplace transform of the uniform measure on
\mathcal{K}

[
BE15
]
, we have

\phi_{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(-\mathbf{c})=\log\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{u}\rangle)d\mathbf{u}.

Consider the distribution over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with following density measure:

p(\mathbf{x})=\exp(-\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}\rangle-\phi_{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(-\mathbf{c}))\cdot\mathbb{I}\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}\}.

Using
Lemma

A.24
, we have

\mathbf{x}^{\star}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim p}[\mathbf{x}]
, is the centroid of
p
. It is an established fact that (see e.g.
[
BE15
, Lemma 1]
)

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim p}[(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\star})(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\star})^{\top}]=\nabla^{2}\phi^{*}_{\mathcal{K}}(-\mathbf{c})=(\nabla^{2}\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x}^{\star}))^{-1}.

Then, by Grünbaum’s Theorem (
Theorem

A.22
), we have we have

\int_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}p(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}\geq 1/e-t,

which implies that

\int_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}p(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}\exp(-\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x})\rangle d\mathbf{x}}{\phi_{\mathcal{K}}^{*}(-\mathbf{c})}\leq 1-1/e+t.

Taking the logarithm of both sides, we get

\phi^{*}_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}(-\mathbf{c})-\phi^{*}_{\mathcal{K}}(-\mathbf{c})\leq\log(1-1/e+t).

This finishes the claim:

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}-\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\phi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})\right\}=-\phi^{*}_{\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H}}(-\mathbf{c})+\phi^{*}_{\mathcal{K}}(-\mathbf{c})\geq-\log(1-1/e+t).

∎

5.2.2
Potential Change Upon Increasing
t

To capture the change in potential due to the update in
t
, we require a technical result derived from properties of conjugates of self-concordant barriers. To obtain this result, we use a helper result from
[
DJLPY22
]
, and based on this lemma, we prove a more general one in
Lemma

5.6
.

Lemma 5.5

(Lemma
4.2
of
[
DJLPY22
]
)
.

Consider a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier
\psi:\textrm{int}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

over the interior of a convex set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define

\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left[t\cdot\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}\rangle+\psi(\mathbf{x})\right]\text{ and }\mathbf{x}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}).

(5.10)

Then for
0\leq h\leq\frac{1}{3\sqrt{\nu}}
, we have

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+th\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t}\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}_{t}}-h^{2}\nu.

Lemma 5.6
.

Consider a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier
\psi:\textrm{int}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

over the interior of a convex set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define

\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}).

(5.11)

Then for
0\leq h\leq\frac{1}{3\sqrt{\nu}}
, we have

\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+th\cdot{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\geq\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\geq\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}-h^{2}\nu.

Proof.

The first inequality holds for
any function
\psi
with the specified definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
and
\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}
:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq t(1+h)\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\text{$\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}$}+\psi(\text{$\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}$})=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+th\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t},

where the first inequality is by plugging in
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
into
\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})
and the second step by using the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
from
Eq.

5.11
. To prove the second inequality, we use the self-concordance of
\psi
. First, using the appropriate Lagrange multiplier
\mathbf{z}
, one can define
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}:=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
to express
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
as the minimizer of an unconstrained problem as follows:

\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t\text{$\widehat{\mathbf{c}}$}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}),\text{ for }\widehat{\mathbf{c}}:=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{z}.

(5.12)

As a result, we may now apply
Lemma

5.5
, which gives

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu

\displaystyle=\xi_{t\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\text{$\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}$})+ht\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu

\displaystyle=\xi_{t\text{$\mathbf{c}$}}^{\psi}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t})+ht\cdot\text{$\mathbf{c}$}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu+\text{$(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}$},

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu+(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t},

(5.13)

where the first and second steps are by
Lemma

5.5
applied to
\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}
and the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
from
Eq.

5.12
, the third step is by using the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}:=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
, and the final step is by applying the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
from
Eq.

5.11
. We now define

{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}).

In the other direction, we have

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\text{$\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}$}}\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\text{${\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}$})

\displaystyle=\xi_{t(1+h)\text{$\mathbf{c}$}}^{\psi}({\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}})+(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}},

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}},

(5.15)

where the first step is by constraining the minimization set, the second step is because, by definition of
{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}
from
Section

5.2.2
, it satisfies
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
, the third step is by replacing
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}:=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{z}
, and the fourth step is by definition of
{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}
in
Section

5.2.2
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\xi_{t(1+h)\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})-(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu+(1+h)t\cdot\mathbf{z}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}),

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\psi}(\mathbf{x})+ht\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-h^{2}\nu,

where the first step is by
rearranging
Eq.

5.15
, the second step is by
Eq.

5.13
, and the final step is by the fact that both
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{t(1+h)}^{\mathrm{new}}
satisfy
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

To finally compute the potential change due to
t,
we combine the result from
Lemma

5.6
with the bound guaranteed by
Item

of
along with the self-concordance parameter of the volumetric barrier.
We may now compute the potential change due to change in
t
in
Item

b
.

Lemma 5.7
.

When
t
is updated to
t\cdot\left[1+\frac{\eta}{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}\right]

in
Item

b
of
, the potential
\Phi

Eq.

5.4
increases to
\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}
as follows:

\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}\leq\Phi+\eta+\eta^{2}.

Proof.

From
Eq.

5.4
,
the change in potential by changing
t
to
t\cdot(1+h)
for some
h>0
may be expressed as

\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}-\Phi=-\underbrace{\left(\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t(1+h)\mathbf{c}}^{\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}}(\mathbf{y})-\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}\xi_{t\mathbf{c}}^{\varphi_{\mathrm{out}}}(\mathbf{y})\right)}_{\text{Bounded via \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:EtChangeConstrained}}}+th\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}.

We may now apply
Lemma

5.6

in the preceding equation to obtain the following bound.

\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}-\Phi\leq th\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}-th\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}}+h^{2}\nu.

We see that
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
as defined in
Eq.

5.11
for
\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
from
Eq.

5.3
is exactly identical to
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
from
Eq.

5.3
.
We can therefore apply the guarantee

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}}+\frac{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}{t}
(from
Item

of
) and

h=\frac{\eta}{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}
and
\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}
to obtain

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}-\Phi

\displaystyle\leq th\cdot\frac{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}{t}+h^{2}\nu=\eta+\left(\frac{\eta}{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}\right)^{2}\nu\leq\eta+\eta^{2}.

∎

5.2.3
Potential Change Upon Growing an Inner Set

Here, we state the technical lemma that describes the change in the universal barrier potential when we add a point to the convex set.

Lemma 5.8

(
[
DJLPY22
, Lemma 4.6]
)
.

Given a convex set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}

and a point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}
, let
\psi_{\mathcal{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\log\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{K}-\mathbf{x})^{\circ}

be the universal barrier defined on
\mathcal{K}
with respect to
\mathbf{x}.

Let
\mathbf{y}\notin\mathcal{K}
be a point satisfying the following condition for some scalar
\eta\leq 1/4

\langle\nabla\psi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\rangle+\eta\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x}}\geq 4d.

(5.16)

Then, the
universal barrier (cf.
Definition

A.21
) defined on the set
\mathrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{K},\mathbf{y}\right\}
with respect
to
\mathbf{x}
satisfies the following inequality:

\psi_{\mathcal{K},\textrm{new}}(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\psi_{\mathrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{K},\mathbf{y}\right\}}(\mathbf{x})=\log\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{K},\mathbf{y}\right\}-\mathbf{x})^{\circ}\leq\psi_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbf{x})+\log(1-1/e+\eta).

5.2.4
Potential Change For the Update of
\mathbf{x}

In this section, we quantify the amount of progress made in
Item

of
by computing the change in the potential

\Phi
as defined in
Eq.

5.4
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Consider the potential
\Phi

Eq.

5.4
. Denote by
\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}
the value of this potential after
\mathbf{x}
takes the update step
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}

as in
Item

.
Assume the following guarantees

•

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}+\frac{4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}{t}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
.

•

\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\rangle+\eta\cdot\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}\leq 4\nu_{i}
for all
i\in[s]
.

Then the potential
\Phi
incurs the following
minimum decrease.

\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}\leq\Phi-\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\leq\Phi-\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma 4.7 in
[
DJLPY22
]
, we include it here for completeness.
Taking the gradient of
\Phi
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
and rearranging
the terms gives

t\mathbf{c}=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi-\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),

(5.17)

where we are overloading notation in
\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})
to mean the
d
-dimensional vector equalling the appropriate entries at the
d_{i}
coordinates corresponding to
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}
and zero elsewhere.
By replacing
t\mathbf{c}
with the expression on the right-hand side of the preceding equation,
we get

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}-\Phi

\displaystyle=t\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}+\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}+\delta_{\mathbf{x},i})-t\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi,\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\underbrace{\left[\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}+\delta_{\mathbf{x},i})-\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})-\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\rangle\right]}_{q_{\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})}.

(5.18)

Note that in substituting
Eq.

5.17
above, we crucially use that
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}
are all disjoint vectors whose coordinates completely cover those of
\mathbf{x}
.
The term
q_{\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})
measures the error due to first-order
approximation of
\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}
around
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}
. Since each
\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})
is a self-concordant function and
\|\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\leq\|\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}\leq\eta\leq 1/4
, this error is known to be small; more precisely,

Theorem

A.20
applies and gives

\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}+\delta_{\mathbf{x},i})-\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})-\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\rangle\leq\|\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}^{2}.

(5.19)

Plugging in
Eq.

5.19

into
Eq.

5.18
, we get

\Phi^{\textrm{(new)}}-\Phi\leq\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi,\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\|\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}^{2}.

(5.20)

We now bound the two terms on the right hand side one at a time. Using
the definition of
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
(as given in the statement of the lemma)
and of
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi
from
Eq.

5.17
gives

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi,\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi,\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\left[\langle t\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\langle\nabla\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}),\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out},i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\left[\langle t\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\left(4\nu_{i}-\eta\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\left[\langle t\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\rangle+4\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}-\eta\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}\cdot\left(-\eta\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{i}}\right)=-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}}.

(5.21)

where the third step follows from the second assumption, and the fifth step follows
from the first assumption. To bound the second term

Item

that

\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\|\delta_{\mathbf{x},i}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}^{2}=\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out,}i}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\|_{\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\mathbf{x},1}^{2}}.

(5.22)

Hence, we may plug in
Eq.

5.21
and
Eq.

5.22

into
Eq.

5.20
to get the desired result.
∎

5.2.5
Total Oracle Cost

Before we bound the communication complexity of the algorithm, we first bound the total potential change throughout the algorithm.

Lemma 5.10
.

Consider the potential function

\Phi(t,\mathbf{x},\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}},\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\left(\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})

as defined in
Eq.

5.4
associated with
. Let
\Phi_{\textrm{init}}
be the potential at
t=t_{\textrm{init}}
of this algorithm, and let
\Phi_{\textrm{end}}
be the potential at
t=t_{\textrm{end}}
. Suppose at
t=t_{\textrm{init}}
in
, we have, for some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
, that
\mathcal{B}_{m}(\mathbf{z},\bar{r})\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
with
\bar{r}=r/\operatorname{poly}(m)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{m}(0,\bar{R})
for
\bar{R}=O(\sqrt{n}R)
. Then we have, under the assumptions of
Theorem

5.1
, that

\Phi_{\textrm{init}}-\Phi_{\textrm{end}}\leq O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}\log\left(\frac{mR}{\varepsilon r}\right)\right).

Proof.

We bound the change in the potential term by term, starting with the following terms depending on the current iterate
\mathbf{x}
, the current time step
t
, and the current outer set
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
:

\Psi(t,\mathbf{x},\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\left(\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\right).

(5.23)

We introduce the notation
\Psi_{\textrm{init}}:=\Psi(t_{\textrm{init}},\mathbf{x}(t_{\textrm{init}}),\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}(t_{\textrm{init}}))
and simplify it as follows.

\displaystyle\Psi_{\textrm{init}}

\displaystyle=t_{\textrm{init}}\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\left(\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}t_{\textrm{init}}\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\leq t_{\textrm{init}}\cdot(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y})-\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq o(m)-\min_{\mathbf{y}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i}),

(5.24)

where the second inequality follows from our choice of
t_{\textrm{init}}
implying
t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=o(m)
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
and also because expanding the set of minimization only decreases the minimum value; note that because of
\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
, the variable
\mathbf{y}
is implicitly already restricted to
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
. We emphasize that
\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
here is the barrier function on the
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
at
t=t_{\textrm{init}}
.
Let
\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}
be the analytic center of
\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
:

\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{y}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i}).

Hence, we may rewrite
\Psi_{\textrm{init}}
using
Section

5.2.5
to obtain

\Psi_{\textrm{init}}\leq o(m)-\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out}}(\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\star}).

To bound
\Psi_{\textrm{end}}
, we define:

\mathbf{y}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\arg\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}(t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i}))

Similar to
\Psi_{\textrm{init}}
, we define
\Psi_{\textrm{end}}:=\Psi(t_{\textrm{end}},\mathbf{x}(t_{\textrm{end}}),\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}(t_{\textrm{end}}))
. Then, with this notation, we may use
\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}}}
to state the following lower bound

\displaystyle\Psi_{\textrm{end}}

\displaystyle=t_{\textrm{end}}\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}}(t_{\textrm{end}}\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{i}))

\displaystyle\geq t_{\textrm{end}}\cdot(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{\infty}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}}})-\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i})).

(5.28)

By
Lemma

A.10
, we may bound the difference
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{\infty}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}}}
as follows:

t_{\textrm{end}}\cdot(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{\infty}-\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}}})\geq-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}}{t_{\textrm{end}}}\cdot t_{\textrm{end}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}.

where we used
A.18
to deduce that the self-concordance parameter of
\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
restricted to
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}
is
\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\nu_{i}
and each
\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}
is
O(d_{i})
self-concordant.
We now claim that

\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i})\leq\psi_{\textrm{out,}i}(\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i})+O(d_{i}\log(dR/r)).

Before proving this claim, we see that by combining
Sections

5.2.5
,
5.28
,
5.2.5
and
5.2.5
, that

\Psi_{\textrm{init}}-\Psi_{\textrm{end}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}\log(5dR/r)+o(m).

We now show the claim in
Section

5.2.5
. In order to apply
A.19
, we consider the ray starting from by
\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}
that passes through
\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i}
. Let
\mathbf{y}_{\textrm{bdry},i}
to be the point where the ray intersects with the set
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
. Note that there is a
s\in(0,1)
, such that

\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i}=\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}+s(\mathbf{y}_{\textrm{brdy},i}-\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}).

We note that

s=\frac{\|\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i}\|}{\|\mathbf{y}_{\textrm{brdy},i}-\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}\|}\geq\frac{\|\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i}\|}{2R}.

By
Lemma

A.10
, we have
\|\mathbf{y}^{\star}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{t_{\textrm{end}},i}\|\geq\frac{\bar{r}}{5d_{i}}
. We finish the proof of
Section

5.2.5
using
A.19
.

Since the potential is a sum of universal barrier and entropic barrier terms, we now need to bound the change in the universal barrier. The proof of this change is identical to the corresponding proof in Lemma 4.8 of
[
DJLPY22
]
, but we include it next for completeness.
Recall the definition of the universal barrier terms
\sum_{i\in[n]}w_{i}\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})
, where

\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})=\log\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}^{\circ}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})).

Our computation follows a purely volume-based argument based on our assumptions about the dimensions of the balls contained in and containing the sets
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
and repeated application of
A.8
, as we now elaborate. Define
\mathcal{B}_{d}(0,r)
to be the
d
-dimensional Euclidean ball centered at the origin and with radius
r
. We note by the radius assumption of
Theorem

5.1
that
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{d_{i}}(0,\bar{R})
throughout the algorithm. By the assumption made in this lemma, we have at the start of
the inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{d_{i}}(\mathbf{z}_{i},\bar{r})\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
. These two inclusion assumptions and
A.8
lead to the following bounds for any
\mathbf{x}_{i}
.

\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}^{\text{end}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\geq\log(\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{B}^{\circ}_{d_{i}}(0,\bar{R}))\text{ and }\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}^{\text{init}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\leq\log(\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{B}_{d_{i}}^{\circ}(\mathbf{z}_{i},\bar{r}))).

Combining
Section

5.2.5
, the fact that
\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{B}_{d}(0,r))\propto r^{d}
,
A.8
, and summing over all
i\in[s]
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[s]}w_{i}\left[\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}^{\text{init}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})-\psi_{\textrm{in,}i}^{\text{end}}(\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[s]}w_{i}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{B}_{d_{i}}(\mathbf{z}_{i},1/\bar{r}))}{\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{B}_{d_{i}}(0,1/\bar{R}))}\right)=\sum_{i\in[s]}w_{i}d_{i}\log(\bar{R}/\bar{r})

(5.33)

We finishes the proof by combining the inequality above and
Section

5.2.5
.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Total oracle cost)
.

Suppose the inputs
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
to
satisfy
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{m}(0,\bar{R})
with
\bar{R}=O(\sqrt{n}R)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}\supseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z},\bar{r})
with
\bar{r}=r/\operatorname{poly}(m)
. Then, when
terminates at
t\geq t_{\textrm{end}}
, it outputs a solution
\mathbf{x}
that satisfies

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K},A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R.

Moreover, if the cost of the separation oracle on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is
w_{i}
and
n_{i}
is the number of times
\mathcal{O}_{i}
is queried for all
i\in[s]
, then the total cost of the separation oracle is at most
O\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}d_{i}\log\left(\frac{mR}{\varepsilon r}\right)\right)
. Namely,

\sum_{i}^{s}w_{i}n_{i}\leq O\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}d_{i}\log\left(\frac{mR}{\varepsilon r}\right)\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{t}}
be the number of times
t
is updated;
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{in},i}
the number of times
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
is updated;
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{out},i}
the number of times
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{out,}i}
is updated;
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{x}}
the number of times
\mathbf{x}
is updated, and
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{total}}
the total number of iterations of the
while
loop before termination of
.
Then, combining
Lemmas

5.3
,
5.4
,
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
gives

\Phi_{\textrm{end}}\leq\Phi_{\textrm{init}}-\sum_{i=1}^{s}0.1w_{i}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{out},i}+\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{t}}\cdot(\eta+\eta^{2})+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{in},i}\cdot w_{i}\log(1-1/e+\eta)+\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}\right).

The initialization step of
chooses
\eta=1/100
,
t_{\textrm{end}}=\frac{8m}{\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R}
, and
t_{\textrm{init}}=\frac{m\log(m)}{\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R}
, and we always update
t
by a multiplicative factor of
1+\frac{\eta}{4\sum_{i}w_{i}\nu_{i}}
(see
Item

b
); therefore, we have

\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{t}}=O(\sum_{i}w_{i}\nu_{i}\log(mR/(\varepsilon r)).

From
, the only times the separation oracle is invoked is when updating
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
or
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}
in
Item

(a)i
and
Item

(a)ii
, respectively. Therefore, the cost of separation oracle on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is
w_{i}n_{i}=w_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{in},i}+\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{out},i})
. Therefore, we have by applying the bound on
\Phi_{\textrm{init}}-\Phi_{\textrm{end}}
from
Lemma

5.10
and the bound on
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{t}}
from
Section

5.2.5

\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{in}}+\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{out}})\leq O(1)\cdot\left[\Phi_{\textrm{init}}-\Phi_{\textrm{end}}+\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{t}}\right]=O(\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}d_{i}\log(mR/(\varepsilon r)),

which is the claimed separation oracle complexity. We now prove the guarantee on approximation. Let
\mathbf{x}_{\text{output}}
be the output of
and
\mathbf{x}
be the point which entered
Item

right before termination. Note that the termination of
implies, by
Item

, that

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\text{output}}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\frac{\nu}{t_{\textrm{end}}}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}+\frac{4(n+m)}{t_{\textrm{end}}}\leq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K},A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}\cdot R

where the first step is by the second inequality in
Lemma

A.10
(using the universal barrier) and the last step follows by our choice of
t_{\textrm{end}}
and the definition of
\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{out}}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{out}}\supseteq\mathcal{K}
.
∎

5.2.6
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

We now use the results from the prior sections to complete our proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

We apply
Theorem

5.13
for each
\mathcal{K}_{i}
separately to find a solution
\mathbf{z}_{i}
. Then
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
satisfies
\mathcal{B}_{m+n}(\mathbf{z},\bar{r})\subset\mathcal{K}
with
\bar{r}=\frac{r}{6d^{3.5}}
.
Then, we modify the convex problem as in
Lemma

5.14
with
\alpha=2^{16}\frac{m^{2.5}R}{r\varepsilon}
and obtain the following:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{minimize}&\bar{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}{\bar{\mathbf{x}}}\\
\mbox{subject to}&\bar{\mathbf{A}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}=\bar{\mathbf{b}},\\
&\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{K}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m+n}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m+n}\end{array}

with

\bar{\mathbf{A}}=[\mathbf{A}\mid\mathbf{A}\mid-\mathbf{A}],\bar{\mathbf{b}}=\mathbf{b},\bar{\mathbf{c}}=(\mathbf{c},\frac{\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}s}{\sqrt{m+n}}\cdot\mathbf{1},\frac{\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}s}{\sqrt{m+n}}\cdot\mathbf{1})^{\top}

We solve the linear system
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}
for
\mathbf{y}
. Then, we construct the initial
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
by setting
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)}=\mathbf{z}
,

\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{(2)}_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{y}_{i}&\text{if }\mathbf{y}_{i}\geq 0,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{(3)}_{i}=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{y}_{i}&\text{if }\mathbf{y}_{i}<0,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then, we run
on the
Section

5.2.6
, with
initial
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
set above,

\bar{m}=3(m+n),\bar{n}=n+2,\bar{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{6\sqrt{n}s},\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\textrm{in}}=\{\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\in B(\mathbf{z},\bar{r}),(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}\}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{\textrm{out}}=\mathcal{B}_{\bar{m}}(\mathbf{0},\sqrt{n}R)
.
By our choice of
t_{\textrm{end}}
, we have

\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}=\frac{8\bar{m}}{\bar{\varepsilon}\|\bar{\mathbf{c}}\|_{2}\bar{R}}\leq\frac{48m}{\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R}.

First, we check the condition that
\alpha\geq 48\bar{\nu}\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}\sqrt{m+n}\frac{R^{2}}{r}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}
, we note that

48\bar{\nu}\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}\sqrt{m+n}\frac{R^{2}}{r}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}\leq 27648\frac{m^{2.5}R}{\varepsilon r}\leq 2^{16}\frac{m^{2.5}R}{r\varepsilon}=\alpha.

Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{output}=(\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(3)})
be the output of
.
Then, let
\mathbf{x}_{output}=\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(1)}+\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(2)}-\mathbf{x}_{output}^{(3)}
as defined in
Lemma

5.14
.
By
Lemma

5.11
, we have

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{in}}}\bar{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\leq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}+\gamma

where
\gamma=\bar{\varepsilon}\cdot\|\bar{\mathbf{c}}\|_{2}\cdot\bar{R}
.
Applying (3) of
Lemma

5.14
, we have

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{output}\leq\frac{\bar{\nu}+1}{\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}}+\gamma+\min_{x\in\mathcal{K},A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq\min_{x\in\mathcal{K},A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon\cdot\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}\cdot R.

The last inequality follows by our choice of
\bar{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}
, we have

\gamma\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R
and
\frac{\bar{\nu}+1}{\bar{t}_{\textrm{end}}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}R
.
Plug this
\bar{\varepsilon}
in
Lemma

5.11
, it gives the claimed oracle cost.

∎

5.2.7
Proof of Main Result of Finite-Sum Minimization (
Theorem

1.13
)

See
1.13

Proof.

First, we reformulate the problem into
Section

5.1
using
Lemma

5.12
.
Then, we apply
Theorem

5.1
to the reduced problem to get the error guarantee. For the communication complexity, we note that during initialization, each machine sends the initial
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in,}i}
it found, which takes
O(\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}L)
bits of communication. Then, in the main loop, each machine sends the output of the separation oracle, which takes
O(\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}^{2}\log(sd/\varepsilon)\cdot L)
bits of communication by setting
w_{i}=d_{i}
.

Finally, we show that it suffices to take the word length
L=O(\log(dR/r))
, which is
O(\log(d))
by our choice of
R
and
r
. Recall that in the algorithm, each message encodes the description of a halfspace
\mathcal{H}
, which is described using two vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}
by
\mathcal{H}=\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v})\geq 0\}
. We describe these vectors in the relative scale of
r
.
Lemma

5.3
shows that if suffices to send
\mathbf{v}
such that
\|\mathbf{v}_{i}-\mu_{i}\|_{(\nabla^{2}w_{i}\varphi_{i}(\mu_{i}))^{-1}}\leq 0.01
. Using
Corollary

A.15
, we know
(\nabla^{2}w_{i}\varphi_{i}(\mu_{i}))^{-1}\succeq 4w_{i}R
, hence it suffices to set word length of
\mathbf{v}
to be
O(\log(dR/r))
. For
\mathbf{u}
, we note that even if
\mathcal{H}
cuts through
\mathcal{K}
, as long as the radius of
\mathcal{K}
is decreasing at rate of
1/d^{C}
for some large constant
C
, the algorithm still works. Therefore, it suffices to choose the word length of
\mathbf{u}
to be
O(\log(dR/r))
.
∎

5.3
Reductions and Initializations

Lemma 5.12
.

Given the same setup of
Theorem

1.13
, there is an algorithm using
O(sd\log(d))
bits of communication, which reduces the original problem to the following formulation,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{minimize}&\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x},\\
\mbox{subject to}&\mathbf{x}_{\textrm{i}}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}+1}\;\forall i\in[s]\\
&\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}.\end{array}

where
\mathbf{x}=[\mathbf{x}_{i}]
concatenates the
s
vectors
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
. Denote
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\times\mathcal{K}_{2}\times\dotsc\times\mathcal{K}_{s}
,
\mathcal{K}
satisfies the following properties:

convexity: Each
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is convex and disjoint with each other.

outer radius
R
: For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}\leq R
.

inner radius
r
: There exists a
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z},r)\subset\mathcal{K}
.

radius ratio:
R/r=O(1)
.

After the reduction, all the machines hold all the data — radii
R
and
r
, vectors
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x}
, matrix
\mathbf{A}
— and the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
machine holds the separation oracle
\mathcal{O}_{i}
for the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
set
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Proof.

The reduction is standard by using using a change of variables and the epigraph trick, but we include it here for completeness.
Suppose each
f_{i}
depends on
d_{i}
coordinates of
\mathbf{\theta}
given by
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{d_{i}}\}\subseteq[d]
. Then,
symbolically define
\mathbf{x}_{i}=[x^{(i)}_{i_{1}};x^{(i)}_{i_{2}};\dots;x^{(i)}_{i_{d_{i}}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
for each
i\in[n]
.
Since each
f_{i}
is convex and supported on
d_{i}
variables, its epigraph is convex and
d_{i}+1
dimensional. So we may define the convex set

\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\textrm{unbounded}}=\left\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},z_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}+1}:f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\leq Lz_{i}\right\}.

Finally, we add linear constraints of the form
x^{(i)}_{k}=x^{(j)}_{k}
for all
i,j,k
where
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
both depend on
\mathbf{\theta}_{k}
. We denote these by the matrix constraint
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
.
Then, the problem is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{minimize}&\sum_{i=1}^{s}Lz_{i}\\
\mbox{subject to}&\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}\\
&(\mathbf{x}_{i},z_{i})\in\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\textrm{unbounded}}\text{ for each $i\in[n]$}.\end{array}

(5.37)

Since we are given
\mathbf{\theta}^{(0)}
satisfying
\|{\mathbf{\theta}^{(0)}-\mathbf{\theta}^{*}}\|_{2}\leq D
,
we define
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(0)}=[\mathbf{\theta}^{(0)}_{i_{1}};\dots,\mathbf{\theta}^{(0)}_{i_{d_{i}}}]
and

z_{i}^{(0)}=f_{i}(\mathbf{\theta}^{(0)})/L
.
Then, we can restrict the search space
\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\textrm{unbounded}}
to

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\textrm{unbounded}}\cap\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},z_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}+1}:\|\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(0)}\|_{2}\leq D\text{ and }z_{i}^{(0)}-2D\leq z_{i}\leq z_{i}^{(0)}+2D\}.

One can then check that
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is contained in a ball of radius
5D
centered at
(\mathbf{x}^{(0)}_{i},z_{i}^{(0)})

and contains a ball of radius
D
centered at
(\mathbf{x}^{(0)}_{i},z_{i}^{(0)})
.
The subgradient oracle for
f_{i}
translates to a separation oracle for
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
We note that this reduction only requires the knowledge of
L
,
R
,
\theta^{(0)}
, and the support
D_{i}
for each
f_{i}
. By sending these information to blackboard, each machine can apply this reduction on their own. This takes
O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}d_{i}\log(d)\right)
many bits of communication.
∎

Now, we show how to construct an initial set
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
and find a good initial point for
by slightly modifying the convex program above. These results first appeared in
[
DJLPY22
]
, which we slightly modify to suit our purpose.

Theorem 5.13

(
[
DJLPY22
, Lemma 5.1]
)
.

Suppose we have separation oracle access to a convex set
\mathcal{K}

satisfying
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z},r)\subseteq\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{0},R)
for some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, there is a randomized algorithm, which in
O(d\log(R/r))
separation oracle calls to
\mathcal{K}
, outputs a point

\mathbf{x}
such that
\mathcal{B}\left(\mathbf{x},\frac{r}{6d^{3.5}}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{K}
.

To find a good initialization for
, we need to slightly modify the convex program, for which we simply invoke the following result from
[
DJLPY22
]
.

Lemma 5.14

(
[
DJLPY22
, Lemma 5.6]
)
.

Given a convex program
\min_{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

with outer radius
R
and some
\alpha>0
,
we define
\mathbf{c}_{1}=\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}_{2}=\mathbf{c}_{3}=\frac{\alpha\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\mathbf{1}
and
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{K},(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2d}:\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\mathbf{x}^{(2)}-\mathbf{x}^{(3)})=\mathbf{b}\}
. We then define the modified convex program by

\min_{(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbf{c}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(2)}+\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(3)}.

Given some
\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}\in\mathcal{K}
where inner radius
r
, and an arbitrary
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we further define the function

f_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})=t(\mathbf{c}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\mathbf{c}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(2)}+\mathbf{c}_{3}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{(3)})+\psi_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}}}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})

where
\psi_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}}}
is some
\nu
self-concordant barrier for the set

\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}}=\{\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}},(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2d}:\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\mathbf{x}^{(2)}-\mathbf{x}^{(3)})=\mathbf{b}\}.

Given
\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(3)})=\arg\min_{(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})\in\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{in}}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)})
, we denote
\mathbf{x}_{\text{in}}=\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(1)}+\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(2)}-\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(3)}
.
Suppose
\min_{\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{in}}}\bar{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\leq\min_{\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{P}}\bar{\mathbf{c}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}+\gamma
and
\alpha\geq 48\nu t\sqrt{d}\cdot\frac{R}{r}\cdot\|c\|_{2}R
, then we have that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\text{in}}=\mathbf{b}
,
\mathbf{x}_{\text{in}}\in\mathcal{K}_{\textrm{in}}
,
and
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\text{in}}\leq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\frac{\nu+1}{t}+\gamma
.
We denote
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\mathbf{c}_{3})

by
\overline{\mathbf{c}}
.

6
Lower Bounds

6.1
Lower Bound Primitives

We introduce two fundamental communication problems, the latter of which is an
s
-player version of the first.

Problem 6.1
.

Alice an Bob hold unit vectors
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
respectively in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. They would like to decide between (a)
\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right\rangle=0
, and (b)
|\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right\rangle|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{d}
under the promise that one of these conditions holds.

See
1.17

For each of these problems we prove a corresponding hardness hardness result. For the two player version of the game we have the following communication lower bound.

Lemma 6.2
.

A protocol solving
6.1
with probability at least
0.9
requires at least
\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
communication for
r
-round protocols when
r\leq c\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}/\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
for an absolute constant
c
.

While our argument requires a technical assumption on the number of rounds, this can almost certainly be removed via a more careful analysis. Moreover, in constant dimension our argument directly implies the bound above, with no requirement on the number of rounds.

Given the lemma above we will show how to boost it to an
s
-player lower bound.

See
1.18

We will prove these results below. Before presenting the proofs we give the reductions to linear regression and linear programming.

6.2
Reduction to Linear Regression

We now present our lower bound for
6.3
and will then return to analyzing the communication complexity of the two problems above.

Our main interest is the following communication problem.

Problem 6.3
.

Each of
s
servers holds a matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
and vector
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}
for
i=1,\ldots,s.
Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
the vertical stack of the the
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
’s and
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}
’s respectively. All entries are held to
L
bits of precision. The coordinator must produce a vector
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
with

\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|\leq 2\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|,

where
\mathbf{x}_{*}=\argmin_{x}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|.

Theorem 6.4
.

A protocol that solves
6.3
with at least
0.99
probability requires
\tilde{\Omega}(sd\log L)

bits of communication provided that there are at most
C\log L/\log\log L
rounds of communication between the coordinator and each server for an absolute constant
C
.
Additionally, if
\mathbf{A}
is promised to have condition number at most
\kappa
then assuming at most
C\log\kappa/\log\log\kappa
rounds per server, any protocol requires at least
\tilde{\Omega}(sd\min(L,\log\kappa)
communication.

Proof.

We reduce from
1.17
above. To construct a linear system, the coordinator first computes an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots\mathbf{u}_{d-1}
for
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}
and then rounds each vector to
L
bits of precision to obtain
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d-1}^{\prime}
. The coordinator then forms a matrix with rows
\alpha\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d-1}^{\prime},
where
\alpha<1
is a small parameter that will be chosen later and where
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
is
\mathbf{v}
rounded to
L
bits of precision. Each server
i
simply holds the vector
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}
which is
\mathbf{w}_{i}
rounded to
L
bits of precision. Let
\mathbf{A}
denote this matrix which is distributed across the servers. Also set
\mathbf{b}
to have all entries equal to
0
, except with a
1
in the entry corresponding to the row
\alpha\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
. We will show that the norm of an approximate regression solution
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
allows us to distinguish between the two possibilities in
1.17
.

Suppose that for some
i
,
|\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\varepsilon/d.

We lower bound the smallest singular value of
\mathbf{A}.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be the matrix with rows
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d-1},\mathbf{w}_{i}.
To lower bound the smallest singular value of
\mathbf{M}
, let
\mathbf{x}
be an arbitrary unit vector and write
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{1}+\mathbf{x}_{2}
where
\mathbf{x}_{1}
is the projection of
\mathbf{x}
onto
\mathbf{v}.
Then
\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}\|\geq\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|
and also

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}\|\geq|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq|\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|-\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|-\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|-\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|.

Thus

\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}\|\geq\max(\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|_{,}\varepsilon^{\prime}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|-\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|)\geq\max(\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|_{,}(\varepsilon^{\prime}/2)\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|),

since the maximum of two numbers is at least their average. Since
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{x}_{2}\|^{2}=1,
this latter quantity is at least
\varepsilon^{\prime}/3.
This shows that
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{M})\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}/3.

Let
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
be matrix
\mathbf{M}
with the rows rounded as above. Note that all entries of
\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
are bounded in absolute value by
2^{-L}
so
\|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\|\leq 2^{-L}d.
It follows that

\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A})\geq\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{M}^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}/3-2^{-L}d\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}/4,

provided that we later choose
\log(1/\varepsilon)\leq L-\log(12d).

Now let
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy
\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}\leq(1+\beta)\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}
(we will later set
\beta=1
). Then we have

(1+\beta)\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}\geq\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{A}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*})\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}x_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2},

and so
\|\mathbf{A}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*})\|\leq\sqrt{\beta}
. So we get

\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A})\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}\|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}\|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}\|+\sqrt{\beta}\leq 1+\sqrt{\beta}.

Hence
\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}\|\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(1+\sqrt{\beta}).

Now suppose that
|\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle|=0
for all
i.
Set
\eta=2^{-L}d
. In this case, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}(\alpha^{-1}\mathbf{v})-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\right\rangle-1)^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left\langle\alpha^{-1}\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\left\langle\alpha^{-1}\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{2}+\alpha^{-2}d\eta^{2}+\alpha^{-2}s\eta^{2}

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{-2}s+\alpha^{-2}d+1)\eta^{2}.

Suppose that
\mathbf{x}_{*}
is the least squares solution and that
\|\mathbf{A}\hat{x}-b\|^{2}\leq(1+\beta)\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}.
Then

\displaystyle(1+\beta)(\alpha^{-2}s+\alpha^{-2}d+1)\eta^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1+\beta)\|\mathbf{A}(\alpha^{-1}\mathbf{v})-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1+\beta)\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(\left\langle\hat{\mathbf{x}},\alpha\mathbf{v}\right\rangle-1)^{2}.

This implies that

\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}\|\geq|\left\langle\hat{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle|\geq\alpha^{-1}\left(1-\eta\sqrt{(1+\beta)(\alpha^{-2}s+\alpha^{-2}d+1)}\right).

We can distinguish the two inputs using
\|\hat{x}\|
as long as

\frac{4}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{4d}{\varepsilon}<\alpha^{-1}\left(1-\eta\sqrt{(1+\beta)(\alpha^{-2}s+\alpha^{-2}d+1)}\right).

To make this happen we choose our parameters. Set
\beta=1
. The quantity in parentheses is at least
1/2
as long as
\eta\alpha^{-1}\sqrt{s+d}\leq 1/4.
So it suffices for this to hold, along with
\alpha^{-1}>\frac{8d}{\varepsilon}.

There is an
\alpha
that satisfies these bounds, as long as
\frac{8d}{\varepsilon}<\eta^{-1}(s+d)^{-1/2}=\frac{2^{L}}{d}(s+d)^{-1/2}
which holds when
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\leq L-4\log(s+d).
Given this bound, we can then take
\alpha=O(\varepsilon/d)
(say rounded to an appropriate power of
2
so that bit precision is unaffected), and take
\varepsilon
such that
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\leq L-c\log(s+d)
for an abolute constant
c.
Then
1.17
that we reduced from, requires
\Omega(sdL)
communication by
Theorem

1.18
, provided that
L\geq 8(s+d)
, and therefore so does
6.3
.

Finally we check the condition number of our hard instance. Note that we always have
\kappa(\mathbf{A})=\frac{\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{A})}{\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A})}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2d+s}}{\alpha}=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\operatorname{poly}(s+d)).
Choosing

\varepsilon\approx\min(\operatorname{poly}(s+d)/\kappa,L-c\log(s+d))

gives the second statement of the theorem.
∎

6.3
Reduction to Linear Feasibility

Problem 6.5
.

(Linear Feasibility) Each of
s
servers holds a matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}
and vector
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}.
All entries are held to
L
bits of precision. They would like to decide whether there is an
\mathbf{x}
satisfying
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{b}
.

See
1.16

Proof.

We give a simple reduction from
1.17
. Recall that in this problem, the coordinator holds a vector
\mathbf{v}
and the
s
servers holds vectors
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{s}.

Set
\eta=2^{-L}\lceil\sqrt{d}\rceil
. Let
v^{\prime}
denote
v
with each entry rounded towards
0
to
L
bits of precision, and similarly for
\mathbf{w}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\prime}.
For our reduction, each server
i
sets
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}_{1}=\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{A}^{(i)}_{2}=-\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}
and sets
\mathbf{b}^{(i)}_{1}=\mathbf{b}^{(i)}_{2}=2\eta
, thereby creating the constraint
