|\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle|\leq 2\eta.

The coordinator similarly adds rows corresponding to the constraints
x_{j}\leq v^{\prime}_{j}
and
-x_{j}\leq v^{\prime}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
, which simply amounts to the constraint
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}.

Suppose that
\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle=0
for all
i.
Then

|\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle|=|\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|=|\left\langle\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle|\leq\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|+\|\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\|\leq 2\eta

for all
i
since
\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}\|\leq\eta
and
\|\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}-\mathbf{w}_{i}\|\leq\eta
. This means that the constraints are satisfied by taking
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}.

On the other hand, suppose that
|\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon/d
for some
i.
Then we must have
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
, but then

|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle|\leq 2\eta

by the same calculation as above, which means that
|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon/d-2\eta.
So the constraints are not satisfiable as long as
\varepsilon
is chosen so that
\varepsilon>4\eta d.
Therefore a protocol that solves the linear feasibility problem above can solve
1.17
for
\varepsilon=O(\eta d)
, which means it requires at least

\Omega(sdL)
communication by
Theorem

1.18
.
∎

6.4
Proof of
Theorem

1.18

6.4.1
Harmonic Analysis Setup

We will use a similar set of tools to
[
RK11
]
. To streamline the analysis, we very briefly recall some facts about Fourier analysis on the sphere. We will use the notation
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
to indicate the space of real-valued square-integrable functions on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.
This is a Hilbert space with inner product given by

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f(\mathbf{x})g(\mathbf{x})d\sigma_{d-1}(\mathbf{x}),

where
\sigma_{d-1}
is the rotationally invariant probability measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

The fundamental fact from Fourier analysis that we use is that any
L_{2}
function
f
on the
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
can be decomposed into a sum of spherical harmonics as

f=\sum_{k}\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}f

where
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is the subspace consisting of spherical harmonics of degree
k
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
is the orthogonal projection onto that subspace. Any operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
that commutes with rotations has an eigen-decomposition with eigenspaces given by the
\mathcal{H}_{k}
’s.

The space
\mathcal{H}_{k}
contains a special class of axially-symmetric functions known as the
zonal spherical harmonics
. The zonal spherical harmonic
z_{k,\mathbf{v}}
of degree
k
and axis of symmetry
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is given by

z_{k,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x})=G_{k}^{(d/2-1)}(\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle),

where
G_{k}^{(\alpha)}:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the so-called
Gegenbauer polynomial
of degree
k
with parameter
\alpha
. There are many ways to define the Gegenbauer polynomials, for example by their generating function
[
Sze62
]
:

\frac{1}{(1-2xt+t^{2})^{\alpha}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}G_{k}^{(\alpha)}(x)t^{k}.

(6.1)

The Gegenbauer polynomials satisfy many interesting identities. We will use the following identity for the derivative of
G_{k}^{(\alpha)}
which follows from differentiating
Eq.

6.1

[
Sze62
]
:

\frac{d}{dx}G_{k}^{(\alpha)}(x)=2\alpha G_{k-1}^{(\alpha+1)}(x).

(6.2)

It is also simple to compute
G_{k}^{(\alpha)}(1)
from
Eq.

6.1
. Plugging in
x=1
gives

(1-t)^{-2\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}G_{k}^{(\alpha)}(1)t^{k},

so
G_{k}^{(\alpha)}(1)=(-1)^{k}\binom{-2\alpha}{k}
by the generalized binomial theorem. In particular we will use the following values below:

\displaystyle G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)

\displaystyle=1

(6.3)

\displaystyle G_{k}^{(3/2)}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{(k+2)(k+1)}{2}.

(6.4)

The zonal spherical harmonics
z_{k,\mathbf{v}}
(in any dimension) are also well-known to have the maximum sup-norm among the degree
k
spherical harmonics of fixed
L^{2}
norm, with this maximum value achieved at
\mathbf{v}.
One way to see this is to recall that for an appropriate normalizing constant
c_{k,d}
, the zonal spherical harmonic
c_{k,d}z_{k,\mathbf{v}}
satisfies the reproducing property
\left\langle c_{k,d}z_{k,\mathbf{v}},f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=f(\mathbf{v})
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
(see
[
Dai13
]
for example). Therefore for
f\in\mathcal{H}(k)
with
\|f\|_{L^{2}}=1
,

|f(\mathbf{v})|=|\left\langle c_{k,d}z_{k,\mathbf{v}},f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}|\leq\left\langle c_{k,d}z_{k,\mathbf{v}},\frac{1}{\|z_{k,\mathbf{v}}\|_{L^{2}}}z_{k,\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\|z_{k,\mathbf{v}}\|_{L^{2}}}z_{k,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{v}).

As a particular consequence of this fact,

\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}|G_{k}^{(n/2)}(x)|=G_{k}^{(n/2)}(1)\\

(6.5)

for all natural numbers
n.

We also recall the spherical Radon transform, also known as the Minkowski-Funk transform
R:L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, which for a function
f
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is defined by

Rf(x)=\int_{x^{\perp}}f(y)d\sigma_{x^{\perp}}(y),

where
\sigma_{x^{\perp}}
is the natural probability measure over
x^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.
In other words,
Rf(x)
is the average of
f
over the spherical equator perpendicular to
x.
The following computation of eigenvalues is classical, and due to Funk
[
Fun11
]
. A similar formula holds in all dimensions, but we will only need the result for
\mathbb{S}^{2}.

Proposition 6.6
.

The eigenfunctions of the spherical Radon transform
R:L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
are precisely the spherical harmonics of degree
k
, and the associated eigenvalues are

\mu_{k}=(-1)^{k/2}\frac{(k-1)!!}{k!!}

for
k
even, and
0
for
k
odd.

To simplify our computations later, we give a simple upper bound on the eigenvalues of
R
that follows immediately from the formula above.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
f_{k}
be a unit spherical harmonic on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
of degree
k
. Then

\mu_{k}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{k}.

Proof.

By the formula above in
Proposition

6.6
, when
k
is even,

|\mu_{k}|=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{3}{4}\cdot\frac{5}{6}\cdot\ldots\cdot\frac{k-1}{k},

so

\mu_{k}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{3}{4}\cdot\ldots\cdot\frac{k-1}{k}=\frac{1}{k},

as desired.
∎

We will also make use of a local averaging operator
T_{\varepsilon}
. Similar to the Radon transform we define
T_{\varepsilon}f(\mathbf{x})
to be the average of
f
over the set
\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle=1-\varepsilon\}
equipped with the probability measure that is invariant under rotations fixing
\mathbf{x}.

Proposition 6.8
.

The eigenfunctions of
T_{\varepsilon}
are the degree
k
spherical harmonics, and when
d=3
, the associated eigenvalues
\xi_{k}
satisfy the bound

|1-\xi_{k}|\leq\min(\varepsilon k^{2},2).

Proof.

It is clear that
T_{\varepsilon}
commutes with rotations, so the
\mathcal{H}_{k}
’s are the eigenspaces for
T_{\varepsilon}.
We analyze the eigenvalues of
T_{\varepsilon}
by considering its action on the degree
k
zonal spherical harmonic
z_{k,\mathbf{v}}=G_{k}^{(1/2)}(\left\langle x,v\right\rangle)
.

Since
z_{k,\mathbf{v}}
is known to be an eigenfunction of
T_{\varepsilon}
, we must have

\xi_{k}=\frac{T_{\varepsilon}z_{k,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{v})}{z_{k,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{v})}=\frac{G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon)}{G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)}=G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon).

To bound this, recall the identity
Eq.

6.2
which gives

\frac{d}{dx}G_{k}^{(1/2)}(x)=G_{k-1}^{(3/2)}(x).

By the Mean Value Theorem, along with
Eq.

6.5
this gives

|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)-G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon)|\leq\varepsilon\cdot\sup_{x}|\frac{d}{dx}G_{k}^{(1/2)}(x)|=\varepsilon\sup_{x}|G_{k-1}^{(3/2)}(x)|\leq\varepsilon G_{k-1}^{(3/2)}(1)=\varepsilon\cdot\frac{k(k+1)}{2}.

Therefore,

|1-\xi_{k}|=|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)-G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon)|\leq\varepsilon k(k+1)=\frac{\varepsilon k(k+1)}{2}\leq\varepsilon k^{2}.

Finally, recall that
|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(x)|
attains it maximum on
[-1,1]
at
x=1.
So,

|1-\xi_{k}|=|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)-G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon)|\leq|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)|+|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1-\varepsilon)|\leq 2|G_{k}^{(1/2)}(1)|=2.

∎

This allows us to bound the operator norm of
R-T_{\varepsilon}R
which we will use in the next section.

Proposition 6.9
.

The operator norm of
R-T_{\varepsilon}R
satisfies the bound

\|R-T_{\varepsilon}R\|_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/4}.

Proof.

Both
R
and
T_{\varepsilon}
commute with rotations, and hence are diagonalized by the spherical harmonics. The operator norm of
R-T_{\varepsilon}R
is bounded by its largest magnitude eigenvalue. The eigenvalue of
R-T_{\varepsilon}R
on the degree
k
spherical harmonics is
\mu_{k}(1-\xi_{k})
, which by
Proposition

6.7
and
Proposition

6.8
is bounded in magnitude by

\min(\varepsilon k^{2},2)k^{-1/2}=\min(\varepsilon k^{3/2},2k^{-1/2})\leq(\varepsilon k^{3/2})^{1/4}(2k^{-1/2})^{3/4}\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/4}.

∎

6.4.2
A Discrepancy Bound for 3D Inner Product

We define two distributions
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
that we we will show are hard to distinguish with low communication. We let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the rotationally invariant distribution over pairs of orthogonal vectors. To describe
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
we give the procedure for taking a sample. First sample
(v,w_{0})
from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Then sample
w
uniformly from
\{x:\left\langle x,w_{0}\right\rangle=1-\varepsilon\}.
The pair
(v,w)
is a sample from
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}.

To continue proving our lower bound, we recall the connection between communication protocols and rectangles. If the universe for Alice’s and Bob’s inputs is
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
then a communication protocol using
b
bits of communication partitions
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
into at most
2^{b}

combinatorial rectangles
, i.e. sets of the form
A\times B
where
A\subset\mathcal{X}
and
B\subseteq\mathcal{Y}.
The information that Alice and Bob learn from running the protocol is precisely the identity of the rectangle that their (joint) input lies in. Randomized protocols with public randomness can simply be thought of as samples from the space of deterministic protocols — Alice and Bob still learn that their input lies in some rectangle. For more details on the basic setup for communication lower bounds, we refer the reader to
[
Rou+16
]
.

One specific approach for showing a communication lower is a so-called
discrepancy bound
over rectangles. To apply the technique, one chooses two distributions
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
over inputs and then shows for all
A\subset\mathcal{X}
and
B\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
the probability of an input landing in
A\times B
is roughly the same under both distributions:

|\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}}((x,y)\in A\times B)-\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}}((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in A\times B))|\leq\alpha.

It is a standard fact that a discrepancy bound of the form above, implies that distinguishing
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with constant advantage requires
\Omega(\log(1/\alpha))
communication. The proof is simple: For a fixed deterministic protocol using at most
N
bits of communication, let
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
be the distribution over rectangles corresponding to samples from
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. Each rectangle contributes at most
\alpha
to the total variation distance between
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
. There are at most
2^{N}
rectangles, and so the total variation distance between the distributions is at most
2^{N}\cdot\alpha.
The extension to randomized protocols is via Yao’s minimax principle.

This discrepancy lower bound technique will give our lower bound for constant
d
after the following proposition. For large
d
, we will need to bootstrap our rectangle lower bound to an information lower bound. This is reminiscent of the main result in
[
BW16
]
, however we will need a slightly stronger version of this lower bound, specialized to our setting, which doesn’t seem to follow from their result. We will therefore ultimately use the discrepancy lower bound in a somewhat different way from what is typical.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
S,T\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
be measurable sets. The following discrepancy bound holds:

|\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}}\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in S\times T\right)-\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in S\times T\right)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/4}.

Proof.

We first rewrite these probabilities in terms of
R
and
T_{\varepsilon}.
Let
\chi_{S}
and
\chi_{T}
be the characteristic functions of
S
and
T
respectively. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}}((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in S\times T)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}}\int_{\mathbf{y}\in x^{\perp}}\chi_{S}(\mathbf{x})\chi_{T}(\mathbf{y})d\sigma_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}(\mathbf{y})d\sigma(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}}\chi_{S}(\mathbf{x})\int_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}\chi_{T}(\mathbf{y})d\sigma_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}(\mathbf{y})d\sigma(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}}\chi_{S}(\mathbf{x})(R\chi_{T})(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\left\langle R\chi_{T},\chi_{S}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Similarly

\operatorname{Pr\/}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}}\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in S\times T\right)=\left\langle T_{\varepsilon}R\chi_{T},\chi_{S}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

By Cauchy-Schwarz along with
Proposition

6.9
above,

|\left\langle(R-T_{\varepsilon}R)\chi_{S},\chi_{T}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}|\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/4}\|\chi_{S}\|_{L_{2}}\|\chi_{T}\|_{L_{2}}\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/4},

since
\|\chi_{S}\|_{L_{2}}
and
\|\chi_{T}\|_{L_{2}}
are both at most
1.

∎

Remark 6.11
.

The above argument only applies in dimension
3
and higher. When
d=2
,
\mu_{k}
does not decay with
k
and so we can not bound the
\min
above uniformly in
k
. This corresponds to the fact that the Radon transform is not smoothing on
\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Indeed for a function on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
which is symmetric about the origin, the Radon transform just performs a
90
degree rotation.

The following Proposition simply checks that samples
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
are likely to have inner product
\Omega(\varepsilon).

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}.
With probability at least
0.95
,
|\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon/20
.

Proof.

By symmetry, we may assume that
\mathbf{v}=[1,0,0]
and
\mathbf{w}_{0}=[0,0,1]
where
\mathbf{w}_{0}
is as in the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
above. Let
\Pi
be the projection onto the
xy
-plane. Then
\left\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle=\left\langle\Pi\mathbf{w},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle
. Note that

\|\Pi\mathbf{w}\|=\sqrt{1-\left\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\rangle^{2}}=\sqrt{2\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}.

Letting
\theta
be the angle that
\Pi w
makes
\mathbf{v}
, we have

|\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right\rangle|=\sqrt{2\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}|\cos\theta|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\cos\theta|\geq\varepsilon|\cos\theta|.

Note that
\theta
is distributed uniformly over the unit circle, so with probability
0.95
,
|\cos\theta|\geq\frac{1}{20}.

∎

6.4.3
Upgrading to Higher Dimensions

As discussed above, since we proved a discrepancy bound over rectangles, previous work
[
BW16
]
immediately implies an information complexity lower bound for the
3
-dimensional version of
6.1
. We will boost this result to
d
dimensions using a direct-sum type result from
[
BJKS04
]
by viewing a
d
dimensional vector as a concatenation of
d/3

3
-dimension vectors. To obtain a direct sum result in our setting, we need the information lower bound on the input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Our next goal is to obtain the necessary information lower bound for public coin protocols. Then we will borrow a “reverse-Newman” result to upgrade to an information lower bound for private-coin protocols.

We recall some basic definitions. Given a protocol
\Pi
depending on the inputs and public randomness
R
, and an input distribution
\mathcal{D}
, the (internal) information cost of the protocol is

\text{IC}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Pi)=I(\Pi;\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{y},R)+I(\Pi;\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x},R)

where
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}.
In other words this is the amount that the players learn about each others’ inputs.

Below we will show that
any
protocol run on our input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
must either consist mostly of large rectangles, or have high internal information cost. This will be in tension with our discrepancy bound above, which shows that having too many large rectangles is unhelpful.

In the proof of the next proposition we will use the following technical fact.

Claim 6.13
.

Assign the natural rotationally invariant probability measure
\mu
to sets of equators through the north pole. Let
T
be a measurable set of equators, and let S be a measurable subset of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Suppose that each element of
T
intersects
S
in a set of measure at least
\alpha
, with respect to the rotationally invariant
1
-dimensional probability measure
\sigma_{1}
along equators. Let
\sigma_{2}
be the spherical probability measure. Then
\sigma_{2}(S)\geq 4\mu(T)\alpha^{2}
.

Proof.

Recall the well-known fact that the map
F
given in cylindrical coordinates by

(r,\theta,z)\mapsto\left(\theta,z\right)

from
\mathbb{S}^{2}
to
[0,2\pi]\times[0,1]
(equipped with Lebesgue measure) is measure-preserving. Geometrically,
F
projects the sphere outwardly onto a circumscribing cylinder, and then unfolds this cylinder into a rectangle. Note that
F
maps great semi-circles through the north pole and south pole onto vertical lines of the form
\{\theta\}\times[0,1].
We make the following easy subclaim.

Subclaim.
Let
f:[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\rightarrow[-1,1]
be the projection
\theta\mapsto\sin\theta
from the half-circle onto the
y
-axis. Let
Y
be a measurable subset of
[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
. Then
\lambda_{1}(f(Y))\geq 8\sigma_{1}(Y)^{2},
where
\lambda_{1}
is the Lebesgue measure, and
\sigma_{1}
is the probability measure on
\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Intuitively, this says that to minimize the measure of the projection we should push the mass to the top and bottom of the semicircle. To see this more rigorously, first suppose that
Y\subseteq[0,\pi/2].
Then we have

\lambda_{1}(f(Y))=2\pi\int|\cos\theta|\chi_{Y}(\theta)d\sigma_{1}(\theta)\geq 2\pi\int_{\pi/2-2\pi\sigma_{1}(Y)}^{\pi/2}\cos\theta d\sigma_{1}(\theta)=\int_{\pi/2-2\pi\sigma_{1}(Y)}^{\pi/2}\cos\theta d\lambda_{1}(\theta)

since cosine is decreasing on
[0,\pi/2].
The latter integral evaluates to

(1-\cos(2\pi\sigma_{1}(Y)))\geq\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}(2\pi\sigma_{1}(Y))^{2}=16\sigma_{1}(Y)^{2}

To finish off the subclaim, for
Y\subseteq[-\pi/2,\pi/2],
partition
Y
into
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
where
Y_{1}\subseteq[0,\pi/2]
and
Y_{2}\subseteq[-\pi/2,0).
Then

\lambda_{1}(f(Y))=\lambda_{1}(f(Y_{1}))+\lambda_{1}(f(Y_{2}))\geq 16(\sigma_{1}(Y_{1})^{2}+\sigma_{1}(Y_{2})^{2})\geq 8(\sigma_{1}(Y_{1})+\sigma_{1}(Y_{2}))^{2}=8\sigma_{1}(Y)^{2}.

Given the subclaim and the stated conditions, we see that
F(S)
intersects at least a
\frac{1}{2}\mu(T)\cdot 2\pi
measure of vertical lines each in a set of measure at least
\frac{1}{2}\cdot 8\alpha^{2}.
The claim follows from Fubini’s Theorem.
∎

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the rotationally invariant distribution over pairs of orthogonal vectors on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and consider a deterministic protocol run on pairs
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Let
I
be the internal information cost of the protocol on this distribution. With probability at least
0.4
,
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
is in a rectangle of measure at least
2^{-60I}
.

Proof.

Fix a deterministic protocol, and let
R=S_{A}\times S_{B}
be the combinatorial rectangle that
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}
lies in.

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap S_{B})
is at least
2^{-10I}
where
\sigma_{1}
is the natural probability measure over
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
. (In other words the event that the equator orthogonal to Alice’s vector has large intersection with
S_{B}
.) Note that
E_{1}
occurs with probability at least
0.9
(otherwise the information cost of the protocol would be larger than
I
).

Let
E_{2}
be the event

\sigma_{1}\left(\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap S_{B}:\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{\perp}\cap S_{A})\geq 2^{-10I}\}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap S_{B}).

In other words
E_{2}
is the event that for a good fraction of
\mathbf{u}\in(\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap S_{B})
, the orthogonal equator to
\mathbf{u}
has large intersection with
S_{A}
.

By the same reasoning as above,

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{w}^{\perp}\cap S_{A})\geq 2^{-10I})\geq 0.9.

For fixed
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}=S_{A}^{\prime}\times S_{B}^{\prime}
, let

P_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},R^{\prime}}=P_{\mathbf{w}}\left(\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{w}^{\perp}\cap S_{A})\geq 2^{-10I}\big|\,\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w})\in R^{\prime}\right).

By the statement two lines above, with probability at least
0.8
over
\mathbf{v}
and
R
,
P_{\mathbf{v},R}\geq 1/2
. Also, conditioned on
\{\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\text{and}\,(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w})\in R^{\prime}\}
,
\mathbf{w}
is distributed uniformly over
(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap S_{B}^{\prime}
. Conditioned on
P_{\mathbf{v},R}\geq 1/2
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Pr\/}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\text{Unif}(R\cap\mathbf{v}^{\perp})}\left(\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{w}^{\perp}\cap S_{A})\geq 2^{-10I}\right)\geq 1/2,

which is equivalent to
E_{2}.

Thus with probability at least
0.7
, events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
hold simultaneously. When this happens,
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}\cap S_{B}
is large, and at least half the points in that set have orthogonal equators that have large overlap with
S_{A}
. So conditioned on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, there is a subset
U
of
v^{\perp}\cap S_{B}
, such that
m(U)\geq 0.5\cdot 2^{-10I}
and
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{\perp}\cap S_{A})\geq 2^{-10I}
for all
\mathbf{u}
in
U
.

We have found that most of the time
S_{A}
has large overlap with a large measure of the great circles passing through a fixed point. This means that
S_{A}
is typically large, as formalized by
6.13
. Applying the claim to our situation gives

m_{2}(S_{A})\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 4(0.5\cdot 2^{-10I})(2^{-10I})^{2}\geq 2^{-30I},

with probability at least
0.7.

(The extra factor of
1/2
is because antipodal points correspond to the same equator.) Symmetrically, the same bound applies to
S_{B}
and a union bound finishes the argument.
∎

The above argument is sufficient to get a lower bound for public coin protocols, however we will need the analogous fact for private coin protocols. To do this we use a “reverse-Newman” type result from
[
BG14
]
which gives a slight improvement over an earlier result from
[
BBKLSV16
]
. This allows us to replace our public-coin lower bound with a private-coin lower bound, albeit at the cost of restricting the number of rounds.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
be as above. Let
\Pi=\Pi(x,y,R)
be the transcript of a public coin protocol with coins
R
, that distinguishes
\mathcal{D}_{0}
from
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
with probability at least
0.9.
Then when
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim D_{0}
we have
I(\Pi;\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x},R)+I(\Pi;\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{y},R)\geq\frac{1}{60}\log\frac{1}{15\varepsilon^{1/4}}.

Proof.

We are considering public-coin protocols, so by Yao’s principle it suffices to consider deterministic protocols. We have an
2\varepsilon^{1/4}
discrepancy bound on rectangles for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
by
Proposition

6.10
. This implies that a protocol that succeed with probability
0.9
must have have probability at least
0.75
of
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
lying in rectangle of measure at most
15\varepsilon^{1/4}
, when
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

To see this, say that a rectangle is small if it has measure at most
15\varepsilon^{1/4}
and large otherwise. Suppose that a sample
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
has probability at least
0.25
of lying in a large rectangle. There are at most
1/(15\varepsilon^{1/4})
large rectangles, so by the discrepancy bound, the probability that
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
lies in a large rectangle is at least
0.25-1/(15\varepsilon^{1/4})\cdot(2\varepsilon^{1/4})=7/60.
Now consider the distributions
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varepsilon}
over rectangles induced by
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}.
We bound their total variation distance. Summing the absolute differences in probabilities for
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varepsilon}
over large rectangles the gives at most
1/(15\varepsilon^{1/4})\cdot(2\varepsilon^{1/4})=2/15
The corresponding sum over small rectangles is at most
(1-0.25)+(1-7/60)\leq 1.64.
So the total variation distance between
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varepsilon}
is at most
\frac{1}{2}(2/15+1.64)<0.9,
which contradicts the protocol succeeding with
0.9
probability.

Combining with
Proposition

6.14
(and noting that
0.4+0.75>1
), we see that a correct protocol with information cost
I
on
\mathcal{D}
must have
2^{-60I}\leq 15\varepsilon^{1/4}
, from which the claim follows.
∎

As an easy consequence, we can construct a distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
for which
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
are hard to distinguish but where
|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon
a.s. when
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}.
This will be more convenient below.

Proposition 6.16
.

Define
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
as the conditional distribution
\mathcal{D}_{20\varepsilon}\bigg|\{|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon\}.
Let
\Pi=\Pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},R)
be the transcript of a public coin protocol with coins
R
, that correctly identifies
\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with probability at least
0.95.
Then when
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim D_{0}
we have
I(\Pi;\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x},R)+I(\Pi;\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{y},R)\geq\frac{1}{60}\log\frac{1}{50\varepsilon^{1/4}}.

Proof.

Suppose that we have a protocol that decides between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with probability
0.95.
By
Proposition

6.12
this protocol gives an algorithm to distinguish between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{20\varepsilon}
with probability at least
0.9
, since with probability at least
0.95
a sample
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
from
\mathcal{D}_{20\varepsilon}
satisfies the condition
|\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon.
The claim now follows from
Lemma

6.15
.
∎

We next state the version of the Reverse-Newman theorem that we use. This is essentially Theorem 1.1 of
[
BG14
]
who states the result for single-round protocols.
[
BBKLSV16
]
shows a similar (slightly weaker) result for one-round protocols and then inductively generalizes to
r
-round protocols. The same induction applies to the one-round protocol of
[
BG14
]
.

Proposition 6.17
.

An
r
-round private coin protocol with internal information cost
I
on an input distribution
\mu
can be simulated by a public coin protocol with information cost
I+O(r\log I).

Proof.

Combine Theorem 1.1 of
[
BG14
]
with the inductive argument given in
[
BBKLSV16
, Section 3.2]
, replacing
c\log(2n\ell)
with
\log I
. Note that by
[
BG14
]
,
\log I
is an upper bound on the information revealed in each round
j
. This is simply because the information revealed in round
j
is bounded by the total information revealed by the protocol, which we assume is
I.

∎

We also mention here a direct-sum technique due to
[
BJKS04
]
that we apply below. The approach is to consider an “OR” of
d/3
independent instances of the three-dimensional inner product problem, and to show that a correct protocol has high information cost on the input distribution
\mathcal{D})^{d/3}
. The idea is that, given an instance of the three-dimensional problem, Alice and Bob can then construct
d/3-1
additional instances consisting of pairs of orthogonal vectors thereby embedding their single instance into a larger OR-instance. Moreover, their instance can be inserted into any of the
d/3
positions, ultimately leading to a
d/3
-factor information cost increase for the OR-instance. To sample the additional pairs of orthogonal vectors from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
however, they need shared information, namely one of the two vectors (then the other vector can be sampled privately). So we actually need to argue that a correct protocol has high external information cost on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, when one of the two vectors is revealed. This is simply the content of our previous lemma bounding the internal information cost on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

6.4.4
Proof of
Lemma

6.2

Proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

Our goal is to apply Theorem 5.6 of
[
BJKS04
]
. Actually, we will apply apply a version of this theorem for protocols with at most
r
rounds for which the same proof applies.

We consider the following problem. Alice and Bob are given
d/3
three-dimension vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbf{v}_{d/3}
and
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{d/3}
. They must output
0
if (a)
\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle=0
for all
i
and output
1
if (b)
|\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i}\right\rangle|\geq\varepsilon
for exactly one
i.
We show that this problem requires
\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
queries under the stated assumption on the number of rounds. This will then immediately imply the lemma. To see this, let
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
be the concatenations of the
\mathbf{v}_{i}
’s and the
\mathbf{w}_{i}
’s. Then
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d/3}}\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d/3}}w
are unit vectors. In case (a),
\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle=0
while in case (b),
|\left\langle\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{d/3}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{d}.
So a protocol to solve
6.1
could in particular distinguish between case (a) and case (b).

We use the notation of Theorem 5.6 in
[
BJKS04
]
. Let
f
be the boolean function above that Alice and Bob wish to compute. Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be the boolean function which is
0
precisely when inputs are orthogonal. In the language of

[
BJKS04
]
,
f
is OR-decomposable with primitive
h
meaning that

f(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=h(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1})\vee\ldots\vee h(\mathbf{v}_{d/3},\mathbf{w}_{d/3}).

To apply Theorem 5.6 of
[
BJKS04
]
we define a mixture of product distributions
\zeta
as follows. Let
D=(a,\mathbf{x})
be a uniformly random sample from
\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}.
Roughly,
\mathbf{x}
reveals the vector for player
a.
More formally, if
D=(0,\mathbf{x})
for some
\mathbf{x}
, then set
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{Y}
uniform over unit vectors orthogonal to
\mathbf{x}.
Similarly if
D=(1,\mathbf{x})
, then set
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{x}
and set
\mathbf{X}
to uniform over unit vectors orthogonal to
\mathbf{x}.
Now let
\zeta
be the distribution for which
((\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}),D)
is a sample from
\zeta.

The distribution of inputs given by
\zeta^{d/3}
is uniform over pairs of orthogonal vectors in each coordinate, and hence in the language of
[
BJKS04
]
, this input distribution is a collapsing distribution for
f
9
9

9

See
[
BJKS04
]
for a detailed definition. Roughly this means that if we replace the coordinate
i
inputs from this distribution with a pair
(\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime})
, then
f(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=h(\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i}).
. It then follows from (the proof of
10
10

10

The only difference is that we impose a restriction on the number of rounds. But
[
BJKS04
]
proves their result by a simulation argument that preserves the number of rounds, so the proof is unchanged.
) Theorem 5.6 in
[
BJKS04
]
that

\text{CIC}_{\zeta^{d/3},\delta,r}(f)\geq\frac{d}{3}\text{CIC}_{\zeta,\delta,r}(h)

where we define
CIC_{\zeta^{d/3},\delta,r}(f)
to be the information complexity of
f
on the input distribution
\eta
for
r
-round protocols that succeed with probability at least
1-\delta
on all valid inputs, given that
D
is observed. In other words
CIC_{\zeta^{d/3},\delta,r}(f)
is the minimum of
I(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y};\Pi|D)
over all (private-coin)
r
-round protocols with success probability at least
1-\delta
, when
(D,(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}))\sim\zeta.

By our definition of
\zeta
,
D
reveals one of the two vectors at random. So

\text{CIC}_{\zeta,\delta,r}(h)=\min_{\Pi}\left(\frac{1}{2}I(\mathbf{X};\Pi|\mathbf{Y})+\frac{1}{2}I(\mathbf{Y};\Pi|\mathbf{X})\right),

where the minimum is over
r
-round protocols that fail with probability at most
\delta
. This latter quantity is simply half of the (internal) information complexity for
r
-round protocols that solve the
3
-dimensional version of
6.1
. Any public coin protocol that solves this problem with at least
0.95
probability can in particular distinguish between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with
0.95
probability, and thus by
Proposition

6.16
, has information complexity at least
\Omega(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
on
\mathcal{D}_{0}.

Then by
Proposition

6.17
any
r
-round private coin protocol with the same parameters requires at least
\Omega(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
information cost when
r\leq c\log(1/\varepsilon)/\log\log(1/\varepsilon)
for an absolute constant
c.

We have therefore shown that
\text{CIC}_{\zeta,\delta,r}\geq c\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
. The lemma follows from noting that communication cost is at least information cost.
∎

6.4.5
From Two Players to
s
Players

Our next task is to bootstrap our two-player lower bound to the
s
-player version. We start with the following simple fact which follows from symmetrizing our hard distributions. In this section we update our notation for the distributions to refer to distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the rotationally invariant distribution over pairs of orthogonal vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
There is another distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}
over pairs of vectors
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
such that for
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
we have
|\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right\rangle|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}}
a.s. and distinguishing
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
0.9
requires
\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
communication for any protocol with at most
C\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}/\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
rounds.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{U}
be a (Haar-)random orthogonal transformation, and let
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
be the hard instance given by
Lemma

6.2
. Then symmetrize by setting
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathbf{U}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
for
i=1,2.
Note that symmetrizing does not make the problem easier, as the players could accomplish this on their own using shared randomness, and no communication.
∎

Lemma 6.19
.

Suppose that there is a protocol for
1.17
that succeeds with probability at least
1-\delta
, uses at most
r
rounds of communication between the coordinator and each server, and uses total communication at most
M
. Then there is an
r
-round protocol to solve the two player
6.1
with probability at least
0.95-\delta
and total communication
20M/s.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a protocol that solves the
s
-player game with the parameters given above. Now we define
s
two-player protocols
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots\mathcal{P}_{s}
that distinguish between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}.

For protocol
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, Alice first samples
s-1
i.i.d. vectors
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k-1},\mathbf{w}_{k+1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{s}
(one for each index other than
k
) orthogonal to her vector
\mathbf{v}
from the distribution that is invariant under rotations fixing
\mathbf{v}.
Then Alice simulates
s-1
servers indexed by
i\in[s]\setminus\{k\}
with server
i
holding
\mathbf{w}_{i}.
Finally server
k
is simply taken to be Bob. Then the
s
-player protocol
\mathcal{P}
is run on these servers. Note that Alice can simulate communication with server
i\neq k
using no communication, and can simulate communication with server
k
by exchanging messages with Bob. By correctness of
\mathcal{P}
each protocol
\mathcal{P}_{i}
correctly distinguishes between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
1-\delta.

Suppose that Alice and Bob’s input comes from
\mathcal{D}_{0}.
Conditioned on
\mathbf{v}
, the random vectors
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{s}
all have the same distribution. Thus there must be a
j
such that
\mathcal{P}_{j}
uses at most
20M/s
communication with probability at least
0.95
. To see this, let
E_{i}
be the expected communication of protocol
\mathcal{P}
between the coordinator and server
i
, when
v
is uniform over the sphere, and
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{s}
are drawn i.i.d. uniformly from
\mathbf{v}^{\perp}.
By linearity of expectation,
E_{1}+\cdots+E_{s}\leq M
, so
E_{j}\leq M/s
for some
j
. Then by Markov’s inequality
\mathcal{P}_{j}
uses at most
20M/s
communication with probability at least
0.9.

We have constructed a two-player protocol
\mathcal{P}_{j}
that is correct and uses at most
10M/s
communication on input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
with probability at least
0.9
. We use this to construct a protocol
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
by terminating
\mathcal{P}_{j}
early if necessary. To run
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
, Alice and Bob simply run
\mathcal{P}_{j}
, while keeping track of the total communication used. If sending the next message of
\mathcal{P}_{j}
would put the total communication above
10M/s
, then that player simply terminates the protocol and outputs
1
. Now we argue that
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
is correct. On the input distribution
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
fails if either
\mathcal{P}_{j}
is terminated early, or
\mathcal{P}_{j}
is incorrect. By a union bound
\mathcal{P}_{j}
fails on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
with probability at most
0.05+\delta.
On
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
only fails if
\mathcal{P}_{j}
fails which occurs with probability at most
\delta.
So
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
distinguishes
\mathcal{D}_{0}
from
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
0.95-\delta.

∎

Remark 6.20
.

For constant
d
, this argument gives a communication lower bound of
\Omega(s\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
for solving
1.17
with constant probability. In this case we do not need to restrict the number of rounds since
Proposition

6.10
immediately implies an
\Omega(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
lower bound for
6.1
when
d=3
(or in fact when
d
is any fixed integer greater than
2
).

Proof of
Theorem

1.18
.
For two-player protocols with at most
r=C\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}/\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
rounds we have a communication lower bound of
\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
from
Proposition

6.18
for distinguishing
mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}.
Thus in the setup of
Lemma

6.19
we must have
20M/s\geq\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
which implies that
M\geq\Omega(sd\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
as desired.

6.5
High-Precision Lower Bound

In this section we show the follow result for obtaining a solution to high additive error precision.
See
1.19

Our main observation is that a Gaussian least-squares problem is somewhat sensitive to each individual row.

Proposition 6.21
.

Consider a communication game between Alice and Bob, where Alice is given a uniformly random vector
\mathbf{v}
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.
Bob would like to produce a vector
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
with
\|\mathbf{v}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|\leq\varepsilon.

Any protocol that succeeds at this game with probability at least
0.1
must use at least
\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
communication.

Proof.

It is a standard fact that there exists a
3\varepsilon
packing of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of size
N:=(c/\varepsilon)^{d-1}
for an absolute constant
c
.
This follows from a volumetric argument for example (see e.g.
[
Ver18
]
).

Now consider the following game. Alice is given a random integer
i
in
\{1,\ldots N\}
which Bob must learn with at least
0.1
probability. This game clearly requires
\Omega(\log N)
communication. To see this, note that it suffices to consider deterministic protocols by Yao’s principle. Any deterministic protocol sending
m
bits, forces Bob into choosing one of at most
2^{m}
outputs, so
2^{m}/N\geq 0.1
which means that
m\geq\log(0.1N).

On the other this game reduces to the game stated in the proposition. Alice identifies the numbers
1,\ldots,N
with points
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{N}
in the packing. Given an index
i
, Alice chooses the point
\mathbf{x}_{i}.
Using shared randomness Alice and Bob choose a Haar-random orthogonal transformation
U
. Then
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{x}_{i}
is uniform over
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
so Alice and Bob may run the a protocol for the above problem, allowing Bob to find
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
with
\|\hat{\mathbf{v}}-Ux_{i}\|\leq\varepsilon.
Then Bob computes
\hat{x}_{i}=\mathbf{U}^{-1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}
which satisfies
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}-\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\|\leq\varepsilon.
Since
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{N}
was a
3\varepsilon
packing, this allows Bob to recover
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and hence
i
as desired. The proposition follows.
∎

We will show the following technical communication result and then apply it.

Lemma 6.22
.

Consider a communication game between Alice and Bob where Alice is given a (rotationally-invariant) random vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|\mathbf{v}\|=\alpha\leq 1
, and both players see a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and a vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Suppose further that
\mathbf{I}\preceq\mathbf{M}\preceq c\mathbf{I}
for an absolute constant
c
and that
\|\mathbf{w}\|\geq 1
. Define

F(\mathbf{x})=F_{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}.

Alice would like to send a message to Bob from which Bob can produce
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|F(\mathbf{v})-\mathbf{y}\|\leq\varepsilon.

Any communication protocol that succeeds with probability at least
0.9
requires at least
\Omega(d\log\frac{\alpha}{\varepsilon d})
communication.

Proof.

Let
S
be the set of points
\mathbf{x}
in
\alpha\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}\geq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}}
. Note that

\operatorname{Pr\/}(\mathbf{x}\in S)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}|\leq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}}\right)\right)\geq\frac{1}{4}.

To see this, note that
\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}\|\geq 1.
Then by rotational invariance

\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(|\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}|\leq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}}\right)\leq\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{1}|\leq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}}\right)=\operatorname{Pr\/}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{1}|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{100d}\right).

The quantity
\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{1}|^{2}
is distributed as
g_{1}^{2}/(g_{1}^{2}+\ldots+g_{d}^{2})
where each
g_{i}
is a standard normal. Note that
g_{1}^{2}>1/10
with probability at least
3/4.
Also
g_{1}^{2}+\ldots+g_{d}^{2}\leq 2d
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cd)
by Bernstein’s inequality for example (see
[
Ver18
]
for example). So when
d
is a large enough constant, the probability above is at most
1/2.
(The constants here are of course not close to optimal.)

For all
\mathbf{x}\in S
we have

\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}}\geq\frac{\alpha}{20\sqrt{d}(1+2\alpha^{2})}.

Thus for
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in S
we have

\displaystyle\|F(\mathbf{x}_{1})-F(\mathbf{x}_{2})\|

\displaystyle=\|\frac{\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{1}}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{1}-\frac{\mathbf{x}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{2}}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

\displaystyle\geq\min\left(\frac{\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{1}},\frac{\mathbf{x}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}}{1+\mathbf{x}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{2}}\right)\|\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}(1+C\alpha^{2})}\sigma_{\min}(M)\|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{10\sqrt{d}(1+C\alpha^{2})}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\|.

\displaystyle:=\beta\|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\|

Condition on
\mathbf{v}
lying in
S
which happens with at least
1/4
probability. A protocol that solves the above communication problem allows Bob to produce a vector
\mathbf{y}
with
\|F(\mathbf{v})-\mathbf{y}\|\leq\varepsilon.
If
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
is another vector satisfying
\|F(\hat{\mathbf{v}})-\mathbf{y}\|\leq\varepsilon
, then

2\varepsilon\geq\|F(\mathbf{v})-F(\hat{\mathbf{v}})\|\geq\beta\|\mathbf{v}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|,

so
\|\mathbf{v}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}\|\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{\beta}.

Thus with failure probability at most
\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{10}=0.85
Bob can produce a
\frac{2\varepsilon}{\beta}
additive approximation
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
to Alice’s vector
\mathbf{v}
. However this latter communication problem requires at least
\Omega(d\log\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon})=\Omega(d\log\frac{\alpha}{\varepsilon d})
communication by
Proposition

6.21
.

∎

Proposition 6.23
.

Consider the following communication game. Alice holds row
\mathbf{A}_{1}
of
\mathbf{A}
which is uniform over
\alpha\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{d}{s+d}}
. Bob holds
s-1
independent rows
\mathbf{A}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{s}
, each with the same distribution as Alice’s. Also
\mathbf{A}_{s+k}=\mathbf{e}_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,d.
Set
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{e}_{s+1}
so that
\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{e}_{1}.
Alice and Bob would like to compute
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
with
\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|\leq\varepsilon+\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|,
with probability at least
0.95
.
This communication game requires at least
\Omega\left(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon(s+d)}\right)
communication.

Proof.

First, observe that
\mathbf{A}
is well-conditioned with high probability. Indeed, with probability at least
\exp(-d)
(which is at most
0.05
for
d\geq 3
), we have
I\leq\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\leq cI
for an absolute constant
c.
The first inequality always holds by construction of
\mathbf{A}.
The latter holds by standard concentration results for the top singular value of a matrix with subgaussian rows (see, for example,
[
Ver18
, Section 4]
). Note that
\mathbf{x}_{*}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}.

Note that
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
satisfies
\|\widehat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*}\|\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon},
since

\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{A}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*})\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}\geq\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2},

and so under the stated conditions

\|\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}_{*}\|^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}-\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}-1^{2}\leq 3\varepsilon,

where the inequality above uses
\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{b}\|^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{b}\|^{2}=1.

Let
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
denote
\mathbf{A}
with row
1
removed. Let
\mathbf{A}_{1}
denote the first row of
\mathbf{A}
as a column vector. To simplify notation, let
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}.

By the Sherman-Morrison formula, we have

\displaystyle x_{*}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{M}+\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}-\frac{(\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1})(\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1})^{\top}}{1+\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}}\mathbf{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+F_{\mathbf{M}^{-1},\mathbf{e}_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}),

in the notation of
Lemma

6.22
.

Note that Bob can calculate
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{1}
directly. So a protocol that computes
\mathbf{x}^{\ast}
to within
2\sqrt{\varepsilon}
additive error, would yield a
2\sqrt{\varepsilon}
additive approximation to
F_{\mathbf{M}^{-1},\mathbf{e}_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}).
By
Lemma

6.22
, this requires at least
\Omega(d\log\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}d})=\Omega(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon(s+d)})
communication.

∎

Now we are ready to prove
Theorem

1.19
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.19
.

Similar to
Proposition

6.23
above, our hard instance is as follows. The matrix
\mathbf{A}
has
s
rows each uniform over
\sqrt{\frac{d}{s+d}}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
each held on a different server. We also include rows
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}
which are known to all servers and set
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{e}_{s+1}
, also known to all servers.

Note the input to each server is i.i.d. and so we can use the symmetrization argument of
[
PVZ12
]
. We sketch the idea here and refer the reader to
[
PVZ12
]
for more details. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a protocol for our
s
-player game with communication
C(\mathcal{P})
. Then, construct a two player game
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
by having Alice choose a uniformly random player
1,\ldots,s
which she simulates and having Bob simulate the remaining players. The protocol
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
uses at most
100C(\mathcal{P})/s
communication with probability at least
0.98
since the expected communication of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is at most
2C(\mathcal{P})/s
. However, Alice and Bob now have a protocol that solves
Proposition

6.23
with at least
0.95
probability, and therefore

100C(\mathcal{P})/s\geq\Omega\left(d\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon(s+d)}\right),

or

C(\mathcal{P})\geq\Omega\left(sd\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon(s+d)}\right).

Finally note that since the matrix
\mathbf{A}\preceq 2\mathbf{I}
, rounding all entries to
L
bits of precision changes the solution error by at most
O(\sqrt{d}2^{-L})
on vectors of norm at most
1
(which is all that we must consider since
\mathbf{b}
has norm
1
).
∎

7
Conclusion and Future Directions

In this section, we discuss a few open problems and possible directions for future research regarding the communication complexity of the convex optimization problems we discussed in the paper.

Linear regression on matrices with special structure.

Many optimization algorithms use linear regression as a subprocedure. One such example is IPMs, which are used to solve linear programs, which we discussed in this paper. In some scenarios, the linear regression problem has a special structure, e.g., the alternating least squares algorithm in tensor decomposition
[
DJSSW19
,
FFG22
]
and least squares with non-negative data
[
DLPS22
]
, which appears in many real-world problems. It would be interesting to investigate the communication complexity of solving linear regression problems that have matrices with special structures.

Inverse maintenance.

Many recent improvements for the running time of convex optimization problems, such as linear programming and semi-definite programming, have relied on the use of inverse maintenance. We also used this to improve the communication complexity of solving linear programs when we use IPMs in a distributed setting. There has recently been some progress on analyzing inverse maintenance for general matrix formulas in an attempt to unify the analysis of many algorithms
[
Bra21
,
ABGZ24
]
. An interesting direction is to analyze the communication complexity of inverse maintenance for such general matrix formulas.

Appendix A
Some Useful Technical Results

In this section, we present some definitions and properties from matrix analysis and convex
analysis that we use. These results are standard
and may be found in, for example,
[
Roc70
,
BBV04
]
.

Fact A.1

(
[
HJ12
]
)
.

Given a positive definite matrix
\mathbf{A}
, if the inequality
\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{I}
holds, where
\mathbf{I}
is the appropriate-sized identity matrix, then we have
\mathbf{A}^{2}\preceq\mathbf{I}

and
\mathbf{A}^{-1}\succeq\mathbf{I}
.

Further, if the inequality chain
\mathbf{0}\prec\mathbf{A}\preceq\mathbf{B}
holds, then the ordering is preserved on premultiplying and postmultiplying both sides by the same positive definite matrix, which implies
\mathbf{A}^{-1}\succeq\mathbf{B}^{-1}
.

Definition A.2
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.
Then the function

f^{\ast}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as

f^{\ast}(\mathbf{y})=\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}\in\textrm{dom}(f)}\left[\langle{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{y}\rangle-f({\mathbf{x}})\right]

is called the Fenchel conjugate of the function
f.
An immediate consequence
of the definition (and by applying the appropriate convexity-preserving
property) is that
f^{\ast}
is convex, regardless of the convexity
of
f.
We use the superscript
\ast
on functions to denote their
conjugates.

Remark A.3
.

Using the above definition of conjugate, for a given function
\psi
, we use the shorthand notation
-\psi^{*}(-t\mathbf{c})
to express
\min_{{\mathbf{x}}}\left[t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}+\psi({\mathbf{x}})\right]
.

Fact A.4

(Biconjugacy)
.

For a closed, convex function
f,
we have

f=f^{\ast\ast}.

Fact A.5

(
[
Roc70
]
)
.

For a closed, convex differentiable function

f,
we have

\mathbf{y}=\nabla f({\mathbf{x}})\text{ if and only if }{\mathbf{x}}=\nabla f^{\ast}(\mathbf{y}).

Fact A.6

(
[
Roc70
]
)
.

A strictly convex, twice-differentiable
function
f
has
\nabla^{2}f^{\ast}(\nabla f({\mathbf{x}}))=(\nabla^{2}f({\mathbf{x}}))^{-1}.

Definition A.7

(Polar of a Set
[
RW09
]
)
.

Given a set
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},
its polar
is defined as

\mathcal{S}^{\circ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle\mathbf{y},{\mathbf{x}}\rangle\leq 1,\text{ }\forall{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{S}\right\}.

Fact A.8

(
[
Roc70
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be closed, compact, convex sets. Then,
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}\supseteq\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
.

Fact A.9

(
[
Roc70
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed, compact, convex
set, and let
\mathbf{y}
be a point.
Then
(\mathrm{conv}\left\{\mathcal{S},\mathbf{y}\right\})^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{H}
,
where
\mathcal{H}
is the halfspace defined by
\mathcal{H}=\left\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y}\rangle\leq 1\right\}
.

Lemma A.10

(Theorem 2 of
[
ZLY23
]
)
.

Given a convex set
\Omega
with a
\nu
-self-concordant
barrier
\psi_{\Omega}
and inner radius
r
. Let
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}t\cdot\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\psi_{\Omega}(\mathbf{x})
.
Then, for any
t>0
,

\min\left\{\frac{1}{2t},\frac{r\|c\|_{2}}{4\nu+4\sqrt{\nu}}\right\}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{\infty}\leq\frac{\nu}{t}.

While the theorem in
[
ZLY23
]
is stated only for polytopes, their proof works for general convex sets.

A.1
Background on Interior-Point Methods

Our work draws heavily upon geometric properties of self-concordant
functions, which underpin the rich theory of interior-point methods.
We list below the formal results needed for our analysis,
and refer the reader to
[
NN94
,
Ren01
]
for a detailed exposition of this function
class.
We begin with the definitions of self-concordant functions and self-concordant barriers:

Definition A.11

(Self-concordance
[
NN94
]
)
.

A function
F:Q\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a self-concordant function on a convex set
Q
if for any
{\mathbf{x}}\in Q
and any direction
\mathbf{h}
,

\lvert D^{3}F({\mathbf{x}})[\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}]\rvert\leq 2(D^{2}F({\mathbf{x}})[\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}])^{3/2},

where
D^{k}F({\mathbf{x}})[\mathbf{h}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{h}_{k}]
is the
k
-th derivative of
F
at
{\mathbf{x}}
along the directions
\mathbf{h}_{1},\dotsc,\mathbf{h}_{k}
. We say
F
is a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier if it further satisfies
\nabla F({\mathbf{x}})^{\top}(\nabla^{2}F({\mathbf{x}}))^{-1}\nabla F({\mathbf{x}})\leq\nu
for any
{\mathbf{x}}\in Q
.

Theorem A.12

(
[
Ren01
, Theorem 2.3.3]
)
.

If
f
is a self-concordant barrier, then for all

{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\textrm{dom}(f)
, we have
\langle\nabla f({\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\rangle\leq\nu,
where

\nu
is the self-concordance of
f
.

Theorem A.13

(
[
Nes98
, Theorem 4.2.5]
)
.

If
f
is a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier such that
{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{y}\in\operatorname{dom}(f)

satisfy
\langle\nabla f({\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\rangle\geq 0
, then
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathbf{x}},\nu+2\sqrt{\nu}).

Theorem A.14

(
[
Nes98
, Theorem 4.2.6]
)
.

If
f
is a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier for convex set
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathbf{z}\in\operatorname{dom}(f)
, and
\mathbf{z}^{\star}
is the
analytic center
for
f
, then

\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{z},1)\subseteq\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{z}^{\star},\nu+2\sqrt{\nu})

Corollary A.15
.

If
f
is a self-concordant barrier for a given convex set
\mathcal{K}
that satisfies
K\subset\mathcal{B}(0,R)
, then
\nabla^{2}f({\mathbf{x}})\succeq\frac{1}{4R^{2}}I
for any
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose
\nabla^{2}f\not\succeq\frac{1}{4R^{2}}I
. This implies
(2R\mathbf{u})^{\top}(\nabla^{2}f({\mathbf{x}}))(2R\mathbf{u})<1
for some unit vector
\mathbf{u}
and for a point
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{K}
. In other words,
{\mathbf{x}}+2R\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathbf{x}},1)\subseteq\mathcal{K}
.
We note that since
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{B}(0,R)
, it is not possible to have
{\mathbf{x}}+2R\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{K}
for any
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{K}
and unit vector
\mathbf{u}
.
∎

Theorem A.16
.

If
f
is a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier for a given convex set
\mathcal{K}
, then
g({\mathbf{x}})=\mathbf{c}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}+f({\mathbf{x}})
is also self-concordant. Moreover, if
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(0,R)
, then

g
is at most
3\nu+12R^{2}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}^{2}
-self-concordant.

Proof.

For the first part, it suffices to note that
\nabla^{2}g=\nabla^{2}f
. For the second part, since
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(0,R)
,
Corollary

A.15
applies to give
\nabla^{2}g=\nabla^{2}f\succeq\frac{1}{4R^{2}}I
. Then, we have

\|\mathbf{c}\|_{(\nabla^{2}g(x))^{-1}}^{2}\leq{4R^{2}}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}^{2}.

Hence, we have

\|\nabla g\|_{(\nabla^{2}g(x))^{-1}}^{2}\leq 3\|\nabla f\|_{(\nabla^{2}f(x))^{-1}}^{2}+3\|\mathbf{c}\|_{(\nabla^{2}g(x))^{-1}}^{2}\leq 3\nu+12{R^{2}}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{2}^{2}.

∎

We now state the following result from self-concordance calculus.

Theorem A.17

(Theorem 3.3.1 of
[
Ren01
]
)
.

If
f
is a (strongly nondegenerate) self-concordant
function, then so is its Fenchel conjugate
f^{\ast}.

Fact A.18

(
[
Ren01
]
)
.

Given a self-concordant barrier
f
with self-concordant parameter
\nu
, the function
f
restricted to an affine subspace
\mathcal{S}
, also has self-concordance parameter
\nu
.

Fact A.19

(Theorem
2.3.8
in
[
Ren01
]
)
.

If
f
is a
\nu
-self-concordant barrier, with
{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{y}
both in
\textrm{dom}(f)
, then for
0\leq s\leq 1
, we have
f({\mathbf{x}}+s(\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}))\leq f({\mathbf{x}})-\nu\log(s)
.

The following result bounds the quadratic approximation of a function
with the distance between two points measured in the local norm.

Theorem A.20

(Theorem 2.2.2 of
[
Ren01
]
)
.

Let
f
be a self-concordant function,
{\mathbf{x}}\in\textrm{dom}(f)
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathbf{x}},1)
, and define the Dikin ellipsoid
\|\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\|_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\langle\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}},\nabla^{2}f({\mathbf{x}})\cdot(\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}})\rangle
. Then, the following bound holds:

f(\mathbf{y})\leq f({\mathbf{x}})+\langle\nabla f({\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\|_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{2}+\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\|_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{3}}{3(1-\|\mathbf{y}-{\mathbf{x}}\|_{{\mathbf{x}}})}.

Finally, we need the following definition of the universal barrier.

Definition A.21

(
[
NN94a
,
LY21
]
)
.

Given a convex body
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
(\mathcal{K}-{\mathbf{x}})^{\circ}=\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\mathbf{y}^{\top}(\mathbf{z}-{\mathbf{x}})\leq 1,\forall\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}\}
be the polar of
\mathcal{K}
with respect to
{\mathbf{x}}
. Then the
universal barrier
of
\mathcal{K}
is defined as
\psi:\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{K})\to\mathbb{R}
by

\psi({\mathbf{x}})=\log\mathrm{vol}((\mathcal{K}-{\mathbf{x}})^{\circ}).

It was shown in
[
LY21
]
that the universal barrier is
n
-self-concordant.

A.2
Facts from Convex Geometry

Since our analysis is contingent on the change in the volume of convex
bodies when points are added to them or when intersected
with halfspaces, we invoke Grünbaum’s result several
times.

Theorem A.22

(
[
Grü60
,
BKLLS20
]
)
.

Let
f
be a log-concave distribution
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with centroid
\mathbf{c}_{f}.
Let
\mathcal{H}=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}{\mathbf{v}}\geq q\right\}

be a halfspace defined by a normal vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,
\int_{\mathcal{H}}f(\mathbf{z})d\mathbf{z}\geq\frac{1}{e}-t^{+},

where
t=\frac{q-{\mathbf{c}_{f}}^{\top}{\mathbf{v}}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim f}({\mathbf{v}}^{\top}{\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{c}_{f}})^{2}}}

is the distance of the centroid to the halfspace scaled by the standard
deviation along the normal vector
\mathbf{v}
and
t^{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{0,t\}
.

Remark A.23
.

A crucial special case of
Theorem

A.22
is that cutting a convex set through its centroid yields two parts, the smaller of which has volume at least
1/e
times the original volume and the larger of which is at most
1-1/e
times the original total volume.
Formally, let
\mathcal{K}
be a convex set with centroid

\mu
and covariance matrix
\Sigma.
Then, for any point
{\mathbf{x}}

satisfying
\|{\mathbf{x}}-\mu\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\leq\eta
and a halfspace
\mathcal{H}

such that
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{H})\geq\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{K})\cdot(1/e-\eta).

Lemma A.24

(Section 3 in
[
BE15
]
; Section 3 of
[
Kla06
]
)
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
p_{\theta}
be defined
as
p_{\theta}(\mathbf{x})\propto\exp({\theta}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}-f(\theta))
,
where
f(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\log\left[\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp({\theta}^{\top}{\mathbf{u}})d\mathbf{u}\right]
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim p_{\theta}}[\mathbf{x}]=\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{K})}\left\{f^{\ast}(\mathbf{x})-{\theta}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}\right\}.
